and in the northeast Herukī.
Thus the eight yoginīs:
Gaurī is yellow, blue, and green;
Caurī is red, blue, and green;
Pramohā is blue, yellow, and green;
Vetālī is white, blue, and green;
Ghasmarī is multicolored,
red, blue, and green;
Caṇḍālī is green, blue, and yellow;
Ḍombī is smoke-colored, blue, and green;
Herukī is blue, green, and red.
Thus all of them, too,
are three-eyed, with hair unbound,
clad in the directions, and dancing,
seated well upon seats of hungry ghosts,
adorned with the five mudrās.
They carry khaṭvāṅga, skull cup, and bell,
damaru, noose, and hook.
All are adorned with garlands of skulls
and crowned with Akṣobhya.
Thus they bestow all the accomplishments
of the pīṭhas and upapīṭhas.
Thus, in the wheel of mind,
the goddesses Gaurī and the rest
are proclaimed to be yoginīs.
Through approach they bestow their grace,
and the unobstructed result is accomplished.
Having accomplished the goddess Khecarī,
one attains the state of moving in the sky.
Outside that is the wheel of speech.
In the east is she of the vīṇā gesture,
in the north she of the flute gesture,
in the west she of the great drum gesture,
and in the south she of the drum gesture.
They are blue, yellow, red, and green.
In the southeast and the other corners are Vajrahāsā,
Vajralāsyā, Vajragītā,
and Vajranṛtyā; they are red,
blue, yellow, and green.
They are one-faced and four-armed,
naked, with hair unbound,
wearing garlands of skulls,
three-eyed and lovely.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
with gestures of the hands,
with the gesture of vajra and bell,
and, holding the wrists level,
with the gesture of turning the lotus.
They are the kṣetras and the upakṣetras,
the chandos and the chandohas,
and they are crowned with Amitābha.
In the east of the wheel of body
is Puṣpā, in the north Dhūpā,
in the west Dīpā, and in the south Gandhā;
they are yellow, smoke-colored, red, and white.
Ādarśā, Rasā, Sparśā, and Dharmā
are in the southeast and the other corners,
moon-colored, red, green, and white.
They are three-eyed,
naked, with hair unbound,
dancing all about with their feet,
bearing offerings in their hands,
holding skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
and carrying a damaru in the hand,
wearing garlands of skulls.
These goddesses are the melāpakas,
the upamelāpakas, the charnel grounds,
and the lesser charnel grounds,
and they are crowned with Vairocana.
At the gates are Vajrāṅkuśī,
Vajrapāśī, Vajrasphoṭā,
and likewise Vajraghaṇṭā,
blue, green, red, and yellow,
in the east, the north, the west,
and likewise in the southern direction.

In the southeast and the other intermediate directions
are Yamadāhī and the others,
red, blue, smoke-colored, and yellow.
Skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
their own emblems, and damaru,
curved knife, and a head they hold.
Three-eyed, with hair unbound,
naked, they abide in a dancing posture.
All are adorned with the five mudrās,
wear garlands of skulls,
bear the crest of their own family,
and abide in their proper order.
In their hearts is the wisdom being.
The three wheels of body, speech, and mind:
one should meditate on the lord of the chief,
white, red, and blue,
the heroes together with the yoginīs.
When one has meditated in this way,
then, from the meditation as taught,
with the hooks of wisdom
summon the wheel of wisdom,
make offerings and confer empowerment in due order,
gather them into one and dissolve them.
Having meditated on the sequence just as it is,
perform the wisdom recitation as well.
Through emanating and gathering, through breath control,
done in the sequence of sending out and drawing in,
the master should recite:
oṃ āḥ hūṃ hūṃ
By a sequence such as this,
having done it countless times,
for as long as one does not grow weary,
rest the samaya wheel above in equipoise and recite.
Taking up a rosary of great conch,
perform the recitation of supreme Saṃvara:
oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kṣmāṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ ḍākinī jāla sambaraṃ svāhā oṃ vajra vārāhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ḍākini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ lāme huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṇḍarohe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ rūpiṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ huṃ gauri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cauri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vettāli huṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ pukkasi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṇḍāli huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ghasmari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ herukasananibhe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra vīne huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra vaṃśe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra mṛdaṃhe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra murje huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra hāsyai huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra lāsyai huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra vīte huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra nṛtye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra puṣpe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra dhūve huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra dīpe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra gandhe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ rūva vajri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ rasa vajri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ sparśa vajri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ dharma dhūtu vajri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra aṃkuśa jaḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra pāśa hūṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra spoṭa vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra āveśa hoḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadāḍhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadūti huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadaṃṣṭi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamamathani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
The one who holds the samaya, with pure words,
should always recite with a devoted mind.
Endowed with the union of the inner self,
having the nature of wisdom and means,
one's own means and the body of the vidyā,
the self that is equal and nondual,
by emanating hosts of wrathful emanations,
and making the three worlds, together with all in them,
into Heruka,
and gathering all of the three worlds without remainder
into that,
one abides in body, speech, and mind.
This is the rite of yoga,
the recitation of the meditation on the wheels.
Through the yoga of one's own deity,
when one sees the moving and the unmoving,
at the time when the holder of samaya rises,
all the tathāgatas,
the heroes and the yoginīs,
the ḍākas and the ḍākinīs,
should all be supplicated.
Arising from the enjoyment
of the wrathful one's delight in bliss,
having been attended by Pramohā,
one enters into the union of bliss:
ra ma he nad ṭa na su ra ā ma hā su hu ba ra jo i ni ka ma la su hu a la la hoḥ su ra a utta ma ma hūṃ ma hā sijja u ma jo i ni ja la ma ho ā ī a ā ra ho a ru he byi nu su ba ya nā ha su ra a su hu de hi bar ṭā i ni jā la su hu bi sa pha na ta ro ṇa ṇa pa de hi ma hā su hū majja mu hū mo kṣu pa la kkha u tu tu sa hū ṇa i su hū bajji u mo khu su hū i na i du khe la tha i mo khu su hū ba sa i paṃ ka u majja ta nu na ba rā a pa da traṃ bhu ja ta nu muñja ma hā su hū i la da ra pā ya u ma hū a ra bī a gi a paṃ u ra su
Having urged the protector with this,
the accomplishment of the maṇḍala is certain.
By singing the song of the king of spring
in a sweet and gentle voice,
the stainless nets of ḍākinīs
look on and perform every action.
Through the union of the lovely vajra and lotus
every accomplishment is achieved.
If one urges them with this delight,
they bestow great bliss.
At the times of union,
the vajra holder should join completely.
At the beginning, in the middle, and at the end,
recite the secret mantra of a hundred syllables.
Having made the wheels stable,
the heroes and the ḍākinīs,
this is the secret mantra of them all,
of the wrathful ones, the buddhas, and the tathāgatas.
Having made them all stable in the heart,
through meditation all become stable.
If one desires accomplishment for oneself,
all accomplishments abide in it:
oṃ vajra heruka samaya manupālaya vajra heruka tvenopatiṣṭha drīḍho me bhava sutoṣyo me bhava anurakto me bhava supoṣyo me bhava sarva siddhiṃ me prayaccha sarva karma suca me cittaṃ śriyaḥ kuru hūṃ ha ha ha hoḥ bhagavāna vajra heruka māme muñca heruka bhava mahā samaya satva aḥ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
Through this the Blessed One pervades all.
The supreme hundred syllables
are supreme Heruka himself.
Whatever is its reversal,
arranged upon sixteen petals,
and all the maṇḍalas that have been taught,
I should meditate upon and make stable.
Through the yoga of one's own deity,
one should abide in bliss just as one wishes.
If one meditates on a yoga such as this,
the three realms too will be seen.
Oneself has the defining mark of the hero,
and thus existence too is empty.
Through meditation one truly abides as oneself.
Here no austerities are taught;
there is no rosary, and no mantra either;
no dhāraṇīs, no rites of fire offering,
and no basis in a sequence of ritual;
no performing of yoga mudrās,
and no sequence of the stance with the right leg extended.
Holding the actual great vajra,
performing every self-arisen accomplishment,
one does not even bring about cessation.
One should worship oneself by oneself.
The full maṇḍala of the moon,
meditate upon it at the center of the navel.

The syllable that has become the supreme site,
meditated upon as the form of the deity,
one should imagine at its center.
Uniting body, speech, and mind,
it has passed beyond the body,
and even the entity of mind cannot be found by searching.
There is no passion and no freedom from passion;
yet it is endowed with dispassion.
At the center of the lotus of the heart,
what is well found through examination
is where the mind comes to rest.
All the yoginīs are women;
they are declared to be the supreme mudrā.
Upon the moon, adorned with the fifty letters,
the contemplation of the wisdom mind
is supreme; there is nothing else to practice.
Through the path of the guru's instructions
and the mind's supreme effort,
this state of peace,
which the many in the world do not realize,
once union and separation from union have been made,
is pervaded by the single state.
Thus the fruit of awakening is hard to find:
first it arises from the syllables of the vowels, and in no other way.
The intellect of those with wrong views, following the treatises of evil non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
is fruitless, like a eunuch, like a blind man's insight into forms.

This concludes the twenty-fifth chapter, on the sequence of the sites of body, speech, and mind, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high.

Then I shall explain the practice.
Buddhakapāla, supreme and sublime:
this practice is supreme among all practices,
bestowing every accomplishment.
The secret should be well concealed
and not shown to anyone at all.
That reality, held as the supreme meditation,
abides in the heart of the yoginī.
The secret should be firmly concealed,
and one should meditate on the guru's words.
In a lovely charnel ground site,
the practitioner, with certainty and firmness,
through the practice of the generation stage,
the sequence of the grounds and the sites,
at the center of the vajra hub
should meditate on the syllable hūṃ.
Through that, oneself becomes in particular
Buddhakapāla, the supreme,
without location, peaceful and pure,
free of all conceptual thought,
self-arisen from the vowels and consonants,
nondual and supremely peaceful,
two-armed and one-faced,
adorned with the six mudrās,
the color of jambu gold, pervading evenly,
massed all about with garlands of light rays,
with a diadem of skull garlands,
three-eyed, with a laughing face.
He is the meditation of all beings,
the nondual Dharma, unsurpassed.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
with a human skin stretched above.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
his right hand in the mudrā of fearlessness.
Crowned with the five buddhas,
he is adorned with a garland of heads.
In his left hand is a skull cup
brimming with marrow,
mixed, for the benefit of all beings,
with the five ambrosias of wisdom.
The khaṭvāṅga is the yoga of the deity;
the skull cup is the heroes and yoginīs;
the fearlessness of the right hand is purity:
freed from all fears, fearless.
All that is vicious and terrifying, without exception,
he presses down here with his feet,
and he removes every fear caused by the vicious.
He holds the peaceful half moon,
his head is marked with a crossed vajra,
his forehead is beautified by a vajra garland,
and he wears lovely anklets and ornaments.
Rising from the center of the hub
of a multicolored eight-petaled lotus,
he presses down with his feet Bhairava and Kālarātri,
passion and aversion.
That wisdom being, possessed of form,
one should meditate upon as arising from the syllable hūṃ.
In the heart of the wheel of mind
one should meditate upon Heruka.
The wheel of speech one should meditate upon at the mouth,
and the wheel of body as before.
Blue, red, and white,
six-armed and three-faced,
he holds vajra and bell together,
three-eyed, with transfigured faces.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
and carries a trident and a skull.
Fully united with the body of his own vidyā,
he abides in the state of great bliss.
At the centers of the four maṇḍalas,
arising from the three syllables and the rest,
with diadems of skull garlands,
endowed with their own supreme emblems,
applying the sequence of the sites and the rest,
supreme and sublime among the three vows,
the Ḍākinī and Lāmā,
Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī,
should be placed at the sites of the lotus's directions.
The four skull cups are supreme.
The Ḍākinī is yellow in color,
Lāmā the color of the palāśa flower,
Khaṇḍarohā is blue,
and Rūpiṇī is red and blazing.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
the right hand in the supreme gesture of fearlessness.
At the gates are four goddesses,
the ḍākinīs Kākāsyā and the rest,
blue, yellow, and likewise red,
and green, wrathful in form.

In the intermediate directions are Yamadāhī and the others,
fierce and utterly terrifying.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
and likewise the supreme gesture of fearlessness,
the damaru and the curved knife.
They are seated upon the four māras,
three-eyed, with hair unbound,
wearing the supreme garment of the directions,
with diadems of skull garlands,
adorned with the five mudrās.
Mirror, taste, scent,
and touch are the four offerings.
Through the sequence of the ten grounds
and the union of body, speech, and mind,
all phenomena, outer and inner,
are the purity of the factors of awakening.
The twenty-four beginning with ka,
pressed at the top with the bindu,
each consonant joined with two hūṃ,
and with phaṭ joined at the end:
these are the secret mantras of all the heroes,
which grant every desired result.
The consonant with the name of the ḍākinī,
and at the end hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ:
in this way the ḍākinī Pracaṇḍā and the others,
the very reality of mantra, grant bliss.
The vajra of body, speech, and mind is
hūṃ āḥ oṃ.
This is utterly secret.
For the ḍākinīs on the lotus petals,
the consonants together with the neuter letters,
with hūṃ as the second,
and at the end the wise join phaṭ.
The secret mantras of Kākāsyā and the others are
ya ra la va
together with the consonants,
and at the end one joins hūṃ and phaṭ.
For Yamadāhī and the others,
śa ṣa sa ha
with the consonants, two hūṃ,
and likewise phaṭ should be joined.
At the center, likewise, one recollects
the secret mantra of Buddhakapāla:
oṃ aṃ āṃ iṃ īṃ uṃ uṃ e ai aṃ eḥ ṣa svāhā
On the elements, aggregates, and sense bases
one should apply the armor.
The wheel of wisdom and the samaya wheel
should be worshiped with equal taste.
The secret mantras of the four offering goddesses
are made from each one's own name, its consonant, and hūṃ,
and in this way joined to the maṇḍalas.
Arising from Buddhakapāla,
wearing vajra ornaments,
all should be joined in their proper order.
Fully joining them in sending out and drawing in,
one should recite the four secret ones.
Through the practice of emanating and gathering,
the sequences of the three wheels:
here, through the practice of the rite,
having joined them at the four times,
one should perform the recitation of the samaya wheel.
The supreme samaya becomes accomplishment.
Of all the secret mantras of the samaya holder,
Buddhakapāla is supreme.
Through the practice outside meditative equipoise,
one should examine everything thoroughly in this way.
The samaya should always be guarded,
and the five ambrosias of wisdom are supreme.
Or else one should recite one's own name;
one should recite the very reality of mantra:
oṃ śrī buddha kapāli bruṃ āḥ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambaraṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
This is the lovely and supreme practice,
the supreme wisdom of the yoginīs.
Wherever the samaya has been broken,
this practice should be given.
When it has been pacified, one should also confer
the unsurpassed empowerment of the yoginīs.
At the four times, in sequence,
one should bathe with outer and inner water.
Or else one should take the samaya vows
and supplicate the guru:
"I am ignorant and deluded;
I have contravened and broken the samaya.
Guru, protector, grant me refuge!
Lord, holder of the vajra,
you whose nature is great compassion,
I go for refuge to the lord of beings."
Having done this without hesitation,
one will certainly become accomplished.

This concludes the twenty-sixth chapter, on restoring the samaya and the generation of Buddhakapāla, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high.

Then, furthermore, I shall explain
the secret reality, so hard to find:
the generation of all the secret mantras,
the abodes of beings and the beings themselves.
All the yoginīs and heroes
are the supreme abode of the buddhas,
inexhaustible, nondual, and utterly pure,
stainless at the beginning, middle, and end.
Arising from the wisdom of a
is Vajrasattva, the great bliss,
three-faced, six-armed, and peaceful,
three-eyed, with the mood of compassion,
perfectly endowed with the great mudrā,
pure in nature and without stain.
His head is adorned with matted locks and a half moon,
his head is marked with a crossed vajra,
he has a diadem of skull garlands,
and is adorned with the five buddhas.
He sits in the cross-legged posture of the sattva,
adorned with the six mudrās.
He holds vajra and bell together,
carries in his hands a sword and a hook,
and holds a beautiful jeweled noose.
He bestows every accomplishment.
His faces are white, blue, and red,
the supreme abode of the three gates of liberation.
The wisdom being is in his heart,
and one should also meditate on the supreme of the three wheels.
From hūṃ comes the wheel of mind,
and its hand emblems are blue;
from āḥ comes the wheel of speech,
and its hand emblems are red;
from oṃ comes the wheel of body,
and its hand emblems are white.
Heroes and yoginīs are joined in equal union,
body, speech, and mind without stain.
From raṃ comes the great mudrā;
one should imagine her at the center of the navel.
From i comes the bhaga, the lotus,
the stainless moon of awakening.
One should imagine u in the heart,
place e upon the head,
place ai at the crown,
and place o in the eyes.
Having made aṃ into armor,
a is the weapon in front.

From ḷ comes the goddess Locanā;
from ṛ comes Samayatārā;
from ḹ comes the goddess Pāṇḍaravāsinī;
from ṝ truly arises the Jewel goddess.
Jewel Lamp and Wisdom Lamp,
white, and the color of the palāśa flower,
red, and the color of the gold of the Jambū River,
they are three-faced and six-armed,
three-eyed, intoxicated with delight,
all of them clad in the directions,
joined in union with their own skillful means.
Wheel and crossed vajra,
red lotus and jewel,
vajra and bell held together,
they bestow every desire.
They hold sword, arrow, and hook,
and hold the bow drawn back;
all the marks are complete in them.
Crowned with skulls,
in the center of a multicolored lotus,
they sit in the posture of the sattva.
These are to be placed at the gates;
in the center is the moon seat of the lord;
at the corners are the four kinds of offering goddesses,
clad in the directions, their hair let loose,
three-faced and six-armed,
three-eyed, wearing garlands of skulls.
With mirror and lute,
bell, conch, and elixir,
they make offering with their hands of a drink of liquor.
In other respects they are just like their lord,
complete with all ornaments,
seated gracefully in the half-crossed posture,
with their hands cupped together,
holding offerings in their hands.
Through a rite such as this comes omniscience.
The stainless reality of the vowels,
the supreme secret so hard to find,
has been taught by Vajrasattva.
The rites of the reality of mantra
are perfectly joined to body, speech, and mind.

oṃ śrī mahā sukha vajra satva āḥ
ā hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambaraṃ svāhā
oṃ siddha locani ḷ āḥ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ samaya tāte tuttare ture ṛ āḥ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ ratna jālini ṝ āḥ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ rūpa vajri bhruṃ
oṃ śabha vajri hūṃ
oṃ rasa vajri hrīḥ

The omniscient one, the supreme lord,
one should meditate upon as dwelling in the center of the bhaga.
The vowels, the vajra, the great secret,
abide in the heart of all sentient beings.
That by which all things are secret,
its secret has now been explained.
The yogin, at the four times,
should engage in recitation and meditation.
Having abandoned all doubt,
the great being, firm in diligence,
having seen the wheel of wisdom,
the one bound by samaya makes offering and confers empowerment.
Having dissolved them into one,
through the practice of emanation and absorption,
the three realms without exception
all become like Vajradhara.
Past, present,
and future, the three times:
thus in the beings of the three times
abides the state of Vajrasattva.
The pure self of innate joy
is the supreme, without beginning.

This is the twenty-seventh chapter, on the generation of Vajrasattva, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the higher of the higher.

Next, the supreme rite is explained,
the practice of Mañjuvajra.
Arisen from the wisdom of maṃ,
Mañjuvajra, great bliss,
has six faces and six arms and is peaceful,
his body a stainless white,
three-eyed, seated in the vajra posture,
adorned with the six blazing mudrās,
crowned with Akṣobhya,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
bearing a peaceful half moon,
his matted locks bound up in a topknot.
White, blue, and saffron,
yellow and green likewise;
the upper face is peaceful,
bestowing the fruit of the deity's accomplishment.
Holding vajra and bell together,
holding sword and book,
and utpala lotus and hook,
he should be visualized with a body of wisdom.
The essence of body, speech, and mind
should be visualized in the center of the wheel.
Through the placement at the sites explained before,
just as for Vajrasattva,
one should call to mind this secret mantra.
The secret of Mañjuvajra
is the three secrets, endowed with wisdom and samaya,
the union of heroes and yoginīs.

oṃ śrī mahā sukha mañju vajra a maṃ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara svāhā

Having empowered oneself,
the one of great discipline should recite the secret mantra.
Having emanated the wheel of the three secrets,
one should gather in the three realms.
This is supremely secret;
it should not be shown to anyone at all.

This is the twenty-eighth chapter, on the practice of Mañjuvajra, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the higher of the higher.

Next, something further is to be explained:
the practice of the nine-syllable one.
Arisen from the wisdom of kṣa,
the devourer of all the wicked,
the devourer who devours the ḍākas,
who devours concepts and brings them to an end,
arisen from emptiness equal to space,
devourer of the enjoyments of space,
with six faces and twelve arms,
three-eyed, his faces contorted,
he stands with his right leg extended,
devouring the wicked māras.
Bhairava and Bhairavī
he holds pressed face down beneath his feet.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
adorned with a garland of heads,
with a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
made beautiful by a half moon,
his matted locks held down by a crossed vajra,
his body adorned with the six mudrās,
the fangs of his mouths bared,
terrifying, utterly and greatly terrifying.
Blue, smoky, green,
red, and the fifth is yellow.

The sixth is the head of a donkey;
the upper face is utterly terrifying,
the color of ash and of clouds,
utterly destroying all the wicked.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he enjoys the enjoyment of delight.
In his lap is Vajravārāhī,
of his color, her upper face the head of a sow,
joined with him in nondual union,
her hair let loose and clad in the directions,
her two thighs entwined about him,
intoxicated with the passion of compassion.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
and wears the skin of Brahmā;
a vajra spear and a ḍamaru drum,
a curved knife, a noose, and a head,
and brandishes a sword in his hand,
fierce, destroying all the wicked.
In his heart is the wisdom ḍāka,
pervaded throughout by Kākāsyā and the others.
In the center of the eight-petaled wheel,
going around from left to right,
in the charnel grounds and the secondary charnel grounds,
one should place the supreme ḍākinīs.

yaṃ raṃ laṃ vaṃ śaṃ ṣaṃ saṃ haṃ

One places the ḍākinīs of the wheel of wisdom.
Having meditated for seven days,
in a sequence of four sessions each,
by meditating at a riverbank or in a charnel ground
one will surely gain accomplishment.
Stealing away and killing,
whatever action one wishes, wherever it may be,
is accomplished by the wheel of the ḍākinīs;
in this action there is no doubt or hesitation.
Blue, the color of the palāśa flower,
red, and the fourth yellow:
these are the four, Yamadāhī and the others,
with beautiful faces of various colors.
Red, blue, and smoky,
and the white face supremely beautiful,
all of their faces three-eyed,
their hair let loose, clad in the directions.
Kākāsyā, with her left and right faces,
and Ulūkāsyā's faces likewise;
the third has the face of a dog,
the fourth the face of a sow.
Their left and right faces
have the forms of fierce goddesses, according to the rite.
The faces of Yamadāhī and the others,
both left and right, are likewise:
the thukri, the camel, the horse,
the lion, the goat, the elephant, the buffalo,
and also the face of a bear;
all should be made alike.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
carry noose and hook in their hands,
bell, ḍamaru, and curved knife,
wear a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
are adorned with the five mudrās,
and dance upon seats of pretas.
Short in stature, with long sexual organs,
their faces with fangs bared,
they are intoxicated with the taste of compassion.
The secret of body, speech, and mind
should be meditated upon and thoroughly cultivated.

hūṃ ā oṃ

This is explained as the secret:
in the center of the vajra, the lotus, the wheel,
the wisdom ḍāka in the heart,
and the wheel of the syllables of the ḍākas.
Having seen that wheel of wisdom,
one should worship it and visualize it dissolving.
Having settled in equipoise, the syllables,
the secret mantra, should be recited with the breath and its control.
Having made one's own offering of scattering and drinking,
one should emanate the wisdom of the three secrets.

oṃ śrī kṣa ḍākinī jāla sambara huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

At the beginning the consonant, the name, and hūṃ;
at the end it is joined with phaṭ.
As taught by the Ḍāka Moon,
it bestows every desired fruit.
These supreme ones are secret;
they should not be given to anyone at all.
Within seven days one gains mastery:
if one meditates one-pointedly,
the accomplishment of the ḍākinīs of the secret of the three wheels
will swiftly be attained.

This is the twenty-ninth chapter, on the practice of the Kṣa Ḍāka, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the higher of the higher.

Next, what is to be explained as supreme
is the practice of the Āli Ḍāka.
Arisen from the wisdom of the vowels,
the Āli Ḍāka, supreme and sublime,
has eight faces and sixteen arms,
three-eyed, fearsome, utterly fearsome,
the fangs of his mouths bared.
He stands with his right leg extended,
a half moon binding his matted locks,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
his crown held down by a crossed vajra,
a vajra garland upon his forehead.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
his body adorned with the six mudrās.
Conceptuality, which is Bhairava himself,
and craving and jealousy, which are Kālarātri herself,
he presses down beneath his feet.
Taming all who are hard to tame,
wearing a garland of the heads of obstructors,
of various colors and gracefully beautiful,
white in color, slender-waisted,
blazing with divine splendor.
Blue, yellow, and smoky:
so too are the faces on the right.
Green, red, and pale yellow:
the faces on the left are also beautiful in their variety.
The upper face, ash-gray, the color of clouds, and peaceful,
gazes with passion and ardent desire.
The taste of compassion is perfectly complete in him,
his body swaying and seductive.
The body of the Āli Ḍākinī
is two-armed and naked,
her head adorned with a garland of skulls,
intoxicated, with her hair let loose,
with three black and terrifying eyes,
of his color, and full-bodied.
Her two thighs entwine him well;
she abides in nondual union.
He holds vajra and bell
and squeezes the breasts of the goddess.
Just as with the mudrā of the ḍāka,
so the ḍākinī too should be joined.
Two of his arms are extended,
and he wears the skin of Viṣṇu.
He holds skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
bears sword and skull cup,
trident and ḍamaru,
axe and hook likewise,
noose and the head of Brahmā,
curved knife and wheel.
In his heart is a twelve-spoked
king of wheels, beautifully adorned.
In the center of its hub
one should visualize the wisdom ḍāka,
with his color, faces, posture,
arms, and mudrās the same.

In the east is the vajra ḍākinī called Supreme Samaya, who arises from the family of the syllable ī. In the north abides the ḍākinī called Supreme Variety, arisen from the syllable ī. In the west is the ḍākinī called Supreme Space, who should be joined with the syllable e. In the south the ḍākinī called Supreme Place abides perfectly, arisen from the syllable ai. In the intermediate directions, beginning with the direction of fire, the four ḍākinīs should be placed. First, the ḍākinī called Supreme Greatness should be joined with the syllable ū. In the quarter of Nairṛti, the ḍākinī called Supreme Fierce One should be joined with the syllable ū. In the direction of the wind, the ḍākinī called Supreme Hero should be joined with the syllable o. In the quarter of Īśāna, the ḍākinī called Supreme Wrath should be joined with the syllable au.

Likewise here, in the four intermediate directions, they are placed in the quarters beginning with Īśāna: from the syllable raṃ the ḍākinī called Supreme Accomplishment; from the syllable ḷ the ḍākinī called Supreme Charnel Ground; from the syllable ṝ the ḍākinī called Supreme Yoga; and from the syllable ḹ the ḍākinī called Supreme Ferocity.

The ḍākinīs, all of them together,
are the twelve links made pure.
They have three faces and six arms,
all three-eyed and contorted,
their bodies white; and for all of them
the two faces, blue and green, in nondual bliss,
the faces on the left and on the right,
the wise should visualize.
Clad in the directions and dancing,
their hair let loose, utterly terrifying,
they hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
ḍamaru and the supreme curved knife,
spear and head; all of them
are of various colors and bear hand emblems.
Seated upon the heads of corpses,
they are complete in the taste of compassion.
They bear the five mudrās,
all adorned with garlands of skulls,
and wear garlands of heads,
all of them crying a la la.
oṃ is the vajra of mind,
āḥ becomes speech,
a is the vajra of body,
in the center of the vajra, the lotus, the wheel.
This is supremely secret,
the supreme practice of the yoginīs,
spoken by Vajrasattva
at the request of Vajragarbha.
The ḍākinīs of the wheel of the maṇḍala,
with their retinues and all the rest, should be placed.
Having seen that wheel of wisdom,
one should meditate as well on the single taste of phenomena.
Through the practice of wisdom recitation,
one should make the scattering-and-drinking offering with one's own mantra.
Joined with the inner self,
one emanates together with the host of the supreme wheel,
and when that supreme one is seen, gathers it back again.
The twelve supreme and sublime links
are the twelve grounds themselves;
the vajra ground is the thirteenth.
The Very Joyful and the Stainless,
the Luminous and the Radiant,
the Hard to Master and the Manifest,
the Gone Far and the Immovable,
Excellent Intelligence and the Cloud of Dharma,
the Formless and the One Possessed of Wisdom:
thus, upon the twelve grounds,
the goddesses of the wheel should be placed.

In the center is the ground of Vajrasattva, called Great Delight, Supreme Bliss.

For the mantras of all the retinue and the rest,
make the name, the consonant, and hūṃ,
and join phaṭ at their end.
With the lord of mantras, the secret mantra,
one places one's own seed in one's own heart
and joins it with body, speech, and mind.
Having seen this wheel of wisdom,
one should gather it into one and worship it.
I, both outwardly and inwardly,
settled in equipoise, recite in silence.
Having made one's own scattering-and-drinking offering,
the intelligent one should recite with control of the breath.

oṃ śrī āli ḍāka a a aṃ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambar hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

All of existence, entirely and without remainder,
has the nature of no entity whatsoever:
the essence of mantra, shape, and dharma,
the very essence of union.
Since the realm of beings abides in its portions,
through the yoga of one's own deity
one should unite oneself and make offering.
Even with a mind not set in equipoise,
one should meditate and thoroughly cultivate it.
Mantra, shape, and the nature of reality:
the single taste of phenomena is emptiness.
Phenomena are selflessness itself;
upon phenomena without intrinsic nature
one should reflect again and again.

This is the thirtieth chapter, the practice of the Āli Ḍāka, the secret of the great secret, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the higher of the higher.

Next, the practice is to be explained
of the supreme Lord of Various Dances,
supreme over all, the great yoga,
who well bestows the great accomplishment.
Through the practice of the generation stage
one should meditate and thoroughly cultivate it.
In the center of a sun disk,
from siṃ oṃ dvaṃ a vajra arises.
That, transformed completely, is oneself,
become the supreme Ḍāka of the Consonants,
the Lord of Variety, of various colors,
supreme and sublime among all the families,
with six faces and twelve arms,
three-eyed, with a quaking sound,
holding a peaceful half moon,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
bearing a crossed vajra upon his head,
his matted locks bound at the crown,
his body marked with the six mudrās,
adorned with divine ornaments.
The ill-fated non-Buddhists (tīrthikas)
he presses beneath his lotus feet;
the succession of the five views
he presses beneath his extended right leg.
His feet dance in the half-crossed posture,
and he is adorned with six legs.
He wears the skin of Viṣṇu,
adorned with a garland of heads,
and is embraced by the vajra goddess,
naked, with apron and girdle,
her hair let loose, three-eyed,
pressing lip upon lip,
vajra and bell entwined together,
pressing the place of the limb of awakening,
her two thighs entwined about him,
blissful with the taste of compassionate passion.
The limbs of the skin of Brahmā
he holds stretched out with two hands.
He bears skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
holds the head of Maheśvara (Śiva),
trident and sword,
and carries ḍamaru, noose, and hook.
White, blue, and green,
yellow, smoky, and saffron.
On the left and right sides,
at Pīlava and Upapīlava,
the two ḍākinīs should be joined.
The vajra ḍākinī
called Supreme Dharma
arises perfectly from the syllable ṇi;
she should be placed on the left side.
Arisen with certainty from the syllable si,
the vajra ḍākinī
called Supreme Ocean
is of various colors, with various faces,
three-eyed, her countenance contorted,
standing in the half-crossed posture,
her feet dancing.

She has five faces and ten arms,
three-eyed, fierce, and terrifying;
Bhairava and Kālarātri
she holds pressed beneath her feet.
Her throats are six and four;
she is adorned with the five mudrās,
clad in the directions, her hair let loose,
with a girdle made of a garland of skulls,
with anklets tinkling with little bells,
adorned with a garland of skulls.
In the center of the moon disk
is the supreme and sublime embrace.
Brandishing the vajra, with the threatening forefinger,
they are adorned in nonduality.
Adorned with the nondual skull cup,
they carry noose and hook in their hands,
skull cup and the supreme curved knife,
wearing the crown ornament of body, speech, and mind,
and bestowing every accomplishment.
With the wisdom ḍākinī in the center,
one should meditate as well on the method of the three wheels.
The wisdom ḍāka of oṃ
is in the center of the wheel of mind;
the ḍākas of body, speech, and mind,
these one should visualize in one's own heart.
Through the sequence of practice explained before,
the succession of the sites and the rest,
one should imagine and visualize them there
and emanate the three in their sequence.
Thereby comes nondual accomplishment;
one should meditate together with the ḍākinī.
Having meditated on the tastes
of the wheel of wisdom, one should recite the secret mantra.

oṃ śrī praṇa bod ḍāka ā ā oṃ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara oṃ svāhā
oṃ hriṃ dharma uttame mahā vajra ḍākinīye dvaṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ sīṃ sāgar uttame mahā vajra ḍākiniya sīṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

When one recites the wisdom of the three secrets,
one should recite the heroes and ḍākinīs:
the consonant, one's own name, and hūṃ twice,
and at the end it should be joined with phaṭ.
Having meditated on the wheel of the three secrets,
having gathered the three and meditated on them,
through the practice of the samaya of the three secrets
one should meditate in the center of the bhaga.
The stainless moon of awakening,
the wheel of mind, the three nādas:
the lords are three,
three ḍākinīs and three ḍākas,
who gather in and who generate.
Vajravārāhī in the bhaga
one should visualize as the three secrets.
Through the practice of meditating on reality,
therefore, having abandoned pride,
through practice at the four times,
one should meditate and recite in this way.
One should always guard the samaya.
What arises from the various milks,
the great brahmin, honey, and butter,
together with mahā māṅsa,
consecrated with the three realities,
one should partake of as food.
Without bustle and without craving,
with no retinue to look after,
enjoying through what is not enjoyment,
one eats the deathless food
and relies also on the five qualities of desire.
Eating the food that is no enjoyment,
one drinks the meditation of yogic meditation,
and otherwise drinks nothing at all.
Joined with the inner self,
having blessed oneself:
the Blessed Ḍāka is Pīlava,
the yoginī is Upapīlava;
method is called Pīlava,
wisdom is Upapīlava;
the outskirts of a city are Pīlava,
the outskirts of a village Upapīlava;
the union of moon and sun
is Pīlava and Upapīlava;
the instantaneous union is Pīlava,
and joy is Upapīlava.
Endowed with many powers,
one is accomplished in this very life.
All the paśu are to be eaten,
and the food offerings for the deities are likewise to be prepared.
When heroes and ḍākinīs have gathered,
having assembled at a fitting time,
with song and dance and food,
one should worship with the supreme gathering.
Having abandoned all conceptual thought,
one practices the conduct of samaya.

This is the thirty-first chapter, teaching the signs of the practice of the Ḍāka of the Consonants, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the higher of the higher.

Next, something further is to be explained:
the sequence of practice for accomplishment,
the union of ḍākinī and ḍāka.
It is the binding (saṃvara) of the supreme samaya,
the binding of all phenomena,
the great bliss of the ḍākinīs,
abiding in the center of the vajra of the sphere of space,
the supreme and sublime samaya maṇḍala.
In its center is a great wheel
adorned with five maṇḍalas,
and in the center a beautiful sun seat,
endowed equally with vowels and consonants,
possessing the thirty-two major marks,
adorned with the eighty excellent minor marks.
Having performed the blessing,
one generates him from one's own seed:
arisen perfectly from the syllable hi,
utterly terrifying like the fire at the end of time,
the fangs of his mouths bared,
three-eyed, his faces with lolling tongues,
he presses all the wicked beneath his feet,
with wrathful and laughing faces,
raging even at the end of the eon,
with a crown ornament of matted locks.
He stands with his right leg extended,
adorned with a garland of heads;
with the skin of Mahābhairava
a girdle is bound about his waist.
With a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
he bears a crossed vajra and a half moon,
and on his forehead a vajra garland.
Seeing the wrathful one, one should contemplate him.
He crushes even all the gods,
holds the observance of the skeleton-bearer,
is the supreme reviver of the dead,
and surely subdues all the wicked.
His body is adorned with the six mudrās,
decked with various ornaments;
he is six-armed and five-faced,
possessing the supreme essence of the various tastes.
Blue and yellow, and likewise red,
green, and the white one peaceful.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he embraces the body of Vārāhī.
He holds the skin of the Great God (Śiva),
stretched out in the manner of a canopy;
he holds skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
and in the sixth hand a ḍamaru.
With his left foot he presses down Kālarātri,
and with his right, Bhairava;
pressing them down beneath his feet,
he makes them stupefied and utterly intoxicated.
Vajravārāhī, of his color,
her hair let loose and naked,
with a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
and a girdle of ornaments made of pieces,
her two thighs entwined well about him,
embraces him at the neck, intoxicated.
She bears his emblems and mudrās,
and holds the curved knife and the supreme ḍamaru,
and in the sixth hand the head of Brahmā.
Vajraḍāka, in this form,
the wise one should place in the center of the grid.
In the east is Buddha Ḍāka,
in the north is Karma Ḍāka,
in the west is Padma Ḍāka,
in the south is Ratna Ḍāka:
white and blue and red,
green and yellow.

Holding wheel and bell together,
he stands with his right leg extended.
Green, yellow, red, blue,
and white, the upper face gazing upward;
with bell and crossed vajra he embraces great bliss,
standing in the posture with the right leg extended.
Red, blue, yellow, and green,
and white likewise:
with lotus and bell he is in union,
squeezing the breasts of the goddess.
Yellow, blue, and likewise red,
green, and the color white:
with jewel and bell he is in union,
standing with his right leg extended.
These are the five ḍākas,
and they are also the five saṃvaras.
In the four quarters of Fire, Nairṛti, Wind,
and Īśāna are
Buddha Ḍākinī, Karma Ḍākinī,
Padma Ḍākinī, and Ratna Ḍākinī,
three-faced and six-armed,
three-eyed, their hair let loose,
adorned with garlands of skulls,
complete with all ornaments,
adorned with the five mudrās,
clad in the directions, supreme in dance,
adorned with garlands of heads.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
are adorned with vajra, arrow, and the rest,
noose and hook beautiful in their hands.
Granting every desire,
perfectly complete in the taste of compassion,
intoxicated with the longing of compassion,
with slightly bashful sidelong glances,
they are blissful, bestowing delight.
Thus by many and various ones
the maṇḍalas are pervaded throughout:
Bahurūpā and Lovely Form,
Vajra Lip and Light Maker,
Horse Face and Fierce Face,
Vajra Fierce Lady and the Renowned One,
Vajradūtī and Vajranakhī,
and Vajrakumārī likewise,
and Kāmbojī likewise,
Vajravetālī,
and Vajramātṛkā likewise,
the Fragrant One and Terrifying Face,
and Moon Crest likewise,
Bhṛkuṭī and Viśvarūpā,
and Vajrakartarī likewise,
Vajraśṛṅkhalā,
the goddess Māyā and Fame Maker,
Nalarki and Piṅgalā,
Vajrajvālā, the One with Joined Palms,
and Vajrabhairavī herself:
these are the ḍākinīs who accomplish the maṇḍala.
All of those ḍākinīs
are three-eyed, their faces contorted,
pot-bellied, their hair let loose,
adorned with garlands of skulls,
perfectly complete with all ornaments,
adorned with the five mudrās,
clad in the directions, supreme in dance,
adorned with garlands of heads,
all seated on seats of corpses,
of various forms, captivating the mind.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
carry noose and hook in their hands,
brandish the supreme vajra,
with bell and the supreme ḍamaru;
their three faces heroic and alluring,
perfectly complete in the sentiment of compassion,
they gaze with sidelong eyes
and sway with the exertions of delight.
Granting every accomplishment,
they are loving toward all practitioners.
In six months the practitioner
accomplishes the wheel of the ḍākinīs.
Without doubt, the supreme accomplishment
the ḍākinīs cause to be attained.
At this very time, immediately,
there is no doubt one will move in the sky.
The inner palace itself
has nine squares and so on, together with gates.
At the gates four goddesses are set,
and the ḍākinīs grant accomplishment.
One should draw the goddesses of the outer maṇḍala:
there, on the four sides of the band,
twenty goddesses should be placed,
the ḍākinīs and the others, in the directions and intermediate directions.
Four-cornered and four-gated,
thus the outer maṇḍala is laid out.
At the gates are four goddesses
who grant every desired fruit.
It is adorned with four portals;
all the portions of the corners,
the gates, and the junctions of the gate recesses
are adorned with vajras and jewels.
One should draw the lines of the outer maṇḍala;
within it nine squares,
and the gates too, are to be made.
Thus the ḍākas and ḍākinīs,
the binding of the net, are emptiness;
the thirty-seven factors of awakening
are the purity of phenomena.
In the center are the thirteen grounds;
outside, the sites and the secondary sites,
the charnel grounds and the secondary charnel grounds.
Purified through practice in this way,
one should meditate on the supreme Saṃvara.
That is the wisdom of mind:
the wisdom ḍākas and ḍākinīs.

To the aggregates, elements, and sense sources
one should apply both of the rites.
This is the vow of supreme wisdom:
set out the wheel of the three secrets.
Arrange it half and half.
The three ḍākas are in the center of the wheel,
the great arising of samaya yoga.
Having summoned the wisdom wheel
and offered water for the feet, one should make it enter.
Having empowered with the water of wisdom,
one should meditate on the lords.
The supreme samaya of all the tastes
of selfless phenomena is stainless.
From all the pores of the body
emanating the samaya of the three secrets,
one should consecrate all sentient beings without exception
as samaya ḍākas.
Then, gathering them in with effort,
one should make them enter the three secrets.
The ḍākas who were accomplished before
should be set in the places of blessing.
This is the great Vajra Vehicle,
abiding in the place of the ḍākas' conduct.
Recitation of the supreme Saṃvara
grants the unsurpassed state.
At the four times, as one wishes,
one should meditate always, without weariness.
In forward order and in reverse,
one should emanate the supreme wheel.
From the pores of the body, in front,
emanating Cakrasaṃvara, one should make offerings.
Having empowered the three vajras,
one should meditate on the wrathful one of cessation.
Together with the wisdom wrathful one,
one should recite like the thunder of the clouds.
Through the practice of going and coming,
the great vajra discipline of silence,
within seven days, with certainty,
might and power will be accomplished:
making things shake and sway,
agitating, distracting, and causing possession,
making the healthy sick,
and making the sick healthy.
Having meditated in this way,
there is no doubt in these rites.
One endowed with the yoga of the inner self,
who delights in meditation and recitation
and delights in samaya food,
is unobstructed, and his life is firm.
Reciting thus according to one's own rite,
one properly enters the state of a buddha.
There the wise should meditate on,
worship, and recite the samaya wheel.
I shall explain these secret mantras:
oṃ śrī vajra ḍāka adhi hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī buddha ḍāka abhruṃ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī karma ḍāka akhaṃ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī padma ḍāka hri hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī ratna ḍāka aṃ hūṃ ḍākinī la sambara huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
Those four ḍākas,
and the Ḍākinī, Lāmā,
Khaṇḍarohā, and Rūpiṇī,
their necks evenly entwined,
are well embraced with both legs.
Their colors, arms and faces,
and their hand emblems too, one should visualize.
Having made the goddesses manifest,
these are their respective heart mantras:
oṃ śrī vajra vārāhi vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī ḍākini ḍāṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī lāme lāṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī khaṇḍarohe khaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī rūpiṇi rūṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

These are of the nature of the five. The mantras of the ḍākinīs of the intermediate directions are:
oṃ śrī buddha ḍākini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī karma ḍākini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī padma ḍākini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī ratna ḍākini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

The mantras of the ḍākinīs at the gates
grant every fruit that is desired.
The four are of various colors,
pervading all according to their families.
oṃ hūṃ rūbe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ hūṃ surūpe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra dunti huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ prabhākari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
On the eastern band
the four goddesses are to be placed,
white in the color of their bodies;
their secret mantras are taught:
oṃ vajra vaḍavamukhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ ghomukhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra ghoṇe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ yaśaspini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
On the southern band
the four deities should be placed.
The color of their bodies is yellow;
their mantras are taught:
oṃ vajra dūti huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra nakhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra kaumāri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra kaṃboji huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

On the western band, these are the mantras of the four goddesses who are red in the color of their bodies:
oṃ vajra betāli huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra sīka huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra garḍari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra bimabadāne huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

On the northern band are four goddesses, green in the color of their bodies; their secret mantras are taught:
oṃ candraśekhare huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ bhṛkuṭi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ biśvarūpe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra katayani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

In the four intermediate directions, beginning with Īśāna in the northeast, are four goddesses with the colors of a twofold form. Their mantras are taught:
oṃ vajra śṛṃkhale huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra māle huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra yaśaskari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajrā nalārke huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

At the gates are four goddesses of various colors, endowed with fierce forms. Their mantras are taught. In the east:
oṃ vajra piṅgale jaḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
In the south:
oṃ vajra jvalani hūṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
In the west:
oṃ vajra kuṇḍali baṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
In the north:
oṃ vajra bhairava ho huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

In all of them, at the end of the consonants,
one joins śrī, following the supreme order.
If the mantrin recites them in this way,
he attains the accomplishment of the Saṃvara
of the net of ḍākinīs,
and within six months gains mastery of bliss.
If they are made one and gathered together,
the great yogin, endowed with splendor,
will accomplish the supreme net.
If all these secret mantras
are recited ten thousand or a hundred thousand times,
the king of secret mantras is accomplished.
By reciting the heart mantras,
the heroes and ḍākinīs are accomplished,
and one becomes whatever one wishes.
Entering into the bodies of others,
and attaining invisibility,
the great yogin roams about
and takes on various forms.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-second chapter, called "The King of Maṇḍalas from Which the Samaya Vow Arises."

Next, I shall explain something further:
the sequence of the rite of Buddha Ḍāka.
One should follow the sequence of the scripture and so forth
of Buddha Ḍāka, explained before.
A yogin who does not know the scripture
is a Buddhist tīrthika;
one should never abandon the scripture.
Not abandoning the Three Jewels,
"To the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha
I always go for refuge."
If one focuses on the perfection of wisdom,
the accomplishment of yoga is achieved.
Through the practice of instantaneous entry,
one should imagine one's own body:
blue and fierce, with a contorted face,
ferocious and terrifying, striking terror,
the fangs of the mouth bared,
matted locks bound at the crown with a half-moon,
the locks held down by a crossed vajra,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
complete with every ornament,
with a body arisen from baṃ hūṃ vajra,
fully endowed with the six seals,
wearing a tiger skin as a garment,
seated well in the half-crossed posture,
three-eyed, with a fierce body,
four-necked and four-faced,
fully endowed with twelve arms,
the perfect, supreme wisdom of emptiness,
with a garland of skulls of all the vicious,
wearing a necklace arisen from the end of time,
pressing all the vicious beneath his feet.
Bhairava, who is the afflictions, with his head,
craving, delusion, and jealousy,
and Kālarātri lying face down,
the vicious and terrifying obstructors,
all are pressed beneath his feet.
Sky-colored, pale,
white, and red,
and green, the face that looks upward.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he embraces Vārāhī in bliss.
Through union that is nondual throughout,
all phenomena are of one taste:
this is called the ascetic virtues.
The glorious great bliss
is Vajra Heruka himself.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga staff,
a Dharma wheel and a ringing staff,
an alms bowl, a spear, and a noose,
a hook, a head, and a hand drum.
With his right and left hands
he stretches out a human skin and wears it.
In the east are Mātaṅgī and Ḍombikā,
Rajakī and Pukkasī,
Khaṭakī and Śabarī,
Jivārī and Samarī.
These eight goddesses
are to be placed in due order.
Yellow, blue, and likewise red,
and the color of smoke,
red, green, and a smoky color;
the eighth is white.
To be placed at the gates are
Herukī, Vajracaṇḍī,
the Vajra Supreme Sister,
and the fierce Vajrabhairavī,
who devour all who are hard to tame.
They are the color of ash clouds, green,
red, and blue, exceedingly terrifying.
The eight in the middle hold a Dharma ringing staff,
a skull cup, a hand drum, and a curved knife,
and hold a garment upon the shoulder.
At the gates are four goddesses
with a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga, the gesture of fearlessness,
a Dharma ringing staff, and a hand drum;
they are one-faced and four-armed.
Upon a seat of a hungry ghost
they sit in the dancing half-crossed posture.
Living on alms, the three robes,
not taking food afterward, always sitting,
accepting any place, a single seat,
the open air, the foot of a tree,
the wilderness, the charnel ground,
rags from the rubbish heap, and felt:
by these, in their due order,
one meditates on the pure ascetic virtues.
The supreme samaya of the sameness of wisdom
one should meditate on as one taste.
oṃ śrī mahāsukha vajra he he ru ru kaṃ āḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ maṃ matāṃgī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ ḍaṃ vajra ḍaṃbinī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ raṃ rājakī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ buṃ pukkasī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ khaṃ khaṭakī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ śaṃ śabarī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ jiṃ jibarī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ saṃ samarī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ heṃ herukī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ baṃ vajra caṇḍī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ hūṃ vajra rūkṣasī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ bhai vajra bhairapī huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
All of them are naked, with loosened hair,
three-eyed and peaceful,
with fangs bared and tongues lolling,
their bodies adorned with the five seals,
wearing head ornaments of garlands of skulls,
with the moods of laughter, peace, compassion,
grace, and menace,
ferocity and wrath.
Reciting and meditating, one emanates and gathers in,
and abides in bliss in body, speech, and mind.
When beings of various inclinations
are thoroughly trained, by the means of taming,
in the domain of bliss,
the fruit of unsurpassed wisdom is attained.
Through this all sentient beings are happy;
through the enjoyment of deathless bliss
the fruit of wisdom is found.
This is the attainment by all sentient beings
of the supreme state.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-third chapter, on generating the ascetic virtues.

Next, I shall explain something further:
the sequence of the rite for bringing down rain.
On the hub, together with its filaments,
of a supreme eight-petaled lotus,
in its center is the Great Vajra,
fangs bared and terrifying,
eight-armed and four-faced,
three-eyed, terrifying even the terrifying.
He brandishes the vajra weapon,
and has the threatening forefinger with a noose;
he holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga staff,
carries a hook and a spear,
wears and holds the skin of a hero,
and a hand drum is said to be the eighth.
With his outstretched right leg
he presses down the terrifying, intoxicated water god.
His locks are bound with a half-moon,
he is adorned with a crossed vajra,
his body is adorned with the six seals,
he is adorned with a garland of skulls,
he wears a tiger skin as a garment,
with a supreme diadem of a garland of skulls.
Biting his lip, his face contorted,
he gazes wrathfully and looks on with terror.
On the eight petals one should draw the eight nāgas:
Ananta and Vāsuki,
Takṣaka, Padma, Mahāpadma,
Śaṅkhapāla and Karkoṭaka,
and Nanda; these are the eight.
White, blue, red, and green,
yellow, and smoke-colored,
multicolored, and yellow.
With palms joined together,
facing toward the Blessed One,
in fear they beg for his kindness
and remain gazing in terror.
The Blessed One, blue in body,
is terrifying and strikes great terror.
The right face is a smoky red,
the left a smoky green;
the upper face is the face of a garuḍa,
contorted and terrifying.

He has the splendor of a red sun,
a light equal to twelve suns.
Vārāhī embraces him at the neck
and entwines him well with both legs.
Or else he is twelve-armed,
the supreme great bliss, twelvefold:
the curved knife, the head of Brahmā,
fully endowed with vajra and bell.
He presses the lips of the goddess.
She is naked, with her hair loosened,
three-eyed, and wearing a girdle,
her body the supreme gold of the Jambu river;
they abide in nondual union.
The nāgas with their own wives,
and Varuṇa too with his wife,
Varuṇa white in body,
beg for his kindness.
At the heart are the wisdom wheels;
one should generate their colors, arms, and faces too.
At its heart one should place hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ,
and likewise for body, speech, and mind.
In the very hearts of the nāgas themselves
one should place hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ.
The rite of the king of secret mantras
should be recited according to the words of the wrathful one.
On a riverbank or by the ocean,
at a well or a nāga lake,
or at ponds and pools,
the intelligent one should do the recitation of the wrathful one.
Or else, in a dwelling of the nāgas,
one should engage in meditation and recitation.
oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ hūṃ nāṃ nāga barṣāpaya hūṃ ḍākinī jāl sambara hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
Having meditated on these, one should recite about ten thousand times,
a thousand at each of the four times.
Within seven days, with certainty,
rain will fall everywhere on all the lands.
At the time of the samaya fire offering and worship,
accompanied by song and dance,
one should eat and drink milk.
The yogin should recite the mantras of the eight nāgas.
By the rite of bathing in milk,
one should make the fire offering, burning and pouring milk.
One should use firewood from milk-sap trees,
and kindling sticks from milk-sap trees,
and with milk one should make the fire offering.
One should fill a vase completely with milk.
In the intermediate directions, beginning with that of Agni, the god of fire, and in the cardinal directions,
one should give tormas with milk.
oṃ aṃ ananāya hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ baṃ busukai hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ taṃ takṣaka hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ paṃ padma hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ maṃ mahāpadma hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ śaṃ śaṃkhapāla hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ kaṃ karkoṭa hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ aṃ anabatapta hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
oṃ hūṃ baṃ baruṇāya nāgādhipataye nāga barṣapataye hūṃ nāṃ hūṃ svāhā
Reciting these together with the wrathful one,
one should do about ten thousand recitations of them all.
One should eat milk and old rice as food,
with senses subdued, in complete silence.
Directly, dispensing with a drawn image,
if one recites about ten thousand times, they will appear.
The accomplishment is direct,
and by merely speaking one brings down rain.
Do not doubt this!
Into a silver vessel
one should pour white mustard seed and water.

Over it one should recite twenty-one times. One should bathe an image of the Buddha with urine mixed with milk. From kneaded earth of the riverbank one should make the figure of a nāga, with its head pointing west, and empower its nine heads with the water. Then one should place it in the river. Immediately the god sends down rain, and rain falls on Jambudvīpa.

The lands become filled with grain,
and the forests flourish.
All sentient beings abide in the touch of bliss.
Taking on in turn the form of the wrathful one,
having meditated on oneself in accordance with the tantra,
by meditating on Heru Vajra
in one's own heart alone,
rain will also fall.

If one climbs to the top of a mountain and recites over a trident, hail is cut off. If one recites over a hand drum, a spreading fire is stopped. If one recites over a noose, one summons. If one recites over a skull cup, one draws forth blood. If one recites over an axe, one kills. If one recites over a khaṭvāṅga, one pacifies. If one recites over a curved knife, one kills those who roam the three worlds. If one recites over a head, one raises the dead. If one brandishes a vajra while reciting, one gains the accomplishment of the underworld. If one recites over a bell, one summons those who are unwilling. If one recites over an elephant hide and spreads it out, one flies through the sky, travels a hundred thousand leagues, and comes back again. If one recites over the six seals, one becomes invisible before others.

One roams everywhere on the earth
and dwells however one pleases.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-fourth chapter, on the rite of bringing down rain.

Next, I shall explain something further:
summarizing the supreme secret,
meditating on oneself in an instant,
one should take the form of glorious Heruka:
blue, fierce, and contorted,
fierce, three-eyed, with six supreme faces,
his matted locks bearing a half-moon,
a crossed vajra on his supreme head,
wearing a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
adorned with a vajra garland,
with a necklace of heads on his body,
adorned with the six seals,
wearing a tiger skin as a garment,
his body adorned by Vārāhī,
standing on a single leg.
The right face is yellow and smoke-colored,
the left is red and green,
and the upper face is a horse face,
white and cloud-colored, fierce and terrifying.
He is endowed with vajra and bell together,
and presses the lips of the goddess.
He wears the skin of a Victor as a garment,
and upholds the family of the ḍākinīs.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
is adorned with the vajra emblem,
holds a noose and a hook in his hands,
holds a hand drum and a head,
and likewise a curved knife and a noose:
twelve arms are thus taught.
The entire maṇḍala too
one should visualize as standing on one leg.
An elephant face and a sparrow face,
and likewise a lion face;
the first is a dog face,
the second an owl face,
the third a pig face,
and the fourth has a crow face.
Embodying the dharma of the shape of the abodes,
he takes on great and various forms,
infinite, renowned as all-victorious,
omniscient and all-encompassing,
bearing whatever body he wishes.
Thus one should meditate and visualize.
Like a jewel of many forms,
the great being sports in play.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-fifth chapter, on accomplishing the manifold.

Next, I shall explain something further:
the practice of the supreme Vārāhī.
Through the practice of the generation stage,
one should meditate on oneself and visualize:
her body has the splendor of twelve suns,
a body like vermilion, unmoving,
like the bandhūka flower.
She is three-faced and six-armed,
complete with every ornament,
and sits in the posture of a sattva.
She wears a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
and holds vajra and bell together.
With her lovely hair she embraces him,
pressing the lips of the Method's face.
She holds an arrow and a bow,
drawing the bow to the end of her ear.
She holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
and summons with the supreme hook.
She sits in the center of a red lotus
and bestows all that is desired.
Or else, twelve-armed
and six-faced, the body of the goddess arises.
Her upper face is the face of a wild boar,
fierce, splendid, with a terrifying brilliance.
On the eastern petal is Vajraguhyottamā;
in the left direction, Vajrasamayottamā;
in the western direction, Vajratejottamā;
in the southern direction, Vajraratnottamā;
in the direction of Agni, Vajrajñānottamā;
in the direction of Nairṛti, Vajravidyottamā;
in the direction of Vāyu, Vajrasiddhottamā;
in the direction of Īśāna, Vajrabhasmottamā.
At the gates are Vajrajvalottamā,
Vajratejottamā,
Vajrakrodhottamā,
and Vajradaṃṣṭrottamā.
All of these goddesses
are red in color and three-faced,
six-armed and three-eyed,
and sit in the posture of a sattva.
Their hair is loosened,
they are adorned with every ornament,
and, holding vajra and bell together,
they embrace the method of their own bodies.
They hold a noose and a hook,
a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga.
In the four corners one should place
those who draw bows and arrows to the full.
At the gates they stand with the right leg outstretched,
naked, with contorted faces,
all of them standing upon seats of hungry ghosts,
brandishing vajras, with threatening forefingers,
and endowed with nooses.
Moreover, mutually,
the retinue and the rest pay homage in due order.
At their hearts are wisdom goddesses,
to be visualized together with body, speech, and mind.
Through the yoga of the wisdom maṇḍala,
the taste of phenomena dissolves in sameness.
Having emanated by the recitation of wisdom,
one should gather them in by the sequence of the rite.
Through the recitation of the supreme samaya,
the reality of the moon will be accomplished.
oṃ śrī vajra vārāhi ā vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra guhyottame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ śrī vajra samayottame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra teja uttame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra ratnottame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra jñāna uttame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra badyī uttame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra siddhottame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra bhasmottame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra jvalottame jaḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra amṛtottame huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra krodhottame baṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī vajra daṃṣṭrottame hoḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
In the order of these,
one should meditate and recite.
Having gathered in and emanated,
all phenomena and all that bears them
are purified through the yoga of one's own deity.
One should carefully examine all sentient beings.
The abode of yoga is the Dharma;
one should lead them into the three secrets.
The abode of the three secrets is the Dharma,
and the Dharma too is likewise thus.
The selfless phenomenon is the Dharma;
phenomena do not come from phenomena.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-sixth chapter, called "The Supreme Samaya of the Three Secrets."

Next, I shall explain something further:
the sequence of the rite of the six yoginīs.
Having drawn a lotus of five petals,
its hub covered with filaments,
in its center is Vārāhī,
red, with a light blazing in many colors,
three-faced and beautiful with six arms,
three-eyed, with loosened hair,
wearing a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
the supreme one who wears the directions as her garment,
in the half-crossed posture,
her body adorned with the six seals,
wearing a garland of heads.
All the vicious māras,
and Kālarātri lying face down,
she keeps pressed beneath her feet.
Her faces are red, blue, and green;
she is adorned with the five buddhas.
She holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
carries a noose and a hook in her hands,
and holds a head and a curved knife.
She bestows every accomplishment.
At her heart is the wisdom samaya,
arisen from union in body, speech, and mind.
In the east is the goddess Yāminī;
going around to the left, one should arrange
Mohanī and Saṃcālanī,
Saṃtrāsanī and Caṇḍikā.
They are three-eyed, with loosened hair,
one-faced and four-armed,
blue, white, and yellow,
green, and smoke-colored.
They hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
a bell, a hand drum, and a curved knife,
wear head ornaments of garlands of skulls,
are adorned with the five seals,
wear the directions as their garment, supreme in dance,
are adorned with garlands of heads,
and bear Akṣobhya as a diadem.
Theirs is the bliss of the mood of compassion,
joined together in nondual union.
The supreme ones, Vajrasattva and the rest, are these:
Vārāhī with Vajrasattva,
Yāminī with Vairocana,
Mohanī with Padmanarteśvara,
Saṃcālanī with Heruka,
Saṃtrāsanī with Vajrasūrya,
and Caṇḍikā with Paramāśva.
Vajrasattva is white in body,
Vairocana is yellow,
Padmanarteśvara is red,
glorious Heruka is blue,
Vajrasūrya is red,
and Paramāśva is green.
Or else one should meditate on them individually.
Vajrasattva is three-faced,
six-armed and three-eyed,
adorned with the emblems of the six seals.
He holds a vajra, a bell, a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga,
a sword, a hand drum, and a head,
wears a human skin, and is dancing.
All the heroes, Vairocana and the rest,
are one-faced and four-armed,
supreme ones marked by a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga,
a hand drum, and a bell.
Or else all of them should be visualized
as twelve-armed and six-faced.
Their secret mantras are these:
oṃ śrī oṃ baṃ baṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī haṃ yoṃ yaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī hrīṃ moṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī hreṃ hriṃ saṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī hūṃ hūṃ saṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śrī phaṭ phaṭ caṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
These are the secret mantras of the six yoginīs.

oṃ haḥ baṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ namaḥ hi bhruṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ svāhā hu hri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ bauṣeṭa he hi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ hūṃ hūṃ ho traṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ phaṭ haṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
These are the mantras of the six heroes.
Merely by meditating on these recitations,
the six wheels are supremely accomplished,
and one attains the state of a universal monarch,
becoming king of the six universal monarchs,
invincible, victorious, fully perfected;
one finds great strength and supreme prowess.
If one always worships the six hand emblems
and praises them according to the rite,
that is swiftly accomplished,
and one also attains the supreme state of king of yogins.
Having accomplished invisibility and the like,
one roams throughout the three realms,
and the sentient beings of the six destinies are liberated.
Through meditation one becomes equal to Vajradhara.
Or else, on a six-cornered wheel
is the great bliss, Vairocana,
Mañjuvajra, the king,
lord of the universal monarchs,
like saffron in color,
with a light that blazes with radiance,
fully endowed with the great seal,
the very nature of the dharmadhātu.
With his right and left hands
holding vajra and bell, he embraces his consort.
A sword, a book, a khaṭvāṅga,
a skull cup, a hook, a noose,
an utpala lotus, and a hand drum.
His faces are saffron-colored, blue,
red, and green, and the upper face is white:
the great purity of the dharmadhātu,
which thoroughly liberates the realm of sentient beings.
Arranged going to the left from the east,
including Paramāśva with Caṇḍikā,
by placing them there
one accomplishes Mañjuvajra himself.
White, red, and yellow,
blue, red, white, blue, and green:
in the six limbs are the heroes
together with the ḍākinīs:
Vajrasattva with Vārāhī,
Vairocana with Yāminī,
Padmanarteśvara with Mohanī,
Heruka with Saṃcālanī,
Vajrasūrya with Saṃtrāsanī,
green and smoke-colored;
one should visualize their body colors thus.
The heroes are one-faced and four-armed;
the goddesses are two-armed
and hold a skull cup and a curved knife.
The heroes hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
their own emblems, in nondual union,
fully endowed with a bell as well,
supreme ones holding a hand drum.
They are three-eyed and stand with the right leg outstretched,
wearing skirts of tiger skin,
wearing head ornaments of garlands of skulls,
and wearing necklaces of heads;
all of them stand upon hungry ghosts.
In the center, the lord presses down Bhairava and Kālarātri,
and the goddess entwines him with both legs,
her hair loosened, wearing the directions as her garment.
At the heart is the wisdom samaya,
to be joined with body, speech, and mind.
One should properly apply the twofold armor.
Having meditated on the wheel of the sites,
one should arrange the heroes and ḍākinīs.
The three secrets abide at the heart.
The ḍākinīs, the Crow-Faced and the rest,
are to be arranged at the outer gates;
in the corners, Yamadāḍhī and the rest.
One should visualize them all as naked,
with the colors and emblems explained before,
seated properly upon seats of hungry ghosts,
standing with the right leg outstretched,
fierce, with loosened hair,
all wearing garlands of skulls.
Outside and inside oneself,
the six perfections are purity;
one should meditate, and meditate intensively.
Through the practice of emanating and gathering in,
one should recite the secret wheel.
When reciting the heroes and yoginīs,
one should meditate, and meditate intensively.
Having emanated the hosts of the wheel of emanations,
one brings the three kinds of beings to fulfillment.
By meditating on the inner self,
having meditated on the secret of the three wheels, one should recite.
With the three realities together with the mantras,
the heroes and the ḍākinīs,
with huṃ hūṃ phaṭ at the end,
one should do the recitation of the three wheels.
In accordance with the rite of yoga,
the one who holds the samaya should recite it.
oṃ śrī mañju vajra āḥ hūṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ haḥ vajra satva huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ baṃ vajra vārāhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ namaḥ hi vajra vairocan huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ haṃ yoṃ yamani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ svāhā hu padma narteśvari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ hrīṃ moṃ mohani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ bauṣaṭ he heruka huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ hrī hrīṃ saṃcalani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ hūṃ hūṃ ho vajra sūrya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ hūṃ hūṃ saṃtrāsani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ phaṭ haṃ parmāśva huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ phaṭ phaṭ caṇḍika huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ kākāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ ulūkāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ śvānāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ sūkarasye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ yamadāḍhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ yamadūti huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ yamadaṃṣṭriṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ yamamathani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
Having set the two armors
on the left and right hands,
one performs all the rites
in whatever way one wishes.
If one performs the rites without doubt,
accomplishment is certain, and not otherwise.
Or else the Protector, master of the ten,
abides in the ten perfections:
generosity, moral discipline, patience, diligence,
meditative concentration, and wisdom as the sixth,
skillful means, power, aspiration,
and primordial wisdom: these are the purity of the gatekeepers.
The Very Joyous and the Stainless,
the Luminous and the Radiant,
the Difficult to Conquer and the Manifest,
the Far-Reaching and the Immovable,
Excellent Intelligence and the Cloud of Dharma:
by these one should meditate on purity.
The four, Yamadāḍhī and the rest,
one should visualize as the noble truths:
suffering and its origin,
cessation and the path.
The Blessed One, glorious Mañjuvajra,
is the purity of the dharmadhātu,
the very nature of primordial wisdom.
Thus one should meditate on purity.
The path of nondual union
is secret and supremely secret;
through the power of yoga it is the supreme secret.
The practitioner should train well in what is taught,
in the divisions of the lords as they are,
and in the sequence of the guru.
Thus the rite, in nondual
union, is the supreme samaya,
the means and the cause of all accomplishments,
the great secret of the yoginī's mind,
which bestows the fruit of all rites.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-seventh chapter, on the meditation that generates the practice of the nondual armor.

Then, further, I shall explain
the code signs, which are supremely secret.
Without fully knowing the code signs,
the accomplishments of union are not attained;
when this is known with certainty,
accomplishment will be attained, without doubt.
dva is explained as a man.
di is a woman.
pu is pressing down.
su is eating itself.
ma is explained as a mother.
ya is a wife.
bha is a sister.
bhi is explained as a female friend.
lu is a daughter.
sri is explained as blood.
so is the drink of peace.
pe is broth,
and pi likewise is meat.
Another syllable is eating.
bhu is mixed.
pi is a charnel ground.
du is a corpse.
ṭī is a yoginī.
gā is Lāmā.
tri is Rūpiṇī.
ku is the Ḍākinī.
ha is Khaṇḍarohā.
jā is the two thighs.
Another syllable is speaking honestly.
sva is welcome.
mi is a fish.
byo is what is not to be eaten.
phyo is what is not to be drunk.
ga is one who may and one who may not be approached.
kā is desire.
sa is one who goes to no other.
ra is a woman without a husband.
du is a woman of bad conduct.
su is a fortunate woman.
na is an unfortunate woman.
sa is one who has samaya.
a is one without samaya.
ī is has come.
na is has not come.
so is passion.
These are the code signs of the letters.
At the time of empowerment the yogin
should give what is to be eaten and what is not to be eaten.
Empowerment is supreme;
knowing the supreme samaya,
at the samaya time of those who have samaya,
at the gathering of the samaya circle,
one should give it to the faithful;
one should not give it to the faithless.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-eighth chapter, on the code signs.

Then, further, I shall explain
the characteristics of the yoginīs;
by merely knowing them
the practitioner gains accomplishment.
A woman white like a lotus root,
with long eyes like lotus petals,
who always delights in white clothes,
who has the scent of fresh sandalwood,
and who delights in the assembly of the sugatas,
should be known as born of that family.
A woman like refined gold,
who delights in red and yellow clothes,
who has the scent of jasmine and campaka,
is a follower of the Excellent One.
One who is wholly dark blue like an utpala,
who delights in blue scents and clothes,
who has the scent of the blue utpala,
follows the excellent hero.
A woman whose color is like a white lotus,
who always smells of the lotus root,
likewise has the mind of a heroine.
A woman red and pale yellow,
of beautiful form, with red clothes,
with the scent of mālakī and utpala,
is born of the vajra family.
A woman yellow and green,
who delights in white clothes
and has the scent of flowers on her head,
is a follower of the tathāgata family.
A woman whose color is red,
who wears clothes of that color,
and always has the scent of camphor,
is a follower of Vairocana's family.
These families of yoginīs,
seven in all, I have explained.
They always delight in left-handed conduct;
by their own colors they have six families.
The vidyā letters of the families
also become six colors.
They bind their own mudrās
and speak words born of their own vidyā.
The woman who comes from the left,
who is always on the yogin's left,
who signals with her left hand,
who glances from the left,
and who talks of goddesses and of dance,
is declared to have samaya.
The woman who listens to what is done,
who speaks much of her family,
who does not abandon the family's duties,
who does not cast aside what she herself has taught,
and who recites the vidyā of her own family,
is declared to have samaya.
She who joins her palms in homage,
who always bows with the left side of her body,
and who speaks of goddesses and of all things,
will certainly go to bliss.
With her left big toe on the ground
she draws lines with her foot,
she moves her head about,
she looks sidelong and shows her hand,
especially to the practitioner;
she calls to mind her own vidyā,
and shows her palm at her cheeks, her jaw,
or her nose;
she looks sidelong and praises once,
repeats it, and looks at the family.
A yoginī who truly goes,
if she certainly has samaya,
is then a yoginī hard to find;
and likewise from the ḍākinīs
the five nectars truly arise.
Yāminī and Saṃtrāsanī,
the Desirous One, Vārāhī, the One of Terrible Form,
and the All-Roaming One come to welcome.
The yoginīs are of seven kinds;
