their own marks are explained in brief:
Rūpiṇī and Cumbikā,
Lāmā, the Transformed One, the One with a Child,
the Unturning One, the goddess Ehikā;
the Ḍākinī is explained before them.
She who looks with acceptance,
who dances with raised eyebrows,
who first gathers in her form
and afterward becomes frightening,
is declared to be Rūpiṇī;
the nondual hero should approach her.
Whether lovely or unlovely,
when she embraces and kisses a child
she should be known as Cumbikā;
she is also the supreme ḍākinī.
She does not look at food but looks sidelong,
with a scowl and a wrinkled face,
twisting her eyebrows, with a threatening finger;
moreover, she does not breathe out.
She who is frightening
is shown to be Lāmā.
Pig, bear, cat,
the jackal kind and tigers:
she who is frightening among all these
is declared to be the Transformed One.
She who laughs with great delight,
who goes and does not return again,
and laughs with passionate attachment,
is declared to be Khaṇḍarohā.
She whose mind is always weary,
and who, with foot or hand,
with the hem of her garment or with a stick,
touches one so that one does not survive,
should be known as the Unturning One;
she is declared not to be practiced with.
The family mudrās of the ḍākinīs:
the hero approaches and looks at the signs.
Skull cup, axe, fang,
and sword are all terrifying;
vajra and conch
are declared to be the family mudrās.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the thirty-ninth chapter, on the signs of the yoginīs.

Then, further, I shall explain
the characteristics of the ḍākinīs,
by correctly knowing which
one abides in the samaya of the ḍākinīs.

The woman who is red and yellow,
who gives off the scent of the lotus,
whose gaze is peaceful by nature,
and who knowingly follows one's gaze;
the woman whose nails are red,
whose eyes are always full of longing,
and who draws a lotus in her house,
is born of the family of Padmanarteśvara.
She who has a trident between her eyebrows,
whose body is blue and pale,
who always meditates on the vajra family,
in whose house a vajra is drawn,
and who always makes offerings:
that glorious ḍākinī
is born of the family of Heruka.
She on whose forehead a trident
and short spears appear,
whose eyes are red, whose complexion is reddish yellow,
whose feet and hands are likewise red,
whose mind always delights
in goats and in chickens,
in whose house is the emblem of the vajra,
and who always makes offerings to it,
is born of the family of Śrī Heruka;
she is a ḍākinī, there is no doubt.
She on whose forehead
or on whose hand a wheel appears,
who, like the blue clouds,
always wears it on her forehead,
who is of great fortune, gathering all,
and supremely truthful,
who draws a wheel in her house
and always makes offerings to it,
is a ḍākinī born of the family of Vajravārāhī,
proud in her strength.
She who is like the color of collyrium,
whose teeth are white and prominent,
who is always fierce,
who has strength of mind,
who always delights in bathing,
who does not talk much,
who draws a vajra in her house
and always makes offerings with virtue,
is born of the family of Vajravārāhī;
she is endowed with ten thousand.
She who is yellow, appearing like the color of gold,
whose eyes are unmoving, whose facial down is yellow,
on whose forehead
or on whose hand a vajra appears,
who always abides in royal power,
who is proud and speaks the truth,
from whom the scent of mālikā comes forth,
in whose house a vajra
is always drawn and greatly worshiped,
is born of the family of Khaṇḍarohā,
the supreme sovereign of yoginīs.
She who delights in horse meat,
who is thin and of the black color of collyrium,
who has something like a spear point on her forehead,
who delights in fierce deeds,
who always goes to charnel grounds,
who is fearless and without pity,
who has a tilaka on her forehead
and draws and worships a skull cup in her house,
is born of the ḍākinī family
of glorious Heruka.
The woman who is like the color of a cloud,
whose teeth are set unevenly,
who always delights in fierce deeds,
who bares her left fang,
and who in her house draws an axe
and always worships it,
is without doubt a ḍākinī
born of the family of the Guide.
These hosts of ḍākinīs
are born of the family of Śrī Heruka.
For the benefit of the practitioner
their characteristics have been correctly explained.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the fortieth chapter, on the characteristics of the ḍākinīs.

Then, further, I shall explain.
The practitioner should know
the particular characteristics
and the various aspects of the Lāmās.
She whose round face
appears perfectly circular,
who always has a moustache on her lip,
whose body hair and eyebrows are long,
whose clothes are fine and clean, who is peaceful,
unagitated, and speaks the truth,
and who always delights in the holy Dharma,
should be known as the hero's sister.
One should give the lotus mudrā,
or else make the tortoise mudrā.
Hide, vase, and ewer
should be known as the reply to the mudrā.
On the tenth days of the month
she draws a lotus in her house.
These are the characteristics
of the Lāmā woman.
Her belly protrudes, her eyes are long,
her eyes are red and tawny;
she is wealthy, fortunate, and glorious;
she is yellow and looks like a campaka;
she is neither very tall nor short;
she delights in multicolored clothes;
she has three lines on her forehead;
looking upward from the corners of her eyes,
she laughs and is joyful;
she stands treading upon the road;
she always delights in tales
of those who die in battle.
When one sees such a proud woman,
one should show the spear mudrā,
and by bending the left leg
one should show oneself as well.
Turning the left ones fully back
is declared to be the reply to the mudrā.
The fourteenth and the eighth days of the month
are declared to be her time.
She draws a trident in her house
and always worships it.
These are the characteristics
of the Lāmā of Lokeśvara.
She in whose cheeks
hollows are seen,
who is likewise always red and yellow,
whose eyes appear green and yellow,
whose hair is likewise curled,
on whose head and forehead a silk band is seen tied,
on whom a single line is clearly present,
whose neck is likewise long,
who always delights in red clothes,
who laughs and sings songs,
who is sometimes angry,
whose mind wavers,
and who delights in quarrels:
when one sees such a proud woman,
one should show the spear mudrā;
as a second, with effort,
one should show the bell mudrā.
Turning back toward the left side
is declared to be the reply to the mudrā.
She who is short, with thick thighs,
who always delights in yellow clothes,
and who wears a cloth over her shoulders:
when one sees such a woman,
one should show the vajra mudrā,
and as a second, with effort,
one should show the conch mudrā.
Turning fully back from the left
is declared to be the reply to the mudrā.
The fourteenth is her time;
she draws a vajra in her house.
These are the characteristics
of the Lāmā of Śrī Heruka.
She who has body hair all over her body,
whose eyes are black and yellow,
who is stout and fearsome in her changes of mood,
whose face is broad and mouth large,
whose lower lip protrudes, whose complexion is black,
whose eyes are round and nose flattened,
who is always skilled in song,
and who is cloud-colored and captivating:
when one sees such a proud woman,
one should show the mudrā of song,
and as a second, with effort,
one should show the spear mudrā.
Turning fully back from the left
is declared to be the reply to the mudrā.
The eleventh day of the month is her time;
she draws a fang in her house.
These are the characteristics
of the Lāmā of Vajravārāhī.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-first chapter, on the characteristics of the Lāmās.

Then, further, I shall explain
the code signs of the ḍākinīs,
by which brothers and sisters
come to recognize one another correctly.

She who shows her left hand
is indicating honest speech.
She who shows her ring finger
gives the reply to honest speech.
She who strikes her belly
is indicating "I am hungry."
She who shows her forehead
means "I have come from the sky."
She who puts a finger in her mouth
is saying "I have eaten."
She who moves her tongue
is saying "I will eat."
She who touches her knee
is indicating "I am tired."
She who touches the tip of her finger
is saying "I am rested."
She who gnashes her teeth
is saying "I eat flesh."
Showing the right hand
indicates "Do it like this."
She who shows a frown
is saying "Bound."
She who shows the garuḍa
is indicating "Freed."
To her who shows a fist
one should show patati.
To her who shows her hair,
having watched her change of manner,
one should show the khaṭvāṅga.
To her who moves her limbs
one should show the fang.
She who shows her hand with her hand
is saying "Eat the torma."
She who shows her right hand
is saying "Do it like this!"
She who touches her ear
is indicating "One should stay."
She who touches nail with nail
means "Carried off by death."
She who draws on the ground
is saying "Today one enters the maṇḍala."
She who touches her breast
means "Protect my child."
She who draws on the ground with her left thumb
is declared to mean "Protect the kinsfolk!"
She who narrows her eyes
is saying "Do it so."
She who touches the joints
is saying "Sleep well."
Whatever messenger woman she may be,
all of them should correctly practice these.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-second chapter, on the code signs.

Then I shall explain the limb mudrās
by their characteristics.
To the woman who touches the crown of her head
one should show the head.
To her who shows her forehead
one should show the cheek.
To her who shows her teeth
one should show the tongue.
To her who shows her lips
one should show the shoulder.
To her who shows her waist and back
one should show the ground.
To her who shows her breasts
one should show the jaw.
To her who shows her belly
one should show the navel.
To her who shows her secret place
one should show the liṅga.
To her who shows her thigh
one should show what is below.
To her who shows her knee
one should show the calf.
To her who shows her foot
one should show the sole.
To her who shows the joints of her fingers
one should show the nails.
To her who shows the ground
one should show the sky.
To her who shows the sky
one should show the sun.
To her who shows a river
one should show the ocean.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-third chapter, on the characteristics of the limb mudrās.

Then, further, I shall explain well
the supreme mudrās of the limbs as they are,
by knowing which, brother
or sister is known without doubt.
By showing one finger
one says "Welcome."
By showing two fingers
one gives the reply to the welcome.
This is well explained
as the limb mudrā of all yoginīs.
Bending the two index fingers is the eye gesture;
to whomever the mantrin shows it,
she at once, for as long as she lives,
comes under his power, without doubt.
To her who shows her middle finger
one should show the index finger.
To her who shows her ring finger
one should show the tongue.
To her who shows patisa
one should show the front.
To her who shows the crown of her head
one should show the edge of the boundary.
To her who shows the ground
one should show the sky.
To her who shows a frown
one should show the space between the brows.
To her who shows her teeth
one should show the lips.
To her who shows her throat
one should show the mouth.
To her who shows her forehead
one should show the navel.
These, in order, are the limb mudrās
of all the yoginīs.
Those keepers of the secret who always show them
will come to the correct view.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-fourth chapter, on the limb mudrās of the ḍākinīs.

Then, further, I shall explain
the characteristics of the ḍākinīs,
by which, from afar, heroes
and heroines are recognized as sisters.
She who has samaya and is devoted
is the ground of yogic enjoyment.
Having made the ground the basis,
she honors the vow of the yogin.
She looks in reverse order,
her face changes,
and the eyebrows of her face always move;
she is to be quickly recognized in various ways.
Likewise the change of manner born of the ḍākinīs
should be known as wandering;
three lines, fully rounded:
these should be known as the signs
of those who truly go as ḍākinīs.
If she turns away,
the meaning explained before is given up.
Her substance, which perishes,
is clear like a vajra.
Her voice, like the cuckoo's call,
is always clear and pleasant.
In the house of the signs
there are mirrors like vajras,
there are mirrors like swords,
and hand emblems like spears always appear;
these are called the clear mirror.
The forms made as signs
that have these characteristics
should be known as
the supreme among the ḍākinīs.

What is called potaṃgi is honest speech. What is called pratipataṃgi is the reply to honest speech. What is called gamu means "go." What is called luṃkaṃ means "come." What is called dehi means "food." What is called grihana means "brahmin." What is called piraṇaṃ is the heart. What is called marṇa is an ill-omened sound. In what is called gāndha, kar is ka. śiro is a lie. kara is the belly. bara is sound. śraramaṇo is the ear. amritasvā is churned. samagama is a man. talika is a ḍākinī. naraka is the maṇḍala. amoka is the charnel ground. kalika is the door. śvasa is the brahmin caste. paridhi is the kṣatriya caste. birati is the vaiśya caste. krur is the śūdra caste.

anatasthā is one with a mole. paśulika is a house. ṭāka is a sister. melapaka is water. griharaṇa is releasing the vajra water. Touching the tongue to the teeth indicates hunger. triśna is asking. kuta means "I have come from such and such a place." kirana is a flower. tumula is laughter. tanta is averting the fall of rain. tṛpata is a request. dhuryo is knowing. nriha is a cloud. dhupa is incense. sanu is a mountain. sarita is a river. aguli is a limb. badana is the face. jātika is the tongue. antana is the teeth. pakṣi is a banner. chando is a garland. cala is the wind. paśu is a wild animal. yanti is the maṇḍala. sanama is a crossroads. phalkuśaṃ is people. mahākṣaraba is great livestock. ga is a tree. na is a man. go is a bull. ma is a buffalo. bha is food. ha is a synonym. adhya is a king. Touching the mouth means "I have eaten." Touching the tongue means "I am satisfied." nasti is the essence. hri is shame. Fire means "destroy it with the hurled substances." Touching the thigh is empty. Above is one. Below is none. These are the mudrās and the replies to the mudrās, the secret code of the speech of the heroes.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-fifth chapter, on the rite of the code signs of the ḍākinīs.

Then, in the yoga tantra,
I shall explain the samaya that is hard to find.
A man or a woman who goes from the left
grants the accomplishments one desires.
Though dwelling in a house or in a field,
even very far away, they will be known.
There is no union other than Heruka.
The ācārya, the yoginī herself,
is, for the practitioner in this tantra,
truly the excellent one;
she should not be despised or abandoned,
but should be honored according to one's ability.
These eight messenger women
increase devotion and faith;
they are always the cause of the accomplishments
and also set the samayas in motion.
Uniting with all the other goddesses,
being bewildered by desire,
nondual and unobstructed,
practicing the samaya as it is to be practiced,
the sovereign of women, wholly united,
the conduct of Brahmā, likewise the ground,
and the continuous conduct without anger:
these eight are the conduct of samaya.
The practitioner who abides in practice
should always know them.
They are common to all the tantras;
without cause they should not be broken.
They are to be taken up by faith alone,
not with the eye and the hand.
This secret should not be revealed;
it should be hidden with great effort.
When the wise one moves his little finger,
the messenger women truly abide.
Pure, impure, and mixed:
the practitioner abides in three ways.
The one who pleases is wholly pure,
and the one who illuminates is a person endowed with qualities.
In land after land the ascetic
should examine the signs of the messengers' forms.
The messenger is like the edge of a sword;
she purifies and increases merit.
Likewise, by being well joined with the messenger
the practitioner gains accomplishment;
even without recitation, offering, and meditation
it will be accomplished very quickly.
With mantra and mudrā the practitioner
fully enters into worship itself.
There is no worship other than this:
thus the treatise is certain.
Delighting in the path of the messenger,
the practitioner who strives for accomplishment,
fully endowed with vidyā-mantra and mudrā,
should always partake of food.
The practitioner, being excellent,
should partake of all
the families placed in equipoise,
and should consider nothing not to be eaten.
Here there is no distinction among them.
The practice of the messenger woman:
when one engages in the rite of accomplishment,
there is the messenger alone.
The accomplishment on the side of awakening,
close to the practices
and obtained through the succession of the gurus' lineage,
by empowerment, well endowed with mantra,
is correctly explained to be among the messengers.
With the mudrā, good union and proper manner,
unattached giving and moral discipline,
born from the cause of wisdom, perfectly complete,
the very self of mind rises at dawn.
Having done all the tasks of the body,
one should always practice keeping
the weapon fully on the body,
abiding on the body.
With a body that has been purified
one should always recite the mantra.
One should clearly show the places of yoga,
and should not step over the weapons.
One should not be angry with those who have samaya,
and should not transgress against those who strive in earnest.
One should be happy together with them.
For one's own benefit and for the benefit of others
one should engage in the conduct of samaya.
Here one should enjoy together.
At night one should always be clothed in sky,
and should always become black and red.
One should wear what comes from the limbs of living beings.
One who desires bliss will attain bliss.
For the sake of worship, obstruction is wholly abandoned, and one is pure.
Blessed by Śrī Heruka,
one is fully endowed with the five mudrās,
fully endowed with them at all times.
One should not consider family or not family.
One should give up binding the crown hair and the like.
The messenger is the first purity;
the second is held to be union;
eating food from a single dish
is declared to be the third purity.
By thoroughly training in these purities,
if one attains the stage of purification by the pure,
from what would accomplishment not arise?
What is taught in the Compendium of Reality,
likewise what is taught in the Cakrasaṃvara,
what is correctly explained in the Secret Tantra,
and likewise what is taught in the Glorious Supreme Primal
and in the Vajrabhairava,
is accomplished through recitation, vows, and the like;
here, by meditation alone,
the mantrin accomplishes it in an instant.
Those who delight in the path of yoga
should also correctly practice this purity.
The merit that comes from purity,
the vast qualities of practice,
cannot be told by a single mouth,
unless one had a thousand faces.
Glorious Herukī, of great wisdom,
who accomplishes all the pure characteristics,
who drinks the water of the merit of bliss,
is the purifier, the destroyer of evil.
Even by touching and by speaking
one is freed from all evil.
The supreme person who practices
without faults of body and speech
is freed from all evil,
and will attain the grounds
of the sugatas, whose very nature is purity.
Birth after birth he is a tathāgata;
he becomes a righteous king of noble family.
Having thus always made worship,
one should give the torma with faith.
Then, through pure worship,
the hero born from nirvāṇa,
and likewise worship of oneself,
all the buddhas assembled together with their heirs,
all the heroes and the yoginīs,
the stable and the moving,

keeping the time of the worship of the brothers,
abiding at the time of the moon,
together with the brothers and the children,
from whom all happiness and suffering arise:
mantra, likewise mudrā,
and wisdom should not be shown there.
Partaking of the purifying food
always becomes the practice of samaya.
The secret mantra should not be given to anyone;
the instructions should not be allowed to decline.
To those who abide in samaya, as is fitting,
the instructions too should be given.
Apart from the messengers, one does not go
to others, deluded by desire.
Always free of attachment to the messenger,
one should enjoy her without transgressing.
Those who do not abide in a lowly birth
are not to be served and honored.
Mother, sister, daughter,
and wife abide as messenger women.
In the circle, or likewise in accomplishment,
hardship will certainly be exhausted.
The highest and the lowest cease together;
thus the substances are ascertained.
The yogin always acts from the left:
he puts the left foot forward first,
he turns the left hand around,
he worships fully with the left half,
he presses down with the left of the body,
with the left he satisfies and eats.
The vow is equal, the vow complete;
the practitioner strives in samaya.
He practices with the family of samaya
and partakes of food together with them.
For those who practice everything within the five families,
the families are proclaimed to be of four kinds.
For those who practice everything within one family,
one worships fully in the practice of yoga.
With beef and horse meat,
dog meat and various kinds,
having made a triangular maṇḍala,
even the buddhas will surely enter.
Likewise the food of the heroes:
the mantrin, fully endowed with mudrā,
should examine all the treatises
on the enjoyments of the emanation of Śrī Heruka.
He should always hold and guard them,
so that in all actions
they are accomplished just as they are, without obstructors;
here others will not come to be.
This is the excellent supreme messenger;
she is diligent and joined together with him.
Unattached, joyful enjoyment is complete:
this is the very division of the yogic substances.
Delighting in the guru's instructions,
its meaning is equal to the supreme.
A brother, or else a father,
is seen in an instant as the lord.
With thick tongue and reddish yellow eyes,
rough, with reddish yellow hair,
in common for all accomplishments,
always delighting in recitation and vows,
fully endowed with mindfulness,
together with the messengers and the rest and their abodes,
the twenty-four supreme ḍākinīs
fully grant the fruit of enjoyment and liberation.
One should always recite the root mantra
that accomplishes all desired aims.
The three worlds, moving and unmoving,
which fully grant the fruit of yoga,
arise from this secret mantra,
fully endowed with mantra and mudrā.
A secret mantra devoid of mudrā
does not bless embodied beings.
As long as there are the deeds of beings,
they should be realized through perfect union.
Whoever hates the yogin
who accomplishes all desired aims
will be born in a hundred births as a dog
and will be born blemished with moles.
For example, someone who wants butter,
though he churns water with effort,
will get no butter or cream;
his body is only wearied.
Holding and worshiping,
relying on another yoga
that has become a cause of a means of livelihood,
is likewise pointless elsewhere.
The hero, great and supreme in yoga,
in whatever region he dwells,
even if it is a low-caste barbarian land,
that land has been fully examined.
As a cause for caring for beings,
he himself still always abides there.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-sixth chapter, on the enumeration of the messengers.

Then, further, I shall explain
the supreme essence of the ḍākinīs,
the distinctive mantra of the heroes,
by merely recalling which
the three worlds are shaken,
and by merely remembering which
true accomplishment is granted step by step.
oṃ sarba buddha ḍākinīye bajra varṇṇanīye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
This secret mantra is the great secret mantra,
supreme in performing all actions.
By reciting the great mantra one accomplishes;
all desires are accomplished.
It is that of the Ḍākinī and the yoginī,
of Khaṇḍarohā and of Lāmā as well.
oṃ hrīḥ h ha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
This secret mantra is the great secret mantra,
supreme in performing all actions.
By merely reciting it one accomplishes;
it is chief among all secret mantras.
The fragment of skull and the rest
are the supreme essence of all heroes.
Those secret mantras, supreme and holy,
accomplish all desired aims.
Higher even than the highest,
it is the supreme vow of the heroes.
This is the secret mantra of all;
this is renowned as the mantra.
Beneath the earth and in the heavens,
and likewise in the realm between,
by whatever actions, just as they are,
the three worlds are accomplished.
Having accomplished everything by it,
through the unsurpassed supreme secret mantra
the three worlds are accomplished.
Through those unsurpassed ones
the practitioner accomplishes everything.
The ḍākinīs and all the heroes
are given in brief, not at length.
Having drawn a beautiful drawing,
adorned with various flowers and fruits,
one should meditate on the maṇḍala upon it:
the Ḍākinī and Lāmā,
the yoginī and Khaṇḍarohā,
the heroes and the heroines,
laughing ha ha he he,
the twenty-four families of heroes,
the vow of the net of ḍākinīs.
The heroes explained before are supreme among the valiant;
the supreme of heroes is all of them.
The twenty-four heroes
pervade countless beings.
The heroes and the ḍākinīs,
the yoginī and Lāmā,
and Khaṇḍarohā likewise,
one should view in the place of the wheel.
They accomplish all actions;
one should always worship those who dwell in the wheel.
Well settled in equipoise, one accomplishes one's desires.
Reciting according to the rite explained before,
by contemplating the vow of the net of ḍākinīs,
all the accomplishments
of the great wheel are accomplished as well.
It is taught by ten million buddhas:
for each single ḍākinī
the number of her retinue is ten million.
The yoginī and Lāmā
and Khaṇḍarohā in particular
are virtuous, bringing about all accomplishments.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-seventh chapter, on the essence of the nonduality of yoginī and hero, the practice of the sequence of actions of the ḍākinīs and heroes.

Then, further, I shall explain
the characteristics of images and paintings.
From great conch alone
the wise should fashion the form,
complete with all its characteristics,
with excellent limbs and minor limbs.
With a mind of devotion going before,
the yogin should take it up and accomplish it.
One should worship it with excrement and urine,
together with rakta and śukra.
The incense of nara, the great glorious substance,
is the practice of the hero's action;
the supreme state is accomplished.
Then, having worshiped oneself,
one will obtain all one's desires
and will win a king's woman.
Following whatever the rite may be,
one should perform the offering of the hands.
Having drawn the painting on a great hide,
if the practitioner rests in meditative equipoise,
it will be accomplished in seven days;
without doubt, one should practice.
Worship the yoginī and Lāmā,
and likewise one is born in the abode of the ḍākinīs.
Having found bliss through that,
one will always perform the rites.
By always worshiping Lāmā,
her accomplishment is swiftly attained.
To accomplish a ḍākinī,
draw the image on an elephant's hide.
To accomplish a yoginī,
draw the painting on a cow's hide.
To accomplish the sky-goers (khecarīs),
draw the painting on a horse's hide.
To accomplish the earth-goers,
draw the painting on a buffalo's hide.
To accomplish those who move beneath the earth,
draw the painting on a pig's hide.
To accomplish a yakṣiṇī,
draw the image on a wooden board.
To accomplish a daughter of the gods,
draw the image on a board of campaka.
To accomplish nāginīs,
draw the painting on a board of nāga tree.
One who wishes to accomplish a human
should draw the image on charnel ground cloth.
For the accomplishment of seeing beyond,
draw the image on a donkey's hide.
One who wishes to gain riches
should draw the image on a rat's hide.
One who wishes to summon liquor
should draw the image on a monkey's hide.
One who wishes to summon blood
should draw the image on a skull cup.
One who wishes to summon seed
should draw the image on a dove's skin.
One who wishes to summon a god
should draw the image on a board of deodar.
One who wishes to summon a city
should draw the image on a jackal's hide.
For the rite of pacifying, draw it on the cloth
of a brahmin woman at the time of her menses;
by this, pacification comes about.
For increasing, draw it on the cloth
of a kṣatriya woman at the time of her menses.
For subjugating, draw it on the cloth
of a vaiśya woman at the time of her menses.
For the fierce rite, draw it on the cloth
of a śūdra woman at the time of her menses.
For those to be made to faint,
draw the painting on the cloth of a banner.
For paralyzing, a slab of stone;
for driving apart, a camel's hide;
for summoning, a lion's.
For all rites, a bird's skin,
or else iron alone.
Otherwise, the various rites
are certainly accomplished by mindfulness alone.
In order to do away with obstructors,
draw it on the skins of hares.
For raising up obstructors,
draw it on the skins of the dead.
On gold, silver, and the like,
on copper, crystal, and deodar,
and from vajra, the rites are to be done;
the supreme accomplishment is the special result.
Sandalwood and deodar,
red sandalwood and campaka:
aśoka brings accomplishment;
śrīvṛkṣa becomes a giver of wealth.
Aguru and walnut wood,
the various kinds of milky trees,
birch wood and the rest
become the accomplishment of the accomplished.
What is made from frog bone,
the various kinds of nāga tree,
tortoise bone, and elephant tusk
especially bring down rain as well.
Brahmin bone is for pacifying;
elephant bone is for increasing;
for subjugating, horse bone;
for killing, buffalo bone;
for driving apart, camel bone;
for enmity, horse and buffalo;
for paralyzing, dog bone;
for summoning, lion bone.
For going beneath the earth,
use the bones of a pig.
What is made from white earth
will always bring about pacification.
For increasing, yellow earth;
for subjugating, it is red earth;
and for killing, black earth.
This is the rite in brief.
Excrement, urine, fat, and blood,
and likewise the great eye salve,
together with ground human bone,
are mixed into the colors.
The self-arisen flower and the seed,
of a low-caste woman and a brahmin woman,
a caṇḍāla woman and a servant woman,
a washerwoman and a dancing woman,
a mother, or else a sister,
or the various kinds of daughter:
having taken these in their presence,
the special śukra and rakta,
having mixed these various things,
one should draw the image according to the rite.
On the earth of an anthill,
with the hair and bone of the dead,
one should draw the excellent one with a brush.
The measuring line is a dead person's thread;
this is the supreme measuring line for images.
Having propitiated with garlands of the departed and so forth,
one should also make lamps of fat.
The incense is the great hero,
and the food offering is made with marrow.
The scent is excrement and vajra water.
The vase is made of skulls,
the vessel for the guest water is a skull,
and the guest water is powdered bone.
With effort the person makes offering,
practicing according to his ability.
Through the application of various mudrās,
endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
whether image or painting,
or otherwise, the wise should perform the rite.
In accordance with whatever the rite may be,
one wears the corresponding clothing,
or naked, with hair let loose,
and having changed one's form and color;
the principal and the yogin,
and the master as well, likewise,
abiding in the proper way of the mudrā,
with excrement, urine, and the excellent hero,
should rest in meditative equipoise and recite.
Songs and dances of many different kinds,
in various forms,
and the finest food and drink should be enjoyed.
These should be done until completion;
this is the sequence of the rite.

At the times of consecration,
it is enhanced with a twofold feast gathering.
Bracelets and wristlets,
armlets and the various kinds of cloth,
finger rings and crest ornaments,
anklets and skull cups,
parasols and yak-tail fans likewise:
various persons give and present them.
The various supreme and excellent heroes,
the various kinds of intoxicating drink,
the supreme rites of samaya,
the various flavors and essences,
the various supreme flowers,
the bodies of supreme goddesses,
the sounds of various music,
the master joined with a mudrā,
and the special assemblies of disciples:
with these and with offerings of food one should worship.
With supreme fragrances of incense,
the various incenses of the victors,
and especially garlands of lamps,
parasols and canopies likewise,
mirrors and yak-tail fans,
nets and half nets,
it is adorned with tassels.
The vases, provided with cloth,
are completely filled with the nectar of wisdom,
with the five jewels and medicines
and the various kinds of fragrance.
The vessel for the guest water especially
should be filled with the five sprouts of cūta,
āmra, jambu, kadamba,
bilva and bījapūra.
Bala, moṭa, kṛtañjaḥ,
together with kedri and karikar,
ekavīra and sahade:
among all the herbs that are known,
these five should be known,
and the wise should place them in the vase.
Gold and nutmeg,
camphor and zhosha likewise.
With golden crest ornaments
and adorned with the six mudrās,
all the practitioners, naked,
sing auspicious songs,
and with a completely pure mind,
an attitude of faith going before,
at all times and on all occasions
they should make various vast offerings.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-eighth chapter, on the rite of consecrating painted cloths and the like.

Then, further, I shall explain
the maṇḍala, the excellent maṇḍala,
called the Exalted Heruka;
it is the condensed essence of the maṇḍala.
The practitioner, perfectly in meditative equipoise,
should always guard the samaya.
One whose samaya is broken
will not obtain empowerment and accomplishment in the maṇḍala.
Honey and vermilion, with camphor,
red and sandalwood combined:
those who have entered the feast gathering
all abide in taking the essence.
All hold the sign of the vajra.
By joining the tips of the stout one, the end and the nape,
one should always practice through the stages of yoga.
If one tastes it as a peaceful drink,
one will always obtain accomplishment.
This becomes the five nectars;
it is the entry to all accomplishments.
The four offerings, and likewise the hero,
of the two empowerments in union,
hold the very suchness of union.
For the approach to be done first,
recite a hundred thousand times according to the rite.
Recite the secret mantra of the wheel a hundred thousand times,
and that of one's own deity also a hundred thousand times;
the others should be recited ten thousand times.
The wheel should be drawn as instructed,
and by radiant, supreme persons
the maṇḍala should be drawn and laid out.
Whatever place was explained before,
there the maṇḍala should be drawn.
The ground should be thoroughly prepared:
having pounded it and pounded it well,
it should be level, with splinters removed.
With dung that has not fallen on the ground
one should smear the ground of the maṇḍala.
With charnel ground ash
together with the five nectars
the ground of the maṇḍala is smeared,
and there one rightly begins the maṇḍala.
Engaging in all conduct in the charnel ground,
with the powder of charcoal from burned corpses
and with the substances of the eight charnel grounds,
the master, well settled in equipoise,
should draw the excellent maṇḍala.
The master is endowed with all the characteristics,
has abandoned all bad signs,
knows correctly and knows the tantra,
and knows the secret mantra of Glorious Heruka;
he is without anger, pure and learned,
knows the union, and has gone beyond in wisdom.
His crown is adorned with skulls,
his limbs are smeared with ash,
his body is adorned with curved ornaments,
and bone garlands rest upon his bones.
A single piece adorns his crown,
he is adorned with a garland of bones,
and the khaṭvāṅga rests in his hand.
Having made oneself into Śrī Heruka,
then beneath that
one should recollect Glorious Heruka
and set him in the heart of this wheel.
Having thus armored oneself,
one should set fences in the directions,
and below one should also set weapons.
Having laid out oneself as the wheel,
above one makes a net of arrows,
and around oneself a net of weapons.
One thus armored
cannot be overcome even by the thirty-three gods.
Having thus protected oneself well
and adorned oneself with mantra and mudrā,
one should draw the terrifying maṇḍala
that bestows all accomplishments.
Then, with a thread from the dead
moistened with great blood,
one should strike the lines of the terrifying maṇḍala
of the supreme city of Glorious Heruka.
It is square, eight cubits across,
with four doors and four corners all around.
The mantra practitioner applies it twofold
and recites the Cakrasaṃvara of the net of ḍākinīs.
What is placed in its center
is a variegated lotus with eight petals,
with petals and a blazing center,
perfectly endowed with anthers.
In the center one should set the hero
in the form of the great Bhairava,
radiant and intensely blazing,
laughing loudly, "Ha ha!",
adorned with ornaments of a garland of skulls,
excellent, with three eyes and four faces,
wearing an elephant hide,
with fine brows and a spreading vajra frown,
holding a khaṭvāṅga in his hand,
and adorned with a garland of fifty heads.
The goddess who stands before him,
called Vajravārāhī,
is exceedingly terrifying, facing the great Bhairava;
she should be given a fierce form with three eyes.
Her skull cup is filled with entrails,
blood drips from her mouth,
and toward gods and asuras, together with humans,
in all directions she makes the threatening gesture.
The twenty-four ḍākinīs
arisen from the family of Vārāhī
should be worshiped in the directions and intermediate directions
and in the hub of the wheel.
Likewise here the heroes too,
abiding properly in the wheel, are worshiped.
If the practitioner desires accomplishment,
he should worship the nondual hero.
Then, as for making the vases:
they should be without a black base and other flaws,
of pearl, gold and jewels,
coral, silver and copper.
A vessel well filled with all kinds of food
should be placed on top.
The neck should be wound with thread,
and the mouth adorned with leaves.
Well wrapped with a pair of cloths,
eight should be set out at the doors.
The ninth vase, wrapped
with a pair of cloths, is placed in the center.
Of gold, or silver, or jewels, or

of pearls, it should be made beautiful.
All around the maṇḍala, with jewels
and with gold, it is beautifully arrayed.
Having worshiped the delightful supreme city,
there is no doubt it will be accomplished.
One's own face and whole body
should be well cleansed with scented water.
If one desires the supreme accomplishment,
one should offer a hundred lamps.
All the ḍākinīs of the sky
should be set above by the mind;
whatever ḍākinīs there are on the earth
should be set throughout the maṇḍala;
and whatever there are, set them below the earth.
All the mātṛkās should be set
in the directions and the intermediate directions.
If one desires the supreme accomplishment,
one should offer scent, flowers, incense
and also a hundred lamps,
and in the maṇḍala of Glorious Heruka
worship the heroes and yoginīs.
With banners red and likewise yellow,
white, black and tawny,
and likewise various cloths,
many garlands of flowers,
canopies, and very beautiful arrows
hung with silk,
and likewise with foods, snacks and drinks,
one should worship in perfect meditative equipoise.
Then, for the sake of accomplishment, the practitioner
should first please the master.
One who desires accomplishment, perfectly in equipoise,
should worship the guru as best he can.
Adorned with the sound of bells, with flowers and incense,
he rings a bell of very sweet sound;
the practitioner beats a drum
and should call out, "Ha ha!"
Having thus worshiped the maṇḍala according to the rite,
with everything desirable,
one covers the face of one's beloved son
well with silk,
fills his joined palms with flowers,
and has him scatter lotuses and circumambulate.
Having circled to the left, he prostrates,
and also utters the sounds of hūṃ and phaṭ.
Standing in the posture with the right leg extended,
one should place the son of accomplishment.
Having scattered the flowers from his palms,
with the thought of where they will fall,
and having also made the circumambulations,
in perfect meditative equipoise,
the practitioner makes him enter.
Then, carrying the offering into the city,
keeping to the right side,
he casts the handful of flowers onto the maṇḍala.
Wherever in the maṇḍala the flower falls,
that is to be shown as his family.
Then the master, well
and perfectly in equipoise, worships the mudrā.
Then, for the disciple, secondly,
with great vermilion recited over three times,
a tilaka is made for him.
Then one uncovers the disciple's face
and should show him the maṇḍala.
Whatever the place of his deity may be,
there one should show it to him correctly.
Together with the master,
having bowed down deeply,
in a sequence that begins from the left,
he should then circumambulate.
To the maṇḍala and to the guru
he should prostrate according to the rite.
Then he should offer the gurus the fee
that the Tathāgata has taught:
offering a hundred thousand in gold,
various kinds of jewels,
a hundred pairs of cloth,
an elephant, a horse, or a kingdom,
earrings and bracelets,
necklaces and excellent rings,
crest ornaments made of gold,
a good wife and a daughter,
male and female servants and sisters:
having prostrated, he should offer them.
"From this day on, as a servant
I offer myself to you":
thus the intelligent one, with complete faith,
should offer himself as well.
What must thus be made certain
the practitioner makes thoroughly certain.
Then the ḍākinīs
and yoginīs become pleased with him.
The Ḍākinī and Lāmā,
Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī:
the practitioner is together with the ḍākinīs,
and all the beings who move with them;
there is no doubt that the practitioner
will do whatever is to be done for them.
When this yoga is accomplished,
he is unhindered in the three worlds:
invisibility, the cavern, abiding in and moving through the sky,
fleetness of foot, and likewise taking the essence;
by merely wishing, the practitioner
will always and continually produce them.
He makes many forms
and will travel through the sky.
Especially among the ḍākas
he overpowers all beings as well;
by the application of a single glance
in an instant he produces union itself.
The mantra practitioner who, without seeing the maṇḍala,
desires to be a yogin
is like one who strikes at the sky with his fist
or drinks the water of a mirage.
This yoga is supreme and most excellent,
the highest of all applications.
Whoever constantly desires it
will go on outshining gods and humans.
Whether in the Compendium of Reality,
or the Secret, or the Vajrabhairava,
this king of maṇḍalas
has not appeared before and will not appear.
Whatever has been explained or not explained,
all of it abides in Glorious Heruka.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the forty-ninth chapter, on the rite of the maṇḍala.

Then, further, I shall explain
what is endowed with the junctures of union.
What is without water is the juncture:
unstained, with attachment abandoned.
The juncture belongs to all beings;
it is all sentient beings.
The juncture is purified existence;
it is Heruka himself, guardian of the vowels.
The yogin should meditate and recite.
He appears, arising all at once,
holding the color of a conch and of jasmine,
complete with all the ornaments,
marked with all the marks,
with three faces and six arms,
three eyes, and the mood of compassion,
his hair loose, a skull on his forehead,
and on the crown of his head the moon:
endowed with the glory of Heruka.
The junctures are the most excellent of accomplishments;
this is to be done in four sessions.

The four joys are joy and the rest:
at the navel, at the heart lotus in the middle of the breasts,
at the root of the tongue and on the top of the head,
are joy, supreme joy itself,
excellent joy and the coemergent.
The first has sixty-four petals;
the second, with eight, is supreme;
the third has sixteen petals;
the fourth has thirty-two petals.
Abiding at the root of Vārāhī's navel
is the coemergent joy, the supreme Heruka.
At the juncture of the time of union
one should meditate on the four noble truths.
Suffering is the wheel of emanation;
the origin is the wheel of Dharma;
cessation is the wheel of enjoyment;
and the path is great bliss.
Having thus blessed oneself at the junctures,
one makes the yoga certain:
white and blue and green,
with three eyes, in the mood of compassion and emptiness,
through the three gates of liberation and the three bodies
one applies them in sequence for complete purity.
Holding vajra and bell together,
holding a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
holding a head and a curved knife in his hands,
he sits in the cross-legged posture of the sattva,
on a moon and a preta in the center of a lotus,
his hair loose, clothed in the directions,
intoxicated with the mood of delight.
If one always meditates on the junctures at their times,
the supreme vow is accomplished.
Excrement and vajra water,
Ratnasambhava and Amitābha,
and Amoghasiddhi likewise:
always mixing these together,
the yogin always bows to the junctures.
These secret mantras are always as well;
Heruka is the supreme Gāyatrī:
oṃ śrī herukāya vidmahe vidyārājaya vidmahe ta vīrā prajodaya āḥ hūṃ
If one always meditates on the junctures,
one should recite the supreme Gāyatrī.
The name Gāyatrī means the Veda;
performing all rites is virtuous.
One will not die an untimely death,
and pacifying, increasing and subjugating are accomplished as well.
The mantra practitioner always speaks of matters
past, future and present.
This accomplishment is thoroughly achieved;
immediately, along with the great accomplishment,
the three worlds are accomplished.
When the lord of practitioners, having meditated
and knowing the rite of Heruka,
abides in the vowed observance for three days,
he goes to the abode of the gods.
Having obtained the wisdom of the gods,
he also takes on the supreme divine body.
He gains mastery over all speech,
and having become omniscient,
he has completely abandoned aging and death.
Attended by buddhas and gods,
he sports as a buddha within existence.
For as long as three days and nights,
he should practice the coemergent with a mudrā.
Having seen her, he should rightly accomplish the sister.
Having worshiped for three days,
Vajraheruka, the glorious deity,
and all the rest will come.
Having planted his knees on the ground,
and having bowed to him,
the practitioner should always worship.
Pleased with what the practitioner desires,
at once the hero knows all.
He holds every chief,
roams throughout the three realms,
and the practitioner becomes supreme.
This is the greatly glorious hero,
who has completely abandoned all duality.
By merely being conscious of him,
the earth encircled by its girdle,
and gods, asuras and humans too,
at once come under the power
of that very practitioner.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the fiftieth chapter, on the junctures of the Gāyatrī.

Then, further, I shall explain
the meditation on the seven syllables.
From the syllable hūṃ there rightly arises
Vajraḍāka, of great bliss,
with six arms, three faces and three eyes,
marked with all the marks
and endowed with the eighty excellent minor marks:
the chief arisen from the vowels and consonants,
united in equal union with Vārāhī,
empty and pervaded by compassion,
united with vajra and bell together.
He wears a human hide as his garment,
holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
and in his right hand a trident;
he bestows all desired fruits.
He has a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
wears variegated vajra matted locks,
and bears a half moon on the crown of his head.
His body is adorned with the six mudrās.
Bhairava together with his consort
he presses down with his foot in the posture with the right leg extended.
He sits on a sun upon a variegated lotus,
seated in the center of six wheels,
united with six yoginīs;
he swiftly bestows accomplishment.
The first is the goddess Herukī,
the second is Vajrabhairavī,
the third is the Fierce Wrathful One,
the fourth is Vajrasūryā,
the fifth is Vajraraudrī,
the sixth is Vajraḍākinī.
They should be set out starting from the left.
These goddesses are exceedingly fierce,
with three eyes and loosened hair;
they wear the supreme garment of the directions.
All are seated on a preta and a sun.
Blue and yellow and green,
red and smoke-colored, and likewise white,
they hold a small drum and a bell.
All wear human hides
and have head ornaments of garlands of skulls.
They sit in the manner of the right leg extended and the left drawn in.
They arise rightly from six syllables.
The goddesses' gaze is the sign.
Just as the protector's color is,
so too is Vārāhī's;
the mudrās and hand emblems are likewise.
Blue and yellow and green,
they wear tiger hides as garments,
embracing evenly the goddess's thighs,
intent on supreme joy.
oṃ hrīḥ h ha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
From those syllables one should meditate
on the ḍākinī with six limbs.
The seventh, set in the center,
is Heruka arisen from the syllable hūṃ.
In the center, the factor of awakening that is mindfulness is the Blessed One, Glorious Heruka. The factor of awakening that is discrimination of phenomena is Vajraherukī. The factor of awakening that is diligence is Vajrabhairavī. The factor of awakening that is joy is the Fierce Wrathful One. The factor of awakening that is pliancy is Vajrasūryā. The factor of awakening that is meditative absorption is Vajraraudrī. The factor of awakening that is equanimity is Vajraḍākinī.
One should meditate on these seven factors of awakening,
applying and meditating on them in their sequence.
Reciting the immeasurable secret mantras,
the supreme abode of all the wrathful ones,
even a practitioner of little merit
gains accomplishment within half a month;
always meditating one day on each,
in seven days, without doubt.
On the full moon of the last month of spring,
with mother, sister, daughter or niece,
by the rite of wisdom and skillful means,
having recited and meditated day and night,
at dawn one becomes equal to the Glorious Heruka.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the high, this is the fifty-first chapter, on generation through meditation on the near-heart mantra.

Further, I shall explain the heart mantra,
which benefits all sentient beings.
Arisen from the wisdom of hūṃ,
great bliss arisen from bliss,
blue and white and fierce,
with eight faces, exceedingly terrifying,
with three eyes and sixteen arms,
he is adorned with a garland of heads.
He has a head ornament of a garland of skulls,
wears matted locks with a half moon,
his head is marked with a variegated vajra,
and his body is adorned with the six mudrās.
The terrifying great Bhairava
he presses down with his right foot extended;
the exceedingly terrifying Kālarātri as well
he presses down with his left foot extended.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he squeezes the breasts of Vārāhī.
Having flayed the skin of Brahmā, he wears it as an ornament.
A trident and an axe,
a khaṭvāṅga, a small drum, a supreme curved knife,
and the eighth holds a hook:
so are the right hands.
A skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
a head, the supreme one holding a noose,
a hammer, and the excellent threatening gesture:
the wisdom of Śrī is perfectly complete.
In his lap is the form of Vārāhī,
fierce, red and white,
her two thighs wrapped well around him,
her hair loose, with three eyes,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
abiding in his faces and hands.
She has a girdle of bone ornaments,
her face with fangs bared,
clothed in the directions, gazing with passion,
the circle of her hips broad.
Blue, white and likewise red,
yellow, green and smoke-colored,
the colors of smoke, ash and cloud:
she abides in faces and forms such as these.
The goddess holds an arrow and a bow,
and has cast aside the skin of Brahmā.
Upon a variegated lotus
she sits at the center of a moon and a sun.
Seated at the center of an eight-spoked wheel,
one should meditate on the outer maṇḍala.
In the center of the hub of the lotus
the Blessed One is certainly to be set.
oṃ is on the eastern petal,
and there one sets the Illuminating Ḍākinī.
va is on the northern petal,
and there one sets the Horse-Faced Ḍākinī.
jra is Vajraraudrī;
the ḍākinī should be set in the west.
he ru is set on the southern petal:
the ḍākinī of golden color.
Yellow and blue-green, white and red,
with three faces, contorted and fierce,
clothed in the directions, with loosened hair,
they are seated upon corpses,
and with the right leg extended they conquer the four māras.
Fierce, with garlands of skulls,
on the petals of the thick lotus,
they gaze with eyes of passion, and they desire;
through the mood of compassion they are bliss.
They hold arrow and bow,
supreme ones holding noose and hook;
they carry skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
and sound the small drum.
Yellow, green and blue;
blue-green, red and white;
white, green and blue;
red, blue and green:
thus, with their colors distinguished,
one should meditate on the supreme samaya deities.
In the four intermediate directions, vases
filled with bodhicitta are placed.
In the eastern direction is he;
her name is called Helika.
In the northern direction is ru;
the ḍākinī is Rucakī.
In the western direction is ru;
she is called Rucakamalī.
In the southern direction is ka;
she is known as Kaṅkālī.
In the direction of Agni is Hūṃkārī,
in the direction of Nairṛti is Phaṭkārī,
in the direction of Vāyu is Ḍamari,
and in Īśāna's is Kilikila.
These eight are great goddesses;
they bestow all accomplishments.
As with the faces and forms before,
so it is here as well.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
bell, small drum and curved knife,
with three eyes and loosened hair,
clothed in the directions, seated with the right leg extended,
ornamented with garlands of skulls,
adorned with the five mudrās,
fully endowed with all ornaments,
fierce, violent and exceedingly terrifying.
One should meditate on all of them seated upon corpses,
with three eyes, intoxicated with passion.
In the corner of Īśāna is she called Blue Utpala;
in the direction of Vāyu is she called Supreme Net;
in the direction of Nairṛti is she called Lambodarī;
in the direction of Agni is she called Śabarī.
Yellow, blue-green and blue,
and red-yellow is the fourth.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
curved knife and the supreme small drum,
and the fourth is a vajra spear.
Clothed in the directions, they sport with passion,
excellent women with hanging bellies,
their vulvas long and wide,
their three eyes rolling and fierce,
their hair loose, exceedingly terrifying,
with head ornaments of garlands of skulls,
decked with all the ornaments.
At the eastern door, from baṃ, one sets the ḍākinī called the Fierce One; at the southern door, from raṃ, the ḍākinī called the Playful Fierce One; at the western door, from sa, the ḍākinī called the Supreme Blazing One. At the northern door, from hā, is the ḍākinī called the Supreme Victorious Garland.
Blue, white and yellow indeed,
holding a hook and a small drum,
carrying skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
curved knife, head, and threatening gesture;
white, blue and green,
holding a noose and a small drum,
holding skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
holding threatening gesture, curved knife and head;
red, blue and green,
with an iron chain, small drum and curved knife,
holding skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
a head and the supreme threatening gesture;
green and white mingled,
with supreme faces yellow and red,
bell, small drum and curved knife,
khaṭvāṅga and skull cup,
and hands with a head and the threatening gesture:
with loosened hair, greatly fierce,
faces with three rolling eyes,
mouths gaping and tongues moving,
they sit in the postures with the right leg extended and with the left leg extended.
With head ornaments of garlands of skulls,
holding the supreme garment of the directions,
they are seated at the center of a sun on a corpse and a lotus,
adorned with garlands of heads.
All bear the five mudrās,
adorned with necklaces of heads,
and wear the crest of Akṣobhya,
their eyes rolling and moving.
Arisen from twenty-two syllables,
the supreme one should be meditated on in the heart.
This, of all the secret mantras,
is the supreme one of twenty-two syllables:
oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla saṃvar hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
Each syllable is set out,
and likewise the heart mantra of the ḍākinīs.
hūṃ śrī are the god and the goddess:
the union of body, speech and mind.

In the center is the purified Blessed One,
who should be meditated on and thoroughly contemplated.
The eight liberations are the various doors.
Seeing in terms of form is empty;
seeing outer forms while having no inner form is also empty;
seeing as one who beholds the beautiful and the unbeautiful is also empty.
Seeing in terms of the sphere of infinite space is also empty;
seeing in terms of the sphere of infinite consciousness is also empty;
seeing in terms of the sphere of nothingness is also empty;
the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception too is empty.
Seeing perception and feeling as ceased is also empty.

The sixteen arms too are emptiness: the emptiness of the inner, the emptiness of the outer, the emptiness of the outer and inner, the emptiness of emptiness, the great emptiness, the emptiness of the ultimate, the emptiness of the conditioned, the emptiness of the unconditioned, the emptiness of what is beyond extremes, the emptiness of what has neither beginning nor end, the emptiness of what is not discarded, the emptiness of intrinsic nature, the emptiness of all phenomena, the emptiness of own characteristics, the emptiness of non-apprehension, the emptiness of the nonexistence of entities, and the emptiness of essential nature.

Just as the Blessed One is,
so too is Vārāhī.
The Blessed One whose form is nondual,
glorious Heruka, is consciousness;
Vārāhī is like space.

The purity of the four goddesses:
the Illuminating Ḍākinī herself
is the analytical knowledge of meanings;
she who is the Horse-Faced One
is the analytical knowledge of phenomena;
the ḍākinī Vajraraudrī
is the analytical knowledge of definitions;
the Golden-Hued Ḍākinī
is the analytical knowledge of eloquence.
These goddesses are in the center of the petals.

As for the four reliances,
Helika is reliance on the meaning;
Rucakī is reliance on the letters;
Rucakamile relies on wisdom;
Kaṅkālī is reliance on consciousness.

On the wheels of the east, the north, the west,
and the south,
the four summaries of the Dharma are set out.
hūṃ is: all formations are impermanent.
phaṭ is: all formations are suffering.
Ḍamari is: all phenomena are without self.
kilikīla is: all phenomena are peace.

The wheels of Agni, Nairṛti, Vāyu,
and Īśāna, all of them,
are purified by the goddesses.
The four fearlessnesses: fearlessness in realizing all phenomena of entities is the Blue Utpala Goddess. Fearlessness in teaching all phenomena is Supreme Mindfulness. Fearlessness in entering the path of renunciation is Lambodarī. Fearlessness in knowing the exhaustion of the defilements is Śabarī.
The maṇḍala goddesses in the intermediate directions
purify the four corners by this.
The goddesses at the four doors:
She Who Makes Fierce is the faculty of faith;
the Playfully Fierce One is the faculty of diligence;
the Supreme Blazing One is the faculty of mindfulness;
the Supreme Victorious One is the faculty of meditative absorption.
The eighty excellent minor marks
are the Blessed One, the glorious Herukavajra.
The thirty-two major marks
are the noble Prajñā, the Blessed Lady,
Vajravārāhī herself.

This is the fifty-second chapter, on generation through meditating on the heart of twenty-two syllables, from the highest of the high, the Abhidhānottara Tantra.

Then, further, what is to be explained
is the purity of the maṇḍalas.
The eye of all the elements
is known as the lines of Vairocana.
The sequence pervaded by the bhaga
the wise know as the lines of the maṇḍala.
The times are known to be of various kinds,
and the various offerings follow the rite.
Pullīrama is the lotus.
Jālandhara is the wheel of mind;
Oḍḍiyāna is the wheel of speech;
Arbuda is the wheel of body.
Godāvarī is the four corners:
Rāmeśvara is the direction of Agni;
Devīkoṭa is Nairṛti;
Mālava is the corner of Vāyu;
Īśāna is Kāmarūpa.
Such is the purity of the four corners;
of this there is no doubt.
Oḍra is the eastern door;
Triśakuni is the southern door;
Kosala is the western door;
Kaliṅga is the northern door.
The eastern archway is Lampāka;
Kāñcī is the southern archway;
Himālaya is the western;
Pretapurī is the northern.
Gṛhadevatā is the divine frieze;
Saurāṣṭra is the jewel ground;
Suvarṇadvīpa is the half-nets;
Nagara is the yak-tail whisks,
the bells, the victory banners and the flags.
Sindhu is the circular vajra line;
Maru is the door alcoves;
Kulutā is the unsurpassed lines
of the vajra, of the jewel,
and of the maṇḍala.
It is four-cornered with four doors,
adorned with four archways,
adorned with nets and half-nets,
and adorned with silks and garlands.
All the corner sections,
and the junctions of the doors and door alcoves,
are adorned with vajras and jewels.
The very body of the wheel of body
is Amoghasiddhi, who moves through it.
The wheel of speech is Amitābha;
the wheel of mind is Akṣobhya;
the center of the lotus holds Ratnasambhava.
The one who has the form of the outer maṇḍala:
meditate on him as Vairocana.
Feeling is the wheel of body;
perception is the wheel of speech;
the wheel of mind is formations;
consciousness is the lotus, without doubt.
The fifth line, which is supreme,
has a nature purified by that.
All the grounds and all the lines
should be meditated on as completely purified by the heroes.
The twenty-four lines
are purified by the heroes.
The wheel is purified by the ḍākinīs.
At the hub is the seed of Vārāhī, kṣaḥ:
it is the glorious supreme Heruka.

The petals are purified by the ḍākinīs.
The maṇḍala, the supreme maṇḍala,
is the purity of the thirty-seven.
To summarize the heart of the maṇḍala:
the first maṇḍala is the letters;
the second maṇḍala is the hand emblems;
the third is said to be the mudrās of the hands;
the fourth is what has become form;
the fifth is the offering of flowers;
the sixth is the maṇḍala of the assembly itself.
The center is the ground Utterly Joyful,
together with the hub of the lotus.
The wheel of mind is the Stainless;
the wheel of speech is the Luminous;
the wheel of body is the Radiant;
the four corners are the Difficult to Master;
the four doors are the Far Gone;
the corner sections are the Immovable;
the vajra lines are Good Intelligence;
the archways are the Cloud of Dharma.
Having drawn such a maṇḍala,
one should always meditate on it and make offerings;
one will obtain vast accomplishments.
The reality of the supreme maṇḍala
of Cakrasaṃvara, the wheel of restraint,
is glorious Heruka, the supreme wisdom.
The maṇḍala of the bhaga lotus
is the supreme body of Vārāhī.
That is the gathering of the maṇḍala,
the maṇḍala of supreme great bliss.
The maṇḍala of hero and yoginī
is the maṇḍala of nondual union.
The reality of the self is meditative absorption;
the reality of the deity is likewise;
the reality of mantra is the mudrā.
Such a maṇḍala, the highest of the sublime,
transcends meditation on all the limbs,
is free of thought and conceptualization,
and transcends the bindu of the mother letters:
that is the supreme maṇḍala.
All phenomena cannot be examined;
it engages in both Dharma and non-Dharma.
Such a maṇḍala, as it is taught,
is like pure space.
Passion and dispassion are equal in passion,
arisen like a ruby and water together.
The passion of one's own mind is the supreme red;
these are the supreme maṇḍala.
Mentation is all the elements,
form and formless, without stain.
What urges and awakens is mentation;
mind is the maṇḍala of the enlightened mind.
The maṇḍala is said to be the body;
the maṇḍala is said to be the bhaga;
the maṇḍala is the mind of awakening,
pervading the moving and the unmoving,
below the earth, upon the earth, and above the earth.

This is the fifty-third chapter, teaching the meditation of the maṇḍala, from the highest of the high, the Abhidhānottara Tantra.

Then the Secret is to be explained,
which grants benefit to all sentient beings:
arisen from the vowels and consonants,
the supreme city of the dharmadhātu,
adorned with nets and half-nets,
and adorned with silks and garlands.
In its center is a great vajra
that transforms into a four-faced one.
In the middle of its hub
is a crossed wheel together with its hub,
the eightfold wheel of mind.
In the center of a sun disc,
in the middle of the wheel's hub,
is a multicolored eight-petaled lotus,
mixed with crossed vajras,
the twentyfold dwelling place of the deities,
taught to be in the middle of the hub.
In the center of the sun disc,
from hūṃ one makes Heruka,
four-faced and eight-armed,
three-eyed, his body white.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he squeezes the breasts of Vārāhī.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
his body adorned with the six mudrās.
With his outstretched right leg
he presses on the brains of all the wicked.
Bhairava and Kālarātri,
fainting and utterly intoxicated:
he presses their brains with his feet.
He tramples the naked ones who cry out,
with the skin of the Victor above.
With his hands he holds a hook,
and likewise a ḍamaru drum,
and holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga;
elsewhere too he holds a head.
His head is marked with a crossed vajra,
and he bears a half moon on his head.
He has a crown ornament of a garland of skulls,
his crest adorned with Akṣobhya,
and he wears the necklace of Brahmā.
Emanating his own seven wrathful ones,
just as the color of the Blessed One,
so too is the supreme Vārāhī.
Her body is black with a red sheen,
beautiful with the luster of splendor.
A curved knife, a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga,
a bell, a ḍamaru drum, a spear,
a hook and a noose likewise:
the goddess has eight arms.
Her faces are white, blue and green;
the upper face is a beautiful red.
The faces to right and left
are yellow and green in form.
Otherwise, just as the lord is,
so too is Vārāhī.
She is naked with her hair loose,
wearing a girdle of unbroken pieces.
On the four lotus petals are the ḍākinīs;
in the corners are vases and skull cups.
The wheel of mind is as explained before.
The heroes and ḍākinīs likewise
are on the two wheels of speech and body,
in the east, the north, the west and the south.
At the doors are the door guardians:
the Crow-Faced One and all the rest.
In the corners are Yamadāḍhī and the others.
The particulars, as one wishes,
were explained by Vajragarbha;
some hold them to be yoginīs.
Following the sequence of the guru's tradition,
the various families explained before
should be meditated on as one wishes.
Further, the faces and the colors
follow the colors of the ḍākinīs,
and the hand emblems and mudrās are as explained before.
Set out the positions and so forth in that order.
Following the rite explained before,
set out the secret mantras in their proper order.
The meaning of the purity of the thirty-seven:
the supreme meditation should be meditated on.
What arises within the vowels and consonants
should be meditated on and thoroughly contemplated.
Into the pure city of the dharmadhātu
the realm of sentient beings fully enters.
This yoga is secret;
it should not be taught to anyone.

This is the fifty-fourth chapter, on the meditation of the city of the dharmadhātu, from the highest of the high, the Abhidhānottara Tantra.

Then, further, what is to be explained
is the meditation of the bhaga lotus.
In response to Vajragarbha's request,
the Blessed One declared:
Truly secret, definitively secret,
the vowels and consonants abide in the triangle.
Meditate on a lotus of three petals.
Vajravārāhī and Heruka
are to be meditated on at the hub.
Vajraḍāka and Lāmā,
and the third is Khaṇḍarohā.

The Blessed One is red and blue;
Vārāhī too is red and blue.
The Ḍākinī is blue and yellow,
Lāmā is green and yellow,
and Khaṇḍarohā is red and yellow.
They are one-faced with four arms.
The Blessed Lord is four-faced,
four-legged and four-armed.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
and is well marked by bell and vajra.
He has a crown ornament of a garland of skulls,
and is marked with a crossed vajra.
Peaceful, he bears a half moon;
Akṣobhya binds his crest.
He always wears the secret garment,
and is adorned with a garland of skulls.
These are the face and marks of Vārāhī:
she embraces him closely with her two thighs;
naked, with her hair loose,
three-eyed, she clings to him in loving passion.
Bhairava and Kālarātri, the two,
he tramples under his feet,
and he stands in the posture with the right leg outstretched.
His body bears the six mudrās,
his fangs are bared,
and he is intoxicated with the passion of compassion.
The right face is white, the left green,
the upper pale blue; the body is white.
Rūpiṇī should be imagined as the bhaga;
the vowels and consonants are the supreme of the bhaga.
Further, in the middle of the triangle,
meditate on the wheel of the lotus.
In the center of the wheel of mind,
imagine a multicolored lotus.
In the center are Heruka and Vārāhī;
on the petals set the Ḍākinī and the others.
In the corners are white skull cups;
the wheel of mind moves in the sky.
In the eastern corner is the wheel of body;
on the wheel in the corner of Vāyu
are the Crow-Faced One and the rest, eight in all.
The wheel of speech is in the corner of Nairṛti.
Of the four wheels in the triangle,
the east is the ḍākinī of body,
the direction of Vāyu is the ḍākinī of Yama,
Nairṛti is the ḍākinī of speech,
and in the center is Vajraheruka.
Blue and white, smoke-colored,
and blue and red: these are the four.
Each holds its own hand emblems alike.
Otherwise, according to the color of each one's own lord,
the wheels of heroes are likewise of that color.
The ḍākinīs of the wheel of mind
should be visualized as red in color;
the color of the heroes is black,
joined in nondual embrace.
The heroes of the wheel of body
should be visualized with white bodies;
the ḍākinīs are yellow in color,
fully joined with the two heroes.
The Crow-Faced One, Yamadāḍhī and the rest:
of the two forms, half is female.
Together with their own means,
they are intoxicated with delight and passion.
The heroes of the wheel of speech are red,
and the ḍākinīs are green.
The signs in the sequence explained before
are again according to the division of the families.
All are seated on seats of pretas,
adorned with garlands of heads.
All bear the five mudrās,
and one sets out the lord of the six mudrās.
All wear the diadem of their own family.
Meditate on them in their sequence, just as they are.
By the application of the time of the ḍākinīs,
having meditated in accordance with how it is,
one should set out the secret mantras of the armor.
The sequences of body, speech and mind,
by the application of the rite explained before:
meditate on the sequence of the hero's limbs.
The calm abiding that is secret and hidden
is the wheel of bliss of the ḍākinīs.
Always resting in equipoise,
always meditate on the supreme union.
This should not be taught to anyone;
its reality should not be spoken to anyone.
If one always meditates on the union of the two organs,
accomplishment will come.
One should always guard the pledges
and also partake of foods and refreshments.
Going and going, at all times,
purify thoroughly at rising and setting.
Whoever listens to this wisdom,
that mantra practitioner will swiftly be accomplished.

This is the fifty-fifth chapter, on the meditation of secret union, from the highest of the high, the Abhidhānottara Tantra.

Then, further, what is to be explained
is the supreme secret wisdom.
The meditation of the vast secret mantras
of the five families I shall explain.
To those whose understanding is wrong understanding
you should not explain it at all.
As it is taught through the succession of the gurus,
by which one quickly comes to understand:
through thousands of koṭis of births,
urged on by the wisdom of illusion,
true faith is generated,
and one abides in the way of yogic conduct.
The mute, the dull, the wicked,
those whose understanding is a child's understanding,
sentient beings not fully ripened,
and those corrupted by outsider treatises:
none of them understand it.
Covered by the darkness of ignorance,
they always delight in conceit,
vicious-minded with pride and the like.
None of them understand
the secret domain of the buddhas.
Glorious Heruka truly enters;
by the great-souled master
accomplishment is swiftly produced,
and immediate conviction is brought about.
One who knows the various vows
should teach the activities of the rite.
The three realms of existence without exception:
he knows the teaching of the Omniscient One.
By seeing, by touching,
by hearing and by remembering,
one is freed from all evils;
thus it is, without doubt.
Arranging the deities in reverse,
reversing the divisions of the mantras,
reversing all the maṇḍalas as well,
and not meditating on them separately;
having extracted the reversed letters,
that reversed secret meditation
accomplishes all the heart mantras.
It gathers all the ritual manuals,
and is common to all the tantras.
For minds that are fully ripened,
here the pledges of the mantras,
of union and the rest, are thus taught.
It is spoken as the Sugata's consolation.
The pure body is without fault;
even a mantra practitioner of little merit
will find me through my delight.
There, there is no protection and nothing to eat,
no maṇḍala and no lord of the maṇḍala,
no recitation, no ascetic discipline and no fire offering;
in short, it has the character of a gathered mind.
The wisdom of the consciousness that is grasped
comes from diligence in what the guru has taught.
For outsider treatises
that path is very far away.
Yogins, by skillful means,
are diligent in attending upon the guru.

The defining characteristic of the Teacher is taught,
and the reality of the hero is taught.
Moment by moment is the self of yoga;
moment by moment it is endowed with the mudrā.
Everything, entirely, is momentary;
in an instant the secret mantra is spoken.
Having set the knees upon the ground,
one makes supplication oneself.
What the Blessed Goddess asked about
is the vast, nondual state.
This abides in all beings,
playing in the moving and the unmoving.
Vajragarbha said:
Listen well to what I shall explain as is fitting,
and grasp it with an examining mind.
That in which the self of all always abides
is just what was explained before:
the body is said to be the maṇḍala,
and it comes to have nine doors.
There the great wheel of the navel
is said to be called the Omniscient.
The vajra holder who has entered there,
partless and free of parts,
plays within embodied beings,
beyond the body and without stain.
Sometimes he dwells in forms;
sometimes in the nose and in speech;
sometimes as sounds in the ear;
sometimes as excrement and vajra water;
sometimes as the mind together with feeling;
sometimes as thirst and sometimes as hunger;
sometimes wrathful and sometimes peaceful;
sometimes he gazes in bliss;
sometimes he is desire for women;
sometimes he kills and destroys;
sometimes he beats the music;
sometimes he dwells as flower and fruit;
sometimes he plays and enjoys;
sometimes he dwells as the life force;
sometimes he is earth, water and fire;
sometimes the five great elements;
sometimes, by the distinctions of caste,
brahmins and caṇḍālas;
sometimes mother and sister;
sometimes wife and daughter;
sometimes Brahmā and Īśāna,
Viṣṇu and the sun itself;
sometimes the moon together with the stars;
sometimes he becomes day and night;
sometimes all the beings of the three realms,
the maker and the inexhaustible victor;
sometimes he is subdued by the power of peace,
the vajra holder of unmanifest emptiness;
sometimes he goes to great awakening;
sometimes he conquers Māra the enemy;
sometimes he has the very nature of a child;
sometimes he is old and decrepit;
sometimes seized by a great illness;
sometimes moving and unmoving;
sometimes blue and vermilion;
sometimes white and yellow;
sometimes the Buddha Vairocana;
sometimes Tārā and Pāṇḍaravāsinī;
sometimes Locanā and Māmakī;
sometimes Durgā, the Peaceful One;
sometimes birds and garuḍas,
the sky and the ḍākinīs;
sometimes nāgas and great serpents;
sometimes prostitutes and monkeys;
sometimes seeing dreams;
sometimes after falling asleep;
sometimes he becomes wind and bile;
sometimes blood and skin;
sometimes sorrow and smoke;
sometimes the burning fires;
sometimes birth in the heavens.
Such is this magical display.
In this in which the self of all abides,
the deity who is free of parts,
this is the variety of his illusion.
The vajra holder, who is not permanent,
is without form within the bhaga.
Within the navel is a great lotus.
Between the sixteen lines laid down,
by joining equally with the letters,
the act of union is done.
At its center, always, is the great secret,
which emits and gathers.
That very one, the vajra holder himself,
alone, self-arisen, the great deity,
is proclaimed by the name
"the reality of all buddhas."
In the delight of the supreme secret,
the self of all always abides.
This is what the Omniscient One explained,
or the path of the unchanging explained.
A hundredth part of the tip of a hair:
contemplate it as having the supreme form.
It is not empty, and cannot be apprehended;
it has no letters, and no emptiness either;
it has no stain and no seed;
it is without form and without signs.
The very entity of consciousness
should be grasped as the wisdom of consciousness.
The lamp that illuminates all
should be seen as the first sign.
A mere flash of illuminating lightning
should be imagined as the second sign.
Having the character of white light,
it is seen in the ten directions;
it is said to expel the afflictions.
The yogin should examine it thoroughly.
The enjoyments of the gods of desire
are taught as the fourth, consciousness.
The goddesses of form, touch and so on
are the five wisdoms of the directions.
The sixth is the wisdom of the formless;
the seventh is the dharmadhātu;
in the eighth, just as one wishes,
is the fruit of buddhahood.
Hidden gazing, the hand,
embracing, the pairs and the rest:
the Tathāgata spoke these as consolation.
The ninth is the highest of the high;
everything is taught here.
Other ways are like straw.
That itself is the life of living beings;
that itself is the unchanging sublime;
that itself is what pervades all,
abiding in all bodies.
That itself is the wisdom of the buddhas,
said to be glorious Heruka.
Male, neuter and so on,
it also holds the subtle essence.
Of that wisdom which always arises,
those who abide on the grounds,
the treatises without exception,
and those who hold the Vedic tenets,
in this world and in others,
without it none will be accomplished.
For the tenets of the Vedas
this is proclaimed to be the essence.
Sitting and rising,
entering and completion,
blessing and empowerment,
meditative absorption and offering: these are summarized.
Then, the rite by another name:
skillful means and wisdom, nondual;
all is born in that very reality,
which emits and gathers.
