a ā i ī u ū ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ e ai o au aṃ aḥ ka kha ga gha ṅa ca cha ja jha ña ṭa ṭha ḍa ḍha ṇa ta tha da dha na pa pha ba bha ma ya ra la va śa ṣa sa ha kṣaḥ
This is that Blessed deity,
the supreme itself, abiding preeminently.
Having known the generation of all the deities
and their accomplishment likewise,
the supreme ultimate is also likewise:
these are the eighty-four vehicles.
If one abides in the conduct of mantra,
by that one is surely victorious in this.
All proceed through wisdom and skillful means.
First one should speak the secret mantra.
Whatever is virtuous or nonvirtuous
is the ornament of the hosts of deities;
it is all the places of the girdle.
One who always holds to this
realizes all phenomena.
If the vowels and consonants are visualized in front,
the great yoga is at rest.
It is the inconceivable mind itself,
and the mind too should not be examined.

Do not think even of the mind itself;
by that one will attain retention.
That which is secret among all the tantras
I explain to you as well.
This should be carefully hidden:
the restraint of nondual nonduality.
Of all the yoginīs and the rest,
and of all the limbs of the hero,
the yogin attains the fruit;
it arises from within existence.
The method of the highest tantra
should not be explained to one who gives no service.
If it is explained, there is suffering,
and through suffering the mind goes round in circles.
The yogin whose mind goes round in circles
does not see nirvāṇa.
Just as a woman rejoices in childish chatter, and also repeats the talk of the babblers she keeps company with,
so, the path not being explained, there comes to be bondage: the bad path of yoga is said to be like that.
The limbs of the body of glorious Heruka,
all bodies, have the nature of the stable and the moving.
The site is the ground Utterly Joyful;
likewise the secondary site is the Stainless;
the field should be known as the Luminous;
the secondary field is the Radiant;
the meeting place is the Manifest;
the secondary meeting place is the Difficult to Master;
the gathering is the Far Gone;
the secondary gathering is the Immovable;
the charnel ground is Good Intelligence itself;
the secondary charnel ground is the Cloud of Dharma.
The conduct of the scripture of Śrī Heruka
has this inner nature.
The ten perfections and the grounds
are the yoginīs' language of signs.
In the heavens, upon the earth and below the earth,
the limbs of the hero are the self of the stable and the moving.
What has been taught since beginningless time
abides with the nature of outer and inner.
The glorious Blood-Drinker, the great king,
the chief lord of all desires,
everywhere possessing hands and feet,
everywhere with eyes, heads and faces,
everywhere possessing hearing in the world,
abides pervading all.
Then, accomplishment is to be accomplished
with a mind endowed with meditative concentration.
For the benefit of the practitioner,
the reality of the Secret has been spoken.
Then, further, what is to be explained
is a pith instruction very hard to find,
which in the hero's language of signs
I have hidden in the tantra.
The garments are the five mudrās and so on;
the limbs of wisdom are the dagger and net.
What is spoken of as the vowels and consonants,
causes and the rest, emptiness, are the preliminary.
From entering, sound and the rest,
up to the condensed, it is endowed with union.
The nectar that satisfies is nirvāṇa;
empowerment is conferred by the offering of the hand.
One is truly protected by the great armor;
it is the offering made with all the mantras.
Thus the fourteen realities
have been explained in summary.
Whatever supreme person practices them all,
the self purified of all evils,
attains the ground of the Tathāgata,
and in birth and old age is born in the family of the Sugatas,
becoming a king who possesses the Dharma.
If one thus performs the rite properly,
it fully grants all accomplishments.
If on the day of the full moon, upon the earth,
a yogin meditates in this way,
that becomes his merit.
Gods, nāgas, humans and all the rest:
by whatever merit of his has come about,
self and others are endowed with true wisdom.
By merely contemplating in meditative concentration,
or by reading, reciting and writing it,
one attains the enjoyments of the heavens,
or else attains the state of a universal monarch.
When the yoginīs pass away,
the glorious Blood-Drinker and the other ḍākas,
holding various flowers in their hands,
with various victory banners and flags,
with various sounds of music,
make offerings with offerings of various songs.
This concept called "death"
carries one to the abode of the sky-goers.
A yogin of such great glory
is hard to find upon the earth.

This is the fifty-sixth chapter, on generating and accomplishing the secret letters, from the highest of the high, the Abhidhānottara Tantra.

Then, further, what is to be explained
is the extraction of the mantras in the order of the rite.
In an utterly solitary place,
the wise one, having leveled the ground,
should smear it with excrement and urine.
Having smeared it with mahārakta as well,
and having made oneself into Heruka,
having taken a low-caste butcher woman,
one should set out flowers and the rest.
Offer the incense of the great hero;
light lamps of fat and the like.
Beat the ḍamaru drum and the bell;
make offerings with song and dance,
with the various kinds of divine music.
Clothed in the directions, with hair loose,
the body endowed with the six mudrās,
having made the vowels and consonants equal,
one should dissolve them into their classes.
Having known them in fours,
one should expand them with the supreme of sages.
The hero dwelling there should be extracted,
who accomplishes all one's aims and wishes.
Whatever is the fifth of the fourth,
and the fifth of the fifth,
and the fourth of the fifth,
and whatever is the third of the first,
and likewise the twenty-ninth;
whatever is the first of the fourth,
and the fourth of the semivowels,
and the second of that same group,
and take truly the thirtieth;
and the twenty-sixth itself,
and likewise half of half of a hundred,
then the thirty-third,
and the first of the first,
and the third of the semivowels,
and the first of the fifth,
and the third of the first itself;
the thirty-second itself;
the fifth of the fourth,
and the fourth of the fifth,
and the first of the semivowels,

and the fifth of the fifth,
and the first of the first,
and likewise the eleventh itself,

and the second of the ūṣmans itself;
whatever is the first of the first,
and likewise the twenty-seventh,
and the twenty-eighth as well;
the third of the first,
and the fourth of the fifth,

and the fifteenth itself,

whatever is the second of the first,
and likewise the twenty-sixth,
and the thirty-second letter;
the last of the ūṣmaṇas;
the thirty-second itself,

the second of the antasthas,

the first of the fifth,

whatever is the first of the fourth,

and the first of the third,
and whatever is the third of the first,

then take the letter to be made.

the second of the antasthas,

whatever is the fifth of the fourth,

The fourth of the antasthas,
and on the fifth of the fifth
set it in the place of the fourth.
On the first of the fifth
join it above, together with Agni.
The third of the ūṣmans,
the first of the antasthas,
and on the first of the fourth,
with the Radiant One made its seat.
The third of the seventh,
and the first of the sixth as well;
the letters ka and sa,
having taken the first ones,
the fourth of the fifth,

likewise combine them and take them.

The fourth of the ūṣmans itself,
with the sun as well made its seat.

each doubled, this is the fourth line:

kara kar kuru kuru bandha bandha trāsaya trāsaya kṣobhaya kṣobhaya hrau hrau hraḥ hraḥ pheṃ pheṃ phaṭ phaṭ daha daha paca paca bhakṣa bhakṣa vasa rudhira antra mālā valaṃ vine gṛhṇa gṛhṇa sapta pātāla gata bhūjig sarvaṃ vā tarjaya tarjaya ākaḍḍhya ākaḍḍhya hrīṃ hrīṃ jṇau jṇo kṣmāṃ kṣmāṃ hī hī hūṃ hūṃ kili kili sili sili hili hili dhili dhili hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ

This is the secret mantra, the king of knowledge mantras (vidyās),
which accomplishes every activity.
Anything more supreme than this
does not exist in the three worlds.
If one knows the mantra of the glorious Blood-Drinker,
all others are like grass without pith.
It is taught as the root mantra.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the highest, this is the fifty-seventh chapter, the rite setting out in detail the extraction of the letters of the root mantra.

Furthermore, the twenty-two letters,
the seven letters of the heart, the near-heart,
the seeds of the eight of the ya group,
and likewise the twelve vowel signs,
with the intervening letters left out:
the six limbs are Heruka,
joined equally with the six heroes.
Meditate on them in six squares,
and one becomes master of Heruka.
They abide fully in single letters:
oṃ namaḥ svāhā vauṣaṭa,
together with hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ;
the practitioner should know them as the heart.
The first is at the heart itself,
the second should be known at the crown of the head,
the third is joined to the topknot,
the fourth becomes the armor,
the fifth becomes the eyes,
and the sixth is held to be called the weapon.
Then the thirteenth vowel itself
is joined with the second letter.
The eleventh vowel is fully joined;
it is to be adorned with the sixth letter.
The fourth of the vowels itself
is joined also with the seventh letter.
The eleventh vowel itself
the wise one joins to the eighth letter.
Joined with the second vowel,
it is to be adorned with the ninth letter.
The second vowel letter itself
is joined with the twelfth letter.
Possessing the second vowel,
take the twenty-first letter.
The wise one joins the fourteenth letter
with the twentieth letter.
The third of the vowels itself
is joined to the fifteenth letter.
Fully endowed with the fifteenth vowel,
the sixteenth letter is to be beautified.
Endowed with the third vowel,
it is held to be the seventeenth letter.
The second of the vowels itself
is joined to the eighteenth letter.
The second vowel itself
is joined to the twenty-first letter.
It is joined also with the fifth of the alphabet;
it is joined to the twenty-third letter.
The thirteenth vowel itself
is joined to the twenty-fourth letter.
Having taken the first class,
it is joined to the twenty-fifth letter.
Endowed with the second vowel,
it becomes the twenty-sixth letter.
The twenty-eighth letter
is joined with the fifteenth letter.
It is fully endowed with the first of the third,
its lower part together with Agni.
Likewise, joined to the second vowel,
is the twenty-ninth letter.
To the thirty-first letter
the second vowel is joined.
The thirty-second letter also
is to be endowed with the second vowel.
The thirty-third letter
is to be fully endowed with the sun.
The fourth of the alphabet itself,
endowed with the thirty-fourth letter,
accomplishes every desired aim.
The fifth of the alphabet itself
is fully endowed with the thirty-seventh;
it is what accomplishes the ḍākinīs.
The thirty-eighth itself,
joined with the second vowel:
that which accomplishes all the gods
is this very one, without doubt.
The forty-second itself
should be formed with Agni and below.
The forty-third itself
is joined with the fifth vowel itself.
The forty-fifth letter
is joined to the second vowel letter.
The fifth vowel itself as well,
endowed with the forty-sixth,
makes offering to all the heroes.
The forty-seventh itself,
joined with the second vowel,
is held to be Vajrasattva, the supreme.
Likewise the fifty-first,
by the fifth vowel itself,
is held to be supreme beauty.
The fifty-second itself,
joined with the second vowel:
that which accomplishes all the gods
is this very one, without doubt.
Likewise the fifty-third as well
is joined with the thirteenth vowel.
This is supreme among the letters;
it surely slays enemies.
It is to be made from the four classes.
The fifty-sixth letter
is joined with the sixth vowel.
The fourth of the antasthas
should be joined to the second vowel,
and likewise joined with the fifteenth;
it accomplishes all accomplishments.
It becomes the fifty-ninth letter.
The sixty-first letter itself
is joined with the second vowel;
this is declared to be supreme.
Likewise the sixty-second,
joined to the second vowel,
is held to be supreme for the ḍākinīs.
The sixty-third letter itself,
endowed with the eleventh vowel,
is supreme among all beautiful letters.
The sixty-fourth itself,
set apart by Vajrasattva,
fully endowed with the second vowel,
grants accomplishment, the great liberation.
The sixty-fifth letter,
fully endowed with abiding upon Agni,
joined with the third vowel,
grants all accomplishments, said the Sugata.
The sixty-sixth letter,
joined with the third vowel,
grants accomplishment in every activity:
thus the Tathāgata has said.
The sixty-seventh letter,
fully endowed with the second letter:
all the purposes of the yoginīs
this very one fulfills, without doubt.
From the sixty-eighth itself,
join there the letter ba,
and its lower part is to be examined.
The seventy-first letter
is fully joined to the second letter;
the beautiful one among all letters
is this very one, without doubt.
The thirty-fourth itself,
joined to the second vowel,
is accomplished from the very beginning:
so it was spoken from the mouth of the Tathāgata.
The seventy-fifth letter itself,
endowed with the sixth of the mātṛkā:
this is the supreme yoga,
most excellent of all virtues.
The seventy-sixth itself,
endowed with the second vowel
and endowed with Agni below:
apart from this, nothing else is beautiful.
The seventy-seventh letter:
there it should be joined with da;
it is held to be the beauty of the ḍākinīs.
The seventy-eighth itself
is joined with the second vowel;
it is held to be the supreme of causes.
The eighty-first letter
is distinguished by the end of the fourth.
This is what accomplishes from the very beginning,
that which leads into all accomplishments.

The eighty-second letter
is likewise joined to the second vowel.
On the fifth of the alphabet,
likewise the eighty-eighth letter.
The fifth of the mātṛkā,
and likewise the eighty-ninth;
having taken the fifth of the mātṛkā,
likewise the ninetieth letter.
Having taken the fifth of the mātṛkā,
it is joined to the ninety-first letter.
The second of the mātṛkā,
and the ninety-sixth letter;
the ninety-ninth letter,
endowed with the second vowel,
terrifies all the wicked
and is the abode of bliss of all the mātṛkās.
The thirteenth vowel letter
is endowed with the second letter.
The thirteenth of the mātṛkā itself
is joined with the fifth letter;
it throws all the wicked into turmoil
and is the abode of bliss of the ḍākinīs.
With the fourteenth vowel
it is joined with the eighth letter,
and fully joined with the fifteenth.
The fourteenth vowel itself
is joined with the ninth letter,
and above it the bindu fully abides.
These letters are to be accomplished;
they are the distinctive mark of the ḍākinīs.
The eleventh vowel itself
is joined with the twelfth letter,
and above it is adorned with the bindu.
The eleventh vowel letter
is joined with the thirteenth letter,
and it too is adorned with the bindu.
It terrifies all the bhūtas
and is the delight of the practitioner.
To the fifth vowel letter
join the thirty-second letter.
The third of the vowels itself
is joined with the thirty-third letter.
With the second of the mātṛkā itself
it is joined to the thirty-fourth letter.
With the second of the vowels
it is joined with the thirty-sixth letter.
The second of the first
is joined with the thirty-seventh letter.
The third vowel letter
is joined with the fortieth letter.
The eleventh of the mātṛkā itself
is joined with the forty-first letter.
The second vowel letter
is joined to the forty-second letter.
With the third of the vowels
it is joined with the forty-fourth letter.
With the second of the mātṛkā itself as well
it is joined to the forty-eighth letter.
The second vowel letter
is joined with the forty-ninth letter.
The second vowel letter
is joined to the fifty-second letter.
With the fifth of the alphabet itself
it is joined with the fifty-fifth letter.
The fifty-fourth letter
is also adorned with the bindu.
The fifty-fifth letter
is also adorned with the bindu;
it is joined to the second vowel letter.
The fifty-eighth letter
should be pressed on its head with the bindu.
The second vowel letter
is joined to the sixtieth letter.
The second of the alphabet itself as well
is joined with the sixty-seventh letter.
It accomplishes every accomplishment against the wicked
and also bestows glory on the practitioner.
The fourth vowel letter
is joined with the seventieth letter,
and it too is adorned with the bindu.
With the fourth of the alphabet
it is joined with the seventy-third letter.
Taking well the fifteenth,
with the fourteenth vowel
it is joined to the seventy-fourth letter.
With the fourteenth of the alphabet
it is joined with the seventy-third letter,
and likewise joined with the fifteenth.
The second vowel letter
is joined with the seventy-fourth letter.
The second letter of the alphabet as well
is joined with the seventy-seventh letter,
and it too is adorned with the bindu.
The second of the alphabet as well
is joined with the seventy-eighth letter,
and adorned with two bindus.
The second of the alphabet as well
is joined with the seventy-ninth letter.
With the fourth of the mātṛkā itself
it is joined with the eightieth letter;
it should be pressed on its head with the bindu.
The fourth vowel as well
is joined with the seventy-ninth letter,
produced by the sound of the bindu,
and it too is adorned with the bindu.
The third vowel letter
is joined with the sixteenth letter.
The number of letters, below and above,
is declared to be ninety-seven.
This the wise
should know as the secret root mantra.
It is the abode of accomplishments,
and when the heart of the ḍākinīs is subdued,
the glorious Blood-Drinker, king of the deity families,
by its recitation makes accomplishment fruitful.
Place oṃ at the beginning
and join hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ at the end:
this is the secret root mantra,
which accomplishes every accomplishment.
Then all the heroes of one's own nature
joined in union
are held to be the net of ḍākinīs,
the wheel of Cakrasaṃvara.

hā svā raṃ va saṃ la jā nī ki ḍā ṭaph hūṃ hūṃ kaṃ ru ru he he jra va śrī oṃ

The heart of the king of knowledge mantras
has never arisen before and will not arise again.

ṭaph hūṃ hūṃ ha ha hrīḥ oṃ

The letters vaṃ and yoṃ,
the letters maṃ and hri,
and hūṃ and phaṭ: so it is declared.

oṃ vaṃ vajra vārāhi haṃ yoṃ yamini hrīṃ moṃ mohani hreṃ hreṃ saṃ calni hūṃ hūṃ saṃtrāsani phaṭ phaṭ caṇḍika

The yoginīs are proclaimed as six;
meditate on them in the six squares.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the highest, this is the fifty-eighth chapter, on meditating on the heart mantra of the armor.

Then, furthermore, I shall explain
the unsurpassed heart mantra of the goddess:

oṃ vajra vairocanīye hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

As for the application of the goddess's heart mantra,
the thirteenfold one is virtuous in nature.
In the center of a lotus is Vārāhī,
and on the petals the goddesses are placed.
In the quarters of the four gates
meditate on the heart mantras of the goddesses:
the Horse-Faced One (Hayāsyā), arisen from oṃ,
Vajragarbha, the Sound of the Wicked,
the Fierce One and the Moving One,
the Stealer of Men and Vimalā,
the goddess endowed with the one who makes hūṃ,
the One of the Burnt Offering and the one who makes phaṭ,
and the Self-Arisen One: these are twelve.
The one who makes ha is the chief on the right.
She who is called Vajravārāhī
bestows every accomplishment.
Blue, yellow and red,
green, with the upper face white,
three-eyed, with grimacing faces,
her fangs fiercely bared,
she sits with the right leg extended, wearing a garland of skulls,
her belly bulging, her hair hanging loose,
clothed in the directions,
and she abides in union with her own consort, her means.
Bhairava, Cāmuṇḍī and the Terrifying Intoxicated One
she keeps pressed down beneath her feet.
A skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
a spear, a curved knife and a ḍamaru drum,
a vajra and bell, a noose,
a hook, the head of Brahmā,
an axe, and the threatening gesture;
a human skin is stretched above her.
With girdle and armlets
she is adorned with the five mudrās,
and wears a crown of the five buddhas,
compassionate, with a wrathful frown, terrifying.
On the petals place all the goddesses.
The mātṛkās are eight:
yellow, red and green,
the south one blue in color,
in Agni's quarter red, and smoky,
blue and white,
placed around as far as Īśāna.
They are three-eyed, with hair hanging loose,
fully endowed with the five mudrās,
seated well in the half cross-legged posture,
with crowns of skulls,
clothed in the directions, with grimacing faces,
and all of them seated on seats of pretas.
Intoxicated with compassion and passion,
they perform the supreme vajra dance,
holding skull cups filled with blood (rakta),
and they sound bells and ḍamaru drums;
they bring delight to the gods.
At the gates are the gatekeepers,
seated upon Indra,
seated with the right leg extended,
three-eyed, with hair standing on end,
with crowns made of garlands of skulls,
decked with ornaments of garlands;
a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
a vajra and a head they hold in their hands.
All are wrathful, with hanging bellies,
all adorned with garlands of heads,
blue and green and white
and yellow, four-armed and of two colors,
naked, with a wide lotus,
crowned with the crowns of their own families,
and all with their own consorts as their second.
Meditate on the thirteen.
This is the meditation on the supreme heart mantra;
it bestows all that one desires.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the highest, this is the fifty-ninth chapter, teaching the meditation on the heart mantras of the armor and the goddesses.

Then, furthermore, I shall explain
an arrangement extremely hard to find:
by merely knowing it,
accomplishment is swiftly achieved.
A piece of ground, level and pleasing,
should be anointed with sandalwood.
On the ground, fragrant and sweet-smelling,
scatter loose flowers as a carpet,
and make it delightful with the scent of incense.
Having done all one's own preliminary tasks
and having performed the binding of the directions,
then make the lacing of a clay drum,
and on a round of forty-nine squares
set out the letters of the alphabet.
From what abides there, the hero
extracts what accomplishes every desired aim:
the practitioner takes vowels and letters
in their order and also out of order.
From that count multiplied by the munis,
take the thirty-second square;
it is to be distinguished by the fifth.
Take well the half of fifty;
it is divided by the twenty-fourth.
Take the thirty-sixth square;
it is distinguished by the third.
Then take the thirty-second;
it is to be distinguished by the fifth.
Then take the twenty-fifth
and join it with the twenty-fourth.
Take well the thirty-third,
endowed with the fifteenth square,
and fully together with the fifth.
Then take the twenty-second,
and the eleventh likewise;
this is held to be supreme beauty.
The nineteenth of the squares
is fully endowed with the son of Agni
and divided in the third square.
The seed of the eight of the ya group,
fully endowed with the thirty-sixth square,
becomes the letter.
Again, those very letters
accomplish every desired aim.
Then, having taken the Self-Arisen,
join it to the fifth of the squares,
and on its crown join the bindu.
Then take the twenty-second,
and the eleventh likewise.
The words "accomplishing all aims"
are declared to be the second phrase.
The nineteenth square itself
is divided by the twenty-seventh
and fully endowed with the third.
Take well the thirty-third
and join it to the second square;
fully endowed with the twentieth,
it is held to be the supreme of beauty.
Likewise the twenty-first itself,
and likewise the twenty-sixth:
in the words recited twice,
extract them in deep equipoise.
Then take them in reverse order:
the first of the squares itself,
fully endowed with the end of the fourth,
and likewise joined with the fifteenth.
Then take the twenty-second,
and the eleventh likewise.
The words of Dharma, wealth, desire and liberation
are declared to be the third phrase.
The seed of the second square,
the thirty-sixth square itself,
likewise the twenty-sixth,
and take well the thirty-third,
endowed with the thirteenth square;
the tenth of the squares itself
the practitioner takes in reverse order.
The nineteenth of the squares,
on the twenty-third square,
is fully endowed with the second,
and above it is to be adorned with the bindu.
Then take the seed of water,
and the eighth likewise,
distinguished by the son of Agni.
Join the letters hūṃ phaṭ at the end.
This is called the King of the Three Realms,
adorned with four mantra phrases.

oṃ is what makes the body shine forth;
hūṃ and phaṭ are joined at the end.
This mantra of the universal monarch among knowledge kings
has never arisen before and will not arise again.
In the Compendium of Reality, the Glorious Supreme,
the Cakrasaṃvara and the Vajrabhairava,
it is endowed with the four wrathful ones,
together with the eight forms,
for the purpose of protecting the maṇḍala,
and especially against all the wicked.
By merely obtaining this,
without any need for examination, they will die.
With the great meats, each and every one,
it is declared, even without a vajra.
What is spoken here is the common yoga;
it brings forth the fruit of every accomplishment.

oṃ sumbha ni sumbha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ grihṇa grihṇa huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ grihṇāpaya grihṇāpaya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ānaya ho bhagavāṃ vajra huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

This is declared to be terrifying
and unbearable to all the wicked.
On the supreme ground of the element of space,
in the very center of the lotus's hub,
meditate on Vajrahūṃkāra,
who surely subdues all the wicked.
He is fully endowed with sixteen arms;
he has eight faces, grimacing,
the fangs of his faces fiercely bared,
and he is adorned with a garland of skulls.
He wears a crown of the five buddhas,
and is decked with every ornament,
with the six mudrās marked with the vajra,
his body fierce, his mood compassionate.
Standing in the posture with the right leg extended,
he presses down the skull of Īśāna
and presses the breasts of the supreme Umā.
He gazes with wrathful eyes,
wearing on his body a necklace of heads,
a garland of heads bound upon his head,
and he forms the mudrā of vajra and bell.
Likewise there is Trailokyavijaya:
binding the hook and the threatening gesture,
it rests fully above the forehead;
the mudrā at the heart and the forehead
is what subdues the obstructors.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
bears a sword and a hook,
a spear, a head and a ḍamaru drum,
and above, a human skin:
so too the sixteen arms.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
a body of bliss, nondual with his own consort.
Blue, white and yellow
and likewise red, the left faces are beautiful;
white, blue and yellow,
and the upper face red,
three-eyed, with red eyes.
He is fully endowed with the four wrathful ones,
and virtuously performs every activity:
the Vajra of Desire, the Blue Vajra,
the Moving Vajra itself,
and the Vajra of Acala,
blue and green and red,
and smoky mixed with saffron,
their faces with fangs fiercely bared,
adorned with garlands of skulls.
With their feet in the right-extended posture
they press down the four māras.
Their bodies are adorned with human bones,
and the various families of wrathful ones radiate from them.
They hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
a vajra and the extended threatening gesture,
and embrace their own consorts, nondual.
They gaze with the wrathful look,
fully endowed with the bell as emblem,
and form the mudrā of holding;
with the mudrā of Trailokyavijaya
all the wrathful ones are bound.
They wear tiger skins as garments,
three-eyed, unbearable, terrifying,
with hammer, club and arrow,
and likewise sword and bell,
or else vajra and bell;
all follow the families of the wrathful ones.
Their embracing arms are likewise;
they have three faces and six arms,
all three-eyed: the supreme accomplishment.
By joining body, speech and mind,
the maṇḍala is the most sacred supreme accomplishment.
The thirty-seven, fully purified:
one should meditate on the maṇḍala of letters.

oṃ sumbha ni sumbha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ grihṇa grihṇa huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ grihṇapaya grihṇāpaya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ānaya ho bhagavāṃ vajra huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

Having seen the heroes and yoginīs,
meditate on generating them all.
Joined in the great maṇḍala,
the very essence of the five unions,
it is the maṇḍala of the yogin;
it is the meditation on the supreme heart mantra.
Likewise the meat of dog and pig,
and likewise of the one with the copper crest:
in the work of fierce rites
make them food, fire offering and torma.
Then all is accomplished without remainder,
and every kingdom is fully won.
Conch, mother-of-pearl and pearl:
if all three arise from a cause,
then who would revile the skull
of one whose body is the wisdom of the Dharma?
The hero wears a garland of skulls
and is adorned with the half moon;
this is declared to be the heroic power of heroes,
the form of Herukavajra.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, highest of the highest, this is the sixtieth chapter, on the generation of Vajrahūṃkāra, the maṇḍala of the four wrathful ones drawn in detail, blessed as the four-faced protection.

Then, furthermore, I shall explain
the meditation on the eight classes.
Abiding in the center of the lotus of the sphere of space,
meditate on Herukavajra:
he has five faces and ten arms,
three eyes, deep red,
and sits with the right leg extended.
His faces grimace, terrifying,
the faces with fangs fiercely bared,
and he is adorned with a garland of skulls.
The fierce one bears the half moon,
his head marked with a crossed vajra,
bound with a crown of matted locks,
and adorned with the signs of the six mudrās.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
in the mood of bliss, nondual with his consort,
on his body a necklace of fresh skulls,
pervasively intoxicated with the mood of compassion.
White and blue, of the color of clouds,
his body is graceful and swaying.
His right and left faces
are yellow and red and green,
and of the color of smoke.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he fully embraces his own consort.
Having flayed the skin of Brahmā, he wears it,
holding it firmly across his back.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
a spear, a ḍamaru drum and a curved knife,
and holds skull cups as well.
The Blessed One, surely arising from vaṃ hūṃ,
keeps Bhairava, together with his own wife,
pressed down beneath his feet.
On the eastern petal the goddesses are placed,
and likewise as far as the southern quarter;
those in Agni's quarter and the rest are also to be placed,
and likewise as far as Īśāna and the rest.
The ḍākinī of a,
likewise the ḍākinī of ka,
the ḍākinī of ca,
and the ḍākinī of ṭa:
white and red and yellow,
and the one of blue color in the south.

red and smoke-colored, green,
and yellow are properly placed as far as Īśāna.
The ḍākinī of ta,
likewise the ḍākinī of pa,
then the ḍākinī of ya,
and the ḍākinī of śa, the eighth:
all of them have three eyes,
one face and four arms.
With the right leg extended,
they press the eight world protectors beneath their feet.
Their faces are contorted, fangs bared;
they wear the supreme garment of the directions,
and are adorned with the five mudrās.
Their hair hangs loose; compassionate, yet like the wrathful ones,
fierce, they throw the land into turmoil
and accomplish every desired aim.
They fulfill the wishes of all beings;
they bring everything to completion, just as desired.
They hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga
and are adorned with bell and ḍamaru.
The ḍākinīs of the classes are bliss:
the yoginī should meditate on them.
Or else Vārāhī should be set in place,
and the mistress too should be placed.
As their lord, Vajrasattva
should also be meditated upon by the wise.
Those joined as body, speech and mind
throw into turmoil what is beneath the earth and upon it.
oṃ āḥ a ka ca ṭa ta ya śa huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
The lord of all that is stationary and moving,
whatever there may be, without exception, is accomplished by this.
The holy and supreme ḍākinīs of the classes
should be meditated upon in the middle of the bhaga.
a śa kā śaṃ carma ca ṭaṃ ta pa ka ta pa sa ta tha pa ba na jaṃ yaṃ ma bha haṃ ca śar bha ca ta tha
Together with honey and butter,
one should always eat these eight at the proper time.
Without aging, sickness or death,
accomplishment comes in this very life.
Together with drinks of liquor,
merely by eating them, one gains power.
Even if they are not found, one should use sālu rice.
Dwelling within the seventh birth,
if one always takes cattle as food,
in an instant one becomes a sky-goer.
About this there is no doubt to weigh.

From the Supreme of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixty-first chapter, on the practice of the classes.

Then, further, this is to be explained:
the donkey (gardabha), by the supreme yoga.
The maṇḍala of heroes and yoginīs
and of the mātṛkās was explained before.
The ḍākinīs and the other sky-goers
should be made in the form of the donkey.
The form of Vārāhī as well,
her face is to be made a donkey's.
The positions, emblems and faces explained before
are further to be understood.
Following the sequence of signs explained before,
the ḍākinīs hold spears.
All those of the mind wheel
are terrifying, with garuḍa faces;
those of the speech wheel have peacock faces,
those of the body wheel lion faces.
Likewise there are faces of dog, pig,
owl and crow;
in the corners, Yamadāḍhī and all the rest
have the faces of buffalo and deer.
All those of the entire maṇḍala
have three eyes, and their eyes are deep red.
This is the holy and supreme yoga;
it brings all suffering to an end.
To the one whose mind is set on yoga,
the supreme yoga will grant accomplishments.
It grants the instructions of the Vajrayāna
and the accomplishment of speech.
By teaching the Dharma not heard before,
beings are freed from existence.
By the unsurpassed itself being taught,
accomplishments such as invisibility arise.
Whoever it may be, the three worlds,
all of them, are seen at once.
The secret mantras of the heroes and yoginīs
explained before are likewise;
likewise those of the ḍākinīs and mātṛkās.
If one recites the lords of mantra explained before,
they grant accomplishment in full,
and one always attains every bliss.
If one recites the near-heart of the yoga,
with the donkey accomplishment one travels through the sky.
Even one without observances attains the observances;
even the impure becomes pure;
even the poor becomes a lord of wealth;
even the sick becomes free of sickness;
even the blind will see;
and one will attain the five superknowledges.
Donkey flesh and horse flesh,
dog, cow, elephant, human,
camel and jackal likewise:
these eight are equal and supreme.
The yogin should eat these eight.
Excrement, water, honey and butter:
having meditated in meditative concentration, and having eaten them,
one will attain the accomplishment of lightness,
the accomplishment of transforming one's form,
and one returns to one's own form as well.
Wherever the yogin dwells,
what is to be eaten is declared to be eaten;
likewise with what is to be approached and not approached:
drinking and drinking, accomplishment comes.
Mother, daughter, sister,
one's own kinswomen, and niece:
by the wise, as they wish,
the mudrā and the great mudrā are accomplished.
If one meditates on the donkey ḍākinī,
the accomplishment of summoning comes swiftly.
This supreme thing too is secret;
it is not to be told to anyone.

From the Supreme of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixty-second chapter, on accomplishing the cause of accomplishment, the donkey activity performed as desired.

Then, further, this is to be explained:
the meditation on the five ḍākas,
the rite of generating them from the families.
Seats, emblems and seed syllables:
this is the supreme seed.
ṅa ña ṇa na ma:
this is the supreme, accomplished seed.
ṇa is Vajraḍāka,
ṇa is Buddhaḍāka,
ṇa is Ratnaḍāka,
na is Padmaḍāka,
ma is Viśvaḍāka.
The five ḍākas are supreme.
Aversion, delusion, pride,
desire and jealousy likewise:
by these five families
the result of freedom from desire is bestowed.
Vajra, wheel, jewel,
lotus, and sword the fifth;
blue, and white,
yellow, red and green.
The vajra mudrā is supreme awakening;
the jewel mudrā likewise;
the lotus mudrā is the fourth,
and the sword mudrā the fifth.
Elephant, lion, horse,
peacock and garuḍa likewise;
Viṣṇu, Brahmā, the Sun,
Maheśvara, and Indra too.
Together with self-awareness and its limbs,
they abide properly in nondual bliss.
In the east, the south, the west,
and in the north likewise, here
they should be placed with their retinue of heroes.
The five are truly the five buddhas;
the five are the five selves.
In the center is a sun maṇḍala,
in the east a moon maṇḍala,
in the south and the west likewise,
and in the north a sun maṇḍala.
Or else, in the center a moon,
and in the east a sun seat.
They have become heroes, namely:

Vajrakapāla, Vajra Equal to Space, Vajragarbha and Vajra Solid. These four are the maṇḍala of Vajraḍāka, and an elephant throne should be made as their seat.

Vajracandra, Vajraketu, Vajrarāja and Vajraśabda: these are the maṇḍala of Buddhaḍāka, and their seat is a lion throne. The Vajra of Desire, Vajrarākṣasa, Vajraṭahara and Vajrahāsa: these are the maṇḍala of Ratnaḍāka, and their seat is a horse throne. Vajra Fire-Glory, Vajra Destroyer, Vajra Burner and Vajravimala: these are the maṇḍala of the lotus, and their seat is a peacock throne. Vajrapatākā, Vajragīta, Vajravīra and Vajrabhairava: these are the maṇḍala of the action ḍāka, and their seat is a garuḍa throne.

These heroes, too, belong to their own families. They wear garlands of skulls and have three eyes. Embracing their own wisdom consorts, they sit in vajra posture on seats of pretas. Their fangs are bared and their faces contorted; they wear tiger skins as garments. Each has one face and four arms, holding a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga, with the sound of the ḍamaru, hūṃ. They are adorned with necklaces of garlanded heads, bear the five mudrās, wear the crown ornament of their own family, and bear matted locks and the moon.

For Vajraḍāka, the east is white, the north green, the west red and the south yellow. For the action ḍāka, the east is yellow, the north blue, the west white and the south red. For the lotus ḍāka, the east is white, the north green, the west blue and the south yellow. For Ratnaḍāka, the east is green, the north yellow, the west white and the south blue. For Buddhaḍāka, the east is blue, the north green, the west red and the south yellow.

In due order, these
are to be encircled all around with the implements:
vajra, wheel, sword,
lotus, and garlands of jewels.
In the four corners one should meditate on the four mātṛkās:
the mātṛkā who bewilders,
the mātṛkā who is aversion itself,
the mātṛkā of desire,
and likewise the mātṛkā of jealousy.
They are to be placed in Agni's quarter and the rest.
White, blue, red and green,
with loose hair and contorted faces,
three-eyed and naked,
they bear the five mudrās.
Four-armed and one-faced,
they sit upon seats of pretas.
They abide in the maṇḍalas
of Mahendra, of fire, of water and of Vāyu,
seated upon the seats of their families.
Wheel, vajra, lotus,
sword, skull cup, khaṭvāṅga,
and the curved knife too they hold in their hands.
One should meditate on the goddesses arisen from the neuter syllables,
wearing garlands of skulls,
seated in half cross-legged posture.

Vajrayakṣa, Vajrarakṣa, Vajra Swaggerer, Vajra Horse-Face, Vajra Bear-Face, Vajrasaṃvara, Vajrasamaya and Vajra Paramāśva: these kings of the wrathful ones
are at the gates and in the intermediate quarters.
Blue, green, red and yellow,
red, blue, smoke-colored and white.
Hook, noose, chain,
bell, skull cup, khaṭvāṅga,
and the ḍamaru likewise;
hammer, club, spear,
axe, skull cup, khaṭvāṅga,
and a head they carry in their hands.
One-faced and four-armed,
their red-yellow hair standing on end,
they bear the five mudrās
and wear tiger skins as garments.
They wear garlands of skulls
and are adorned with necklaces of heads.
Upon a sun and a lotus
they stand with the right leg extended,
seated on thrones of the eight protectors of the directions.
Fangs bared, faces contorted,
three-eyed, crowned with the diadem of their own family,
they join nondually with their own consorts.
Endowed with the flavor of compassion,
they should be meditated upon as encircled
on the outside by vajra pretas.
First the consonant, the name and hūṃ,
and at the end one should join phaṭ.
One should make the protection with the rite explained before.
On the aggregates, elements and sense sources,
and on body, speech and mind, one should meditate.
The ḍākas have six faces and twelve arms;
with their own emblem, the bell,
they are joined in equality, without duality.
Spear, hook, skull cup,
khaṭvāṅga, head,
ḍamaru and curved knife,
and human skin they hold.
Blue, yellow, red, green,
smoke-colored and white;
white, blue, yellow, red,
green, and the color of ash;
yellow, blue, white,
red, green, and the color of the lotus;
red, white, blue,
yellow, green, and the color of the sky;
green, white, red,
blue, yellow, and the color white:
these are the distinctions in the colors
of the faces of the five ḍākas.
Or else one should meditate on them with one face and two arms.
The garland secret mantras together with the names,
the wise place from the four corners and the intermediate quarters.
At the end one should join huṃ hūṃ phaṭ.

oṃ mahāsukha vajraḍāka hūṃ ḍākinījāla saṃpara āḥ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ mahāsukha buddhaḍāka bhruṃ ḍākinījāla saṃpar āḥ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ mahāsukhar ratnaḍāka hraṃ kinījāla saṃbara ā hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ mahāsukha padmaḍāka hrīḥ ḍākinījāla saṃbara āḥ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ mahāsukha viśvaḍāka khaṃ ḍākinījāla saṃbara āḥ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

These are the heart secret mantras of the five ḍākas, and this is what has become the heart of wisdom.

oṃ vajraḍāka ṅaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ buddhaḍāka ñaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ratnaḍāka ṇaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ padmaḍāka ṇaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ viśvaḍāka maṃ hūṃ phaṭ

Having meditated on the wisdom being and the pledge being
as one in taste, the wise one
bestows empowerment well through the pledge,
and meditates on various offerings.
Recalling the state of all the maṇḍalas,
one also bestows empowerment.
The supreme of various excellences:
likewise embracing and kissing (cumbana),
the union of bhaga and liṅga,
the supreme heroism of the ripening of wisdom.
The six mudrās of wisdom and means
one should observe and examine with certainty.
The first joy is wisdom;
supreme joy is joining in union;
in the joy of bliss is the great accomplishment;
the coemergent joy is unsurpassed.
Union and complete union,
great union and the union of wisdom:
these are the stages of the rite.
If one knows the practice of the system of the drop,
one should observe through the subtle practice.
The cause of the wisdom of the Omniscient,
the state of the dharmadhātu, is attained.
Having accomplished the rite with certainty,
the yogin swiftly gains accomplishment.
Of this alone there is certainty.

From the Supreme of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixty-third chapter, on secret wisdom.

Then, further, this is to be explained:
the supreme heart essence of the mind.
Abiding in the center of the vajra of the sphere of space,
one should meditate on the moon of wisdom.
Arisen from the letter ā
is Vajrasattva, the great bliss,
in color like snow and kunda flowers,
equal to pure crystal.
Three-faced and six-armed, peaceful,
three-eyed, poised in compassion,
he sits in the center of a moon upon a variegated lotus.
Arisen from the wisdom of emptiness,
he binds his matted locks with a half moon,
has a crown ornament of skulls,
and is perfectly complete with every ornament.
He sits in vajra cross-legged posture,
abiding in union with his own body.
Manifest in compassion and passion,
his faces are white, blue and red,
having the nature of the three gates of liberation.
He is joined equally with vajra and bell,
pressing the lips of the goddess.
He holds a vajra, a spear, a skull cup,
a khaṭvāṅga and a skull cup.
With the letter aḥ, seated in the center of a sun,
one should meditate on Vajrasattva.
From i comes Vajracandra;
from ī comes Vajramāyā;
from e comes Dharmavajrī;
from ai comes Viśvamāyā.
These four goddesses
bear the colors of his faces.
With loose hair and large eyes,
intoxicated with compassion and passion,
together with their own consort, the means,
in the center of a moon maṇḍala,
they should be meditated upon above various pretas.
The lord and his wisdom consort, in cross-legged posture,
wearing crown ornaments of garlands of skulls,
adorned with the five mudrās,
joined equally with vajra and bell,
carry spear and curved knife in their hands,
hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
and grant every accomplishment in full.
Just as in the yoga of the hero,
they bear his colors and hand emblems.
The secret union of the four goddesses
is the supreme practice of the heart essence.
In the delight of the supreme secret,
the self of all always abides.
That very thing, the supreme of all secrets,
the supreme heart essence of the ḍākinī,
is the pledge of secret wisdom;
it is the nature of the dharmadhātu.
u is to be meditated upon at the heart,
ū likewise at the head,
o at the crown of the head,
and au is to be placed at the eyes.
oṃ is to be placed as armor,
and aḥ is to be placed as the weapon.
Through joining body, speech and mind,
one abides in meditating on oneself.
Through joining the wisdom being and the pledge being,
one also makes unsurpassed offerings and bestows empowerment.
Through joining in the pledge of sameness,
phenomena likewise are the same.
All things are of one taste:
the supreme nondual is accomplished.
According to the pledge, at the junctures of the sessions,
one should meditate at the four times.
The practice of the four unions
one should contemplate in the four sessions.
With compassion one should go out to all,
and meditate without partiality.
All are Vajrasattva;
wisdom follows without partiality.
Joined with the vajra lord of yoga,
oneself is the lord of the vajra.
It is the bone beyond counting;
it is declared to be the recitation of wisdom.
Recitation is the meditation of wisdom,
the emanating of the six emanations.
Therefore the recitation of the pledge
should be performed according to the rite.
oṃ āḥ a ā i ī u ū e ai o au oṃ aḥ hūṃ svāhā
Having taken a rosary of great conch,
one should always recite the king of secret mantras.
It is a purifier, a great merit,
virtuous, and a destroyer of faults.
It destroys all suffering
and is the fruit of all virtue;
it is the teacher of all secret mantras.
In a place of utter solitude,
arisen from the bliss of delight
of the heart essence of the ḍākinī,
the four goddesses are joy,
and the lord of yoga is supreme bliss.
The bliss of the family is the Blessed One,
the self-arisen Vajrasattva.

From the Supreme of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixty-fourth chapter, on the secret union of the four goddesses.

Then the supreme practice is explained,
which devours all the vicious
and destroys the savage, outer and inner.
From what is like empty space,
manifestly arisen from the letter kṣa,
is Cakrasaṃvara, the Net of Ḍākinīs,
two-armed and one-faced,
three-eyed, with a terrifying face.
Standing with the right leg extended,
he presses down the savage enemy, Māra.
Joined equally with the great mudrā,
he embraces her fully with skull cup and bell.
In the middle of the skull cup
it is marked with a lovely supreme vajra;
filled completely with the nectar of wisdom,
it is placed in the right hand.
His form is of every kind,
and his body is adorned with the six mudrās.
His face is fierce, with fangs bared,
and his crown is ornamented with skulls.
His head is marked with a crossed vajra;
he bears matted locks and a half moon,
and wears the crown ornament of Vajrasattva.
This is the meditation on Vajrabhairava.
With a mind that has abandoned doubt and is completely pure,
one makes no distinction among the stains of conceptual thought.
One's body is adorned with the ornaments of love and compassion;
one is well trained in the yoga of uninhibited bliss.
In a great charnel ground of animals, utterly terrifying,
vetālas and rākṣasas cry out with every kind of sound;
it is made terrible by the sound of pheṃ.
Moreover, the practitioner should practice in a place suited to yoga.
Or else he has four faces,
and here one should meditate on him with eight arms.
White, blue, red and yellow,
his faces are contorted and terrifying.
He holds spear, vajra, khaṭvāṅga,
ḍamaru, head and curved knife;
with various arms, and a moving tongue,
he proclaims the sounds ha hā hi hī huṃ hūṃ and
the one called phaṭ.
The single hero, terrifying,
tames all who are hard to tame.
Through the practice of body, speech and mind,
the wise one sends out clouds of wrathful ones.
In order to accomplish the joy of meditative concentration,
one should practice in the body of the wrathful one.
One should eat the pledge substances and recite;
having made the pledge union, one should recite.
Abiding in equipoise and having recited,
one should meditate on all phenomena without exception.
oṃ akṣa hūṃ phaṭ
It exhausts the misdeeds of the three existences
and exhausts even the five acts of immediate retribution.
It exhausts all suffering
and purifies existences too.
The holy supreme practice
is surely accomplished merely by reading it.
Vicious and harmful beings,
those attached to savage deeds,
and those with wrong views:
these should certainly be devoured.
One sends forth the ornaments of the buddhas' emanations
and liberates the three realms.
The mantra practitioner should recite that supreme mantra
together with the name.
oṃ phaṭ so-and-so bhakṣaya phaṭ kṣāḥ hūṃ phaṭ
With the mind of a wrathful one oneself,
eating meat, and naked,

with loose hair, trampling a corpse,
holding a noose and a hook in the hands,
according to the rite of the secret mantra,
one should recite ten million times and meditate.
According to the rite of yoga,
the three realms will be slain.
With the ḍākinī and her retinue,
one should also visualize them being eaten.
Oneself, as the wrathful one bearing a vajra needle,
should be made to enter the heart.
Tearing apart the heart, the entrails, the marrow and the bones,
the flesh and the blood,
those who commit the five acts of immediate retribution
and the vicious should be eaten.
The ḍāka, seated at the navel,
is joined together with the ḍākinī.
Through the thorough union of the intelligent vajra,
within seven days he will come.
Merely by performing the meditative concentration,
the vicious one will be eaten by me.
At midday and at midnight
one should engage in these actions.
Being naked, with hair let loose,
facing toward Yama's quarter,
by the practice of the rooster seat,
one should recite the secret mantra of fire.
phaṭ hūṃ raṃ raṃ raṃ this one, the perpetrator of immediate retribution, so named, raṃ raṃ raṃ hūṃ phaṭ
Merely by performing the recitation properly,
within fourteen days,
even Vajradhara in person
will die, without doubt.
If the evildoer turns back,
pacifying rites should be performed for him.
Having recited the secret mantra extensively,
by this too he will be pacified.
oṃ svāhā a ā baṃ a mu kaṃ pā pa bī ra ho bha bi ṣḍya ti ta sya si ba śāntiṃ bha bantu baṃ aṃ aḥ oṃ svāhā
Drinking milk and wearing white clothes,
with a peaceful mind one abides in emptiness,
and anoints the body with white scent.
One should eat the flesh of crow, dog, cat
and mongoose.
All becomes thoroughly established;
about this there is no doubt to weigh.

From the Supreme of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixty-fifth chapter, on the union and subjugation of the lord, the great wrathful Vajrabhairava.

Then, further, this is to be explained:
the one of seven births within existence,
the pledge of the various paśus.
Having a divine form, captivating,
having a divine form, majestic and glorious,
he is king among the paśus,
fortunate, lovely, going in bliss.
The shoots of his hair are likewise long;
his eyes are long and his eyes are also red;
his form is like lotus petals.
Fragrant pollen issues from his body;
divine scent issues from his body,
and the breath of his mouth smells of camphor;
because of this, bees always circle him.
His urine and feces smell of musk.
His words are gentle,
and his hands and feet are soft.
He has the fragrance of the utpala,
and is free of white hair and wrinkles.
His body is glossy and supple.
Heart, skull and navel:
therefore he becomes gorocanā.
Casting seven shadows, he walks with the gait of a goose.
He has a compassionate disposition,
always has faith in the Three Jewels,
and possesses generosity and moral discipline.
If the yogin recognizes such a one:
he is not killed and is not burned by fire;
no vicious creature of any kind
whatsoever is able to eat him.
He is not seen at night;
he is the supreme lord of invisibility.
Having recognized such a one, the mantra practitioner
takes him himself and practices giving.
Excrement, water and bodhicitta
the wise one takes and makes into pills.
Wrapped in the three metals,
they should be accomplished by the intelligent one.
Having fully joined the two organs,
one recites the near-heart mantra a hundred thousand times.
Having placed this in his hand:
from this giving, it is given to me.
This one, immediately,
finds the activity of Akṣobhya.
Having taken him to a solitary place,
one should settle in equipoise and practice.

Further actions are to be explained in full,
the holy supreme means of accomplishing siddhis.
Having cut the hair from the crown of his head,
and also the hair of an animal,
mixing them and making them into a thread,
by tying it around the neck of anyone,
that person turns into a form like his,
and also takes on various forms.
Having taken his skin,
one draws glorious Heruka upon it.
The accomplishment of the wrathful one in person
brings about glorious Heruka.
Having taken the skin of the hands and feet,
mixing it with the hair of a bhūta,
and having made it into powdered pills,
one consecrates them with mantras and performs the rite.
The wise one, on the day of victory,
wraps them in the three metals
and takes it with the left hand.
With a fist concealing the thumb,
if that fist is placed on the head,
at once the thief is paralyzed.
With one's own hands one takes both his arms;
taking one's own hair as a noose and holding it,
holding the hook in the right hand,
one wraps it with five threads.
Whatever direction is filled with wealth,
seeing that direction, one should take it.
Having set it in the house and sitting upon it,
having recited the mantra, one travels in the sky.
oṃ jaḥ a ppaṃ ba hūṃ ba hūṃ ba rā na du khe ca ri ja ri hrīḥ svāhā
Wealth and grain and likewise clothing,
whatever the mind desires,
all of it is swiftly summoned,
and the house is seen to be completely full.
Having eaten the meat,
one turns into a divine body.
The yogin becomes a vajra body,
and by this death is brought under control.
Having taken the sinews according to the rite,
and mixed them with hair, one twists them;
having twisted them into thread
according to the rite, one makes them into nooses.
Standing with the right leg extended, abiding in wrath,
with the recitation of someone's name,
if the noose is cast, immediately
the goddess is summoned and stands before one.
With the powder of his bones
the wise one draws a maṇḍala,
and here the accomplishment of the maṇḍala,
whatever one desires, abides in it.
Further, one makes ornaments
and beautifies them with vajra marks.
For worship one holds them with the sinews.
One who keeps the observance of being anointed
with sandalwood ground there
will travel in the sky.
The practitioner, having made eye ointment
from the great sesame oil,
will not be seen, even his shadow.
If the yogin anoints himself with the fat,
the night too becomes like day.
If the yogin eats the marrow,
he is satisfied, and not otherwise;
for as long as he lives he does not hunger,
and his intellect and memory increase.
The yogin remembers his former births
and becomes Vajrasattva.
Having made the entrails into powder,
the wise one dries them in the shade as well.
When they are placed on someone's head,
immediately that person is drawn under one's power.
If the mantra practitioner eats the lungs,
he at once becomes free of sickness.
If the foot is taken and struck on the ground,
it brings down a stream of rain.
The intelligent one takes the secret part and dries it,
mixes it with red sandalwood,
and makes it into powder;
if it is given to any woman,

that woman, tormented by desire,
comes under one's power and does one's bidding.
A knee bone made into a peg,
about twelve finger-widths in length,
hidden away with someone's name,
brings that person's family line to an end.
If the yogin eats the tongue,
he gains the accomplishment of speech.
If the yogin eats the heart,
the mind becomes invincible.
Taking the four kings of the teeth,
above and below,
with the wing of a bee
and powder of tsupma petals:
if the practice is done with this powder,
the moving and the unmoving are bewildered.
With the veins made into powder,
all women are made to wither.
Self-arisen flower, gorocanā,
lac and saffron:
if the mantra practitioner applies them as a tilaka mark,
even a universal monarch comes under his power.
With powder of the yogin's eyeballs,
the enemy army is split apart.
Merely by eating the breasts,
milk is immediately drawn forth.
Merely by eating the yogin's sexual organ,
a woman's seed is drawn forth at once.
With powder of the yogin's teeth and nails,
scattered on the head of whomever one will,
the eight accomplishments are accomplished;
even father and son
are made to hate each other.
By the practice with the lips made into powder,
the mouth is suppressed.
By the practice with the nose made into powder,
the breath is suppressed.
By the practice with the ears, one becomes deaf.
If one binds on the skin of the head
and recites before the mouth,
one becomes chief of all the gods.
By the practice with the skull,
blood is drawn forth.
By turning the skull mouth downward,
only harm is done.
Indravāru and gorocanā:
having mixed the two together,
whoever has a tilaka mark made of it
will see the abode of Indra.
By anointing with gorocanā
together with nyangtré,
one gains invincible strength
and overcomes adversaries.
Putrañjali and gorocanā
made into powder together:
on whatever woman the powder is scattered,
she becomes intoxicated with desire
and remains for as long as she lives.
By anointing with gorocanā
and putra together,
all the bhūtas, male and female,
saying, "What shall we do?",
will come to sit before him
and will grant whatever he desires.
Gorocanā and jīvantī
made into powder together,
combined with a dead man's eye and eye ointment:
one will live
for as long as the eon endures.
Makara's hand and gorocanā
made into powder together,
made into pills
together with nāgamado,
and mixed with vajra splendor:
if this is made into an eye medicine,
even the elephant of the Wealth Guardian
will appear in person,
and if one rides upon it,
with a great retinue of elephants,
one becomes invincible to others.
One destroys the enemy army,
gains power over the lands of others,
and is granted whatever one desires.
Through the practice of brahmadaṇḍī
together with gorocanā,
there will be no challenger in the Vedas.
The gorocanā of peacock bile
together with hare's blood:
if pills are always prepared from it
and bound with a peacock tail feather,
one flies like a bee,
gains the accomplishment of summoning the gods,
and the vidyādharas will come.
For a mere trifle in return,
they will grant whatever one desires.
One carries off the celestial palaces of the gods,
and the daughters of the gods too are summoned.
If one wishes, they remain under one's power;
if one does not wish it, one works for the benefit of beings.
If the tail feather is turned downward,
treasures too will be seen.
By taking them, the perfection of generosity
becomes completely fulfilled.
At the doors of the treasures beneath the earth,
if one circles them with the tail feather,
one should enter by opening the doors.
One sports together with the daughters of the asuras,
and takes the essence and the elixir of the gods.
Having seized it and eaten it,
one turns into the form of a god of desire.
By taking the substances of accomplishment,
one goes about working for the benefit of beings.
Then, as for the rite
of the five limbs:
having taken hair, skin, flesh,
bone and marrow,
one should make pills from them together with
excrement, water, semen
and vermilion, and practice.
Wrapped in the three metals,
if they are hidden inside the mouth,
one will travel through the sky.
Together with gorocanā,
one becomes a great king in the three realms.

From the Supreme of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixty-sixth chapter, on accomplishing the great paśu of seven births.

Then, further, this is to be explained:
meditating on the mind of one's own dharma,
joining it to the letters of one's own dharma,
one should meditate correctly on the selfless dharma.
The letters of one's own dharma are supreme joy;
the dharmakāya body is self-arisen.
Certainly arisen from the letter he,
Heruka is to be meditated upon.
Certainly arisen from the letter hi,
Vajraḍāka is to be meditated upon.
Arisen from the wisdom of hūṃ,
Vajrahūṃkāra is to be meditated upon.
Arisen from the letter hūṃ,
Akṣobhya is to be meditated upon.
Generated from the wisdom of baṃ,
Vajrasattva is to be meditated upon.
Arisen from oṃ bhūṃ oṃ bhūṃ,
Buddhaḍāka is to be meditated upon.
Arisen from aṃ trāṃ raṃ sva,
Ratnaḍāka is to be meditated upon.
Certainly arising from hriṃ hriṃ a,
the lotus ḍāka is to be meditated upon.
Arisen from khaṃ ho ha haṃ,
Viśvaḍāka is to be meditated upon.
From ḍā comes the ḍākinī herself;
from yaṃ likewise the yoginī;
from bhrūṃ the goddess who moves on the earth;
from baṃ the one who moves in the sky;
from maṃ the mātṛkā herself,
the consort of the hero, the renowned one.
he is the lord of mind;
from ru speech arises;
ka is the vajra of body:
Heruka arises from the three syllables.
From maṃ comes Mañjuvajra;
from saṃ comes Samantabhadra;
from laṃ comes Lokanātha;
from kṣiṃ comes Kṣitigarbha;
from baṃ comes Vajrapāṇi;
from meṃ comes Maitreya himself;
from khaṃ comes the one renowned as Ākāśagarbha;
from gaṃ comes Gaganagañja.

From saṃ comes Sarvanivaraṇaviṣkambhin, the Remover of All Obscurations;
from gaṃ comes Gandhahastin;
from jñāṃ comes Jñānaketu as well;
from aṃ comes Akṣayamati;
from praṃ comes Pratibhānakūṭa;
from saṃ comes Sarvāpāyañjaha, who leads beings out of all the lower realms;
from saṃ comes the Destroyer of All Lower Realms;
from jaṃ comes Jālinīprabha;
from caṃ comes Candraprabha;
arising from raṃ is Ratnaprabha;
from sūṃ comes Sūryaprabha;
from baṃ comes Vajraprabha as well;
from maṃ comes Mahāsthāmaprāpta;
suṃ is Sudhana;
from baṃ comes Bhadrapāla himself;
from saṃ comes Sāgaramati.

Samantabhadra, Mañjughoṣa,
Lokanātha, Kṣitigarbha,
Vajrapāṇi and Maitreya,
Ākāśagarbha, Gaganagañja,
Sarvanivaraṇaviṣkambhin,
and Gandhahastin himself;
Akṣayamati and Jñānaketu,
Pratibhānakūṭa the supreme,
and the Destroyer of All Sorrow himself;
Sarvāpāyañjaha himself, and
Jālinīprabha, Candraprabha, Ratnaprabha,
Sūryaprabha, and Vajraprabha the supreme;
Sudhana and the Jewel-Attainer,
Sāgaramati and Bhadrapāla:
these are the ones accomplished of old.
Their garland secret mantras should be arranged.
They are taught as the pledge,
and they grant the accomplishment of siddhi.

Vajradūtī herself,
the Inciter, and Ratnolkā,
Vajrasūryā likewise,
Vetālī, Vajrakumārī,
Kampoji likewise,
Buddhabodhī likewise,
Jñānolkā and Vajrabhairavī,
Jñānolkā and Jayakarī,
Tārā and Pāṇḍaravāsinī,
Locanā and the supreme Māmakī,
Dhvajā, Pratisarā,
Mayūrī and Śabarī,
Śṛṅkhalā and Pratyaṅgirā,
the goddess Uṣṇīṣā and Vajrabhairavī:
these are the goddesses accomplished of old,
who grant every fruit one desires.
Together with the manifesting syllable and the name,
one should add hūṃ phaṭ at the end;
at the very end join svāhā as well.
Then they fully grant the accomplishment of the mantra.

Yamāri and Paramāśva the Victor
are those who subdue the obstructors;
these four, with Prajñāntaka,
accomplish every aim one desires.
Ṭakkirāja, Nīladaṇḍa, Mahābala,
and eighth, the renowned Acala:
the great wrathful ones, accomplished of old,
surely subdue all who are vicious.
Gaurī and the other pledge goddesses
are the eight goddesses of the Blood-Drinker.
The ḍākinīs explained before
abide nondual with the eight wrathful ones.
The mantras endowed with the eight root syllables
should be properly joined with the names.
oṃ is the manifesting syllable in every case,
and at the end join hūṃ phaṭ svāhār.
The manifesting syllable, the name and hūṃ,
and at the end join phaṭ and svāhā.

The goddesses of form and sound,
and the goddesses of smell and taste,
are to be arranged on the four petals.
Sky-clad, with hair let loose,
she who is renowned as Sparśavajrā, the Vajra of Touch, through her touch,
abides truly nondual.
In the center the king, the Blood-Drinker,
abides in bliss through the goddess's touch.
The essence is renowned as the dharmadhātu;
it arises from the perfection of wisdom.
The wisdom being abides nondually;
she is Vajradharmadhātvīśvarī.
The nondual wisdom of equality
is that dharmadhātu, which cannot be apprehended.
That is the supreme secret reality,
which the Vajra Holder truly bestows.

The supreme of greatness is the Samāja;
Vajrasattva is the supreme of bliss;
the one that follows the Yoga Tantra;
the Yoginī of Going and Not Going;
the Ocean of Knowledge is supreme;
Vajraḍāka, a little;
it is the Māyāsaṃvara Tantra;
the Tantra of the Arising of Yoginīs;
it is the Secret Ocean Tantra;
the Abhidhāna Tantra is unsurpassed;
the supreme Samāja is the explanation.
According to the particular dispositions of beings,
as a cause for taking sentient beings into its care,
this sublime supreme Samāja
bestows the fruit of a buddha's awakening.
The families are taught to be of five kinds.
Of the many bodhisattvas,
whichever one one wishes
should be made the presiding lord.
In accordance with the names,
following the stages of the Abhidhāna Tantra,
the vajra practitioner
should perform the rites of the Abhidhāna Tantra.
Abandoning every doubt in the mind,
this meditation of nonduality,
sublime and supreme in the Great Vehicle,
leads one to possess great siddhi.

Next the supreme hand emblems shall be explained,
the sequence of the rite of form and color.
Emerging from the letter he,
one should meditate on oneself as Heruka.
His body is white and very fierce,
terrifying with bared fangs;
he has three eyes and five faces,
six arms and four legs.
He wears a crown of a garland of skulls;
his matted locks are bound with a half moon on his head;
he wears a crown ornament of a crossed vajra;
his body is adorned with the six seals.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment
and is adorned with a necklace of heads.
White, yellow and likewise red,
green, and the upper face blue;
laughing, heroic and amorous,
furious, peaceful and wrathful.
Holding vajra and bell together,
he abides nondual through the vajra touch.
He also holds the supreme hide of the Victor
and bestows the fruit of every bliss.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga staff,
with a vajra spear in a supreme hand.
Standing with the right leg extended,
he presses the four māras beneath his feet.

Sparśavajrā is green,
with a form of white radiance.
Her faces are green, yellow and red,
and her left face is white;
her upper face is blue and terrifying.
Her hair is let loose; she is intoxicated;
she bears the seal of the lion's release;
her two thighs are closely entwined.
She wears a crown of a garland of skulls;
her three eyes are deep red.
She is joined with vajra and skull cup,
adorned with a bell and a ḍamaru drum,
and holds a spear and a khaṭvāṅga in her hands.
Of great passion, with the radiance of fire,
sky-clad, in the mood of peace,
she tames all who are hard to tame
and tames sentient beings of every kind;
the mood of compassion is wholly complete in her.
At the center of moon and sun,
she sits in the heart of a multicolored lotus.
Bhairava and Kālarātri
one should visualize as pressed beneath their feet.

All the retinue have three faces
and wear crowns of garlands of skulls.
They are fierce and yet peaceful,
their forms distinguished by the three families,
and they hold the emblems of the three families.
Those whose forms are distinguished as goddesses
are joined in the nondual pledge.
All of them sit on seats of pretas,
pressing down with the right leg extended;
their two thighs are closely entwined.
One should meditate on wisdom and means.
In the intermediate directions are vases
and skull cups brimming with the moon.
At the corners are the gatekeepers,
each according to its share of color.

They stand with the right leg extended,
pressing the world protectors beneath their feet.
They wear crowns of garlands of skulls,
intoxicated with compassion and wrath.
The colors, white and the rest, are assigned
in the order of the right-hand faces,
and the order of the seals is likewise.
In forward order, and in the order reversed,
the retinue and the rest, the supreme ones, are to be placed.
In the center of the vajra lotus,
in the midst of the three family wheels,
is the supreme one who holds his own blessing.
The buddhas keep it hidden from the tīrthikas.
This is utterly secret;
it rests in the words of the guru;
it is the kindness of the yoginīs.
Arranging the sequence of the sacred places and the rest,
one should set out the thirty-seven purities.
It is a being whose self is wisdom and means;
it draws in even the supreme of the tenth ground;
it abides in the ten perfections;
it is the purified meaning of the three unions.
This is the stainless supreme meditation.

This concludes the sixty-seventh chapter of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high: the chapter on the meditation of the blessing in which oneself is generated from the letters of the Dharma.

Next I shall explain in the highest way
the sequence of meditation on oneself in the rite.
Māmakī and Locanā,
the goddess Tārā, Pāṇḍaravāsinī,
Marīcī and Jambhala,
the goddess the Inciter, and Dharaṇī,
arise from their own seed syllables.
They are adorned with twelve arms
and have three eyes; they stand with the right leg extended;
they sit in the posture of the sattva;
they abide in the vajra posture.
They have the faces of the four Vedas,
are sky-clad, and sit on corpse seats.
With hair let loose, they are terrifying;
they wear crowns of garlands of skulls;
they gaze with wrath, yet in the mood of compassion.
The goddess Prajñāpāramitā
arises from the supreme wisdom of reality.
She is the very self of each perfect union:
this, without doubt, is the wisdom of reality.
The wrathful ones of various forms
arise from the hero himself.
The supreme wrathful ones and the arising ḍākinīs as well,
unstained, in their moods,
are to be joined to him as their lord.
This is the meditation on oneself.
Through the practice outside of meditative equipoise as well,
one should meditate and contemplate.

The subtle wrathful Vajra Holder,
in the center of the lotus at the heart,
Vajraḍāka, the loving one,
makes his dwelling right here.
All the perfect buddhas, the mind of awakening,
and the three kinds of beings abide in this.
By worshiping this very one,
all who are worthy of worship are worshiped.
Whoever constantly looks upon this,
the Victor regards as one worshiped.
Through the vajra of inner union,
the yogi is held to be a vajra holder.
Therefore, through vajra worship,
what the mind longs for is sought within the body.
What the tantras teach,
that by the practice of the five offerings
one swiftly attains buddhahood,
is held to be something other, external.
The placing upon the four insubstantials,
the meditation in the form of the deity,
is the great worship of sameness,
which fully accomplishes all the buddhas.

A tantra that comes about well
merely as a teaching that shows mantras and reality
is not the ultimate.
When the ultimate truly arises,
there is no teacher and no teaching.
The sacred places and the rest, divided into twenty-four,
abide right here in this.
Therefore wandering among them
is wearisome, and not for one who has reality.
If reality is lacking,
what will wandering in error accomplish?
And if reality is present,
what error is there in it?
The supreme sacred places and secondary sacred places
are made stainless by being taught.
Or else, for incapable and careless people,
the places of men should be proclaimed.
What I have explained in this way
is by force of the dispositions of sentient beings.
Since there is no bliss other than meditative concentration,
one should therefore devote oneself to supreme meditative concentration.
The yogi who wanders outside
exhausts his bodily elements.
Therefore, abandoning the external,
one should abide in the play of yoga.

The glorious one of vajra yoga,
with eyes of pure wisdom,
his heart long pained with compassion,
looks upon those who practice vajra yoga.
Being pleased with them, he
grants the supreme siddhi, whatever they desire.
Therefore one should strive with great effort
to accomplish the noble lady Wisdom.
Endowed with various conduct,
having practiced conduct and austerities,
here abandon conduct and austerities,
and take delight in the austerity of reality.
Living on whatever food there is,
the one who keeps the austerity in that way,
being devoted to meditative concentration as supreme food,
should live by the conduct of a madman.
Naked or not naked,
whichever it may be, so it is.
Focusing on one's own chosen deity,
one should contemplate that alone.
Having become as one who is mute,
if the yogi practices thus
for about six months, then certainly
this great yoginī will become clear.
When one sees the supreme Jñānolkā,
the yoginīs gather together well,
just as flocks of geese gather
at a delightful lotus pond.
Like a magician in the world,
one works for the good of all beings.
One will become manifest as glorious Vajradhara:
of this there is no doubt.
"So that through this conduct it will surely
come about just as has been explained,
purify your minds!"
This I implore of sentient beings.
Whoever possesses marvelous compassion,
whatever he speaks comes true.

Through the practice of the fierce vajra,
one should place the ḍākinī at the heart.
At the heart, apply the vajra hook;
join it within by the vajra of mind.
Further, the vajra that is to be placed
should be examined within the dharmadhātu.
Having joined oneself to the sky,
meditate on holding a form of splendor.
Placing one's own mind of Dharma,
one's own warmth, one's nature, stainless,
through the practice of one's own meditation,
meditate on one's own letters of Dharma.
In meditating on the form of another,
according to whatever one has realized,
one should not meditate on some other lord
produced by the mind's delusion.
According to whatever one's inclination,
one should look upon the form of another.

This concludes the sixty-eighth chapter of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high: the chapter on the worship that tames oneself.

Then all the blessed tathāgatas spoke this secret, the secret wisdom.

"Phenomena, by their nature selfless,
are stainless like the sky.
With the vajra of the wisdom of emptiness,
one should meditate on emptiness always."

Having given their cries of "Excellent!",
from the union in which the tantra was born, they said:
"Excellent, excellent, great wisdom,
the Abhidhāna, supreme of the supreme!
Excellent, fierce mode of the vajra,
the highest kindness of a buddha's awakening!
Excellent, the union of nonduality!
For the accomplishment of the supreme and the supreme,
'Excellent!' is surely to be applied.
The meditation free of desire,
excellent, protector of all beings!
This teaching of the Great Vehicle,
the supreme nondual wisdom,
the first of those who have received the instructions,
excellent, all vajra gurus!
The yoginī taught to them,
excellent! To the maṇḍala of the assembly
she has granted the unsurpassed siddhi."
Having finished, they fell silent.

When the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, had spoken thus, the wrathful vajras of all the tathāgatas praised what the Blessed One had taught, the unsurpassed Abhidhāna Tantra, the Saṃvara of the Net of Ḍākinīs.

This concludes the sixty-ninth chapter of the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high, called the Secret of Secrets, the Great King of Tantras of Cakrasaṃvara: the chapter teaching meditation on reality itself, free of doubt.

This completes the Great King of Tantras of Cakrasaṃvara, the Secret of Secrets, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high.

It was translated, edited and finalized by the Indian preceptor, the great paṇḍita Dīpaṃkaraśrījñāna, and the great Tibetan translator, the monk Rinchen Zangpo. Later it was also edited by the great paṇḍita Jñānaśrī and the translator Khyungpo Chökyi Tsöndrü. Later still, the paṇḍita Ānanda and Lochung supplied what was missing, removed what was superfluous, proofread it, corrected it well, and finalized it. There is nothing more profound than this.

Here is contained volume Kha of the Tantra Collection.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

In "the abode in the bhaga where existence, sound and form arise," the Tibetan སིད is read as སྲིད ("existence"); the line remains obscure. The verse beginning "the supreme ones, the breath-releasing and the intermediate" uses coded language for ritual substances, and its exact referents are uncertain. Names of the heroes are given in their standard Cakrasaṃvara forms, which the Tibetan renders: ཐོད་པའི་དུམ་བུ Khaṇḍakapāla, ཀེང་རུས Kaṅkāla, མཆེ་བ་རྣམ་པར་གཙིགས་པ Vikaṭadaṃṣṭra, ལྷ་དགྲ Suravairin, མྱུ་གུ Aṅkuraka, རལ་པ་ཅན Jaṭila, and so on.  The names of the yoginīs, ḍākinīs and mātṛkās are given in their standard Sanskrit forms from the Cakrasaṃvara cycle, which the Tibetan renders; the first four goddesses (ལྟུང་བྱེད་མ, གསོད་བྱེད་མ, འགུགས་བྱེད་མ, and the like) are reconstructed from their meanings as Pātanī, Māraṇī, Ākarṣaṇī and Narteśvarī, which is likely but not certain. The mātṛkā written གཤིན་རྗེ་བསྲེག་མ ("Yama burner") is taken as Yamadāḍhī (also spelled Yamadāhī). དབང་ཆེན ("Mahendra") here names the earth maṇḍala in the stack of elemental maṇḍalas.  The syllables after the statement on the charnel grounds are, in the Cakrasaṃvara tradition, the initial syllables of the twenty-four sacred places (pīṭhas), which the following verses call "the twenty-four." The spirit class grib gnon is rendered literally as "shadow-oppressing spirits," as its Sanskrit equivalent is not certain. In the verse on liberation, the text reads tshos ("dye, color"), which makes little sense; it has been read as tshes ("lunar day"). In the line "arose from the exalted Heruka," mngon mthe is unclear as written and has been read as mngon mtho, "exalted"; this is uncertain.  "She Who Kisses" renders 'o byed ma literally; in this list of the elements she corresponds to space, but her Sanskrit name cannot be identified with confidence. In the line "When they are painted," the text reads 'dri ("ask"), which makes little sense here; it has been read as 'bri ("paint, draw"). Paśu, "the beast," is the tantric term for the unawakened being bound by fetters, here the prospective disciple whose characteristics the mantrin examines. "The five mudrās" are the bone ornaments of the charnel-ground yogin.  In the line "I shall make offerings and praise them," the text reads rjed ("forget"), which makes no sense here; it has been read as brjod ("praise"). In the list of the six senses, "awareness" (rig pa) stands in the place of the body or the sense of touch.
