"The four abodes of Brahmā" are the four immeasurables (brahmavihāras): loving-kindness, compassion, joy and equanimity.  "Hearth Union" renders thab sbyor literally; this member of the eight wrathful ones cannot be identified with confidence. In the list of the eight wrathful ones, rdo rje nag po stobs po che has been taken as two figures, Vajrakāla and Mahābala, which gives the eight names that match the eight colors listed next.  Māṃsa, śukra and rakta are flesh, semen and blood, among the substances of the tantric pill. The four-line verses in long meter are dense; "the steeds" (rta) is taken to refer to the winds, the mounts of mind, and this reading is tentative. The mantra list names Kuṇḍali as the fifth wrathful one, which suggests that the figure rendered literally as "Hearth Union" (thab sbyor) in the previous passage is Vajrakuṇḍali.  The six sense-vajra deities (the Vajras of Delusion, Aversion, Jealousy, Passion, Avarice, and Lordship) are rendered by meaning rather than with reconstructed Sanskrit names. The colors of the five elements are given as the Tibetan lists them (yellow, blue, red, smoky, blue). "Twofold form" for the goddesses of the intermediate directions renders gzugs gnyis pa literally; its precise sense is unclear. Khaṇḍarohā appears both on the western petal and in the north as the text stands.  The lines on the womb-born and song are obscure; the rendering "some yogis wish the womb-born to be brought under the sway of song" is tentative. "The five deathless wisdoms" likely refers to the five nectars. The term rendered "sacred thread" is the older Tibetan equivalent of yajñopavīta.  "Vajra water" (rdo rje chu) here denotes urine, paired with excrement as pledge substances. The color list after the six deities names seven colors for six figures, as the text stands. The verse beginning "The supreme, all the middling" is obscure and its rendering tentative. Śyāmā (sngo bsangs ma) and Khagānanā (bya gdong ma) are identified from the parallel Cakrasaṃvara maṇḍala and the mantras that follow.  In the pledge-substance list, "vajra water" and "Vairocana" are coded terms, generally understood as urine and excrement. The Sanskrit names of the mind-wheel heroes, goddesses and sacred places are identified from the parallel Cakrasaṃvara maṇḍala.  The goddess whose exalted state is attained through one-pointed meditation is named bdag med ma, Nairātmyā, "the Selfless One."  "By merely kissing your mouth" renders ཞལ་ནི་ཁུམས་ཙམ, whose verb ཁུམས is uncommon; the sense of kissing is inferred from context. In "the opened supreme mouth of the lotus," བྱེ་བ is taken as "opened" rather than the number ten million (koṭi), which is also possible. In the description of Vārāhī, བསམས་པ ("imagined") is read as བསྣམས་པ ("holding"), which fits the context. The colors of Kākāsyā and the others are listed as the Tibetan gives them; how they are distributed among the four goddesses is not stated.  Pātanī, Māraṇī and Ākarṣaṇī are standard Sanskrit forms of the first three goddesses; གར་མ ("dancer") is rendered Nartakī, a form inferred from its meaning. In the opening of the eighth chapter, རེག་བྱ "the tangible" is given literally; it may refer to the class of stop consonants (sparśa) in the syllable arrangement, which the text does not make explicit. In the sixth left hand, དྲག་པོའི་མགོ་བོ is taken as "the head of Rudra"; it could also mean simply "a fierce one's head." The text has the embracing hands hold "skull cup and bell" where vajra and bell would be usual; this is translated as written.  Santrāsanī is written སྐྲ་ག་བྱེད་མ, an evident slip for སྐྲག་བྱེད་མ. At the throat the male deity is given as རྡོ་རྗེ་འོད, Vajraprabha, the same name as the deity at the eyes; this is translated as written. The terms "sites," "secondary sites," "fields," "secondary fields" and "chandohas" render the classes of sacred places (pīṭha, upapīṭha, kṣetra, upakṣetra, chandoha).  Two slips are read as evident: ཁལ for ཁ་ལ "at the mouth," and སྐྱོད་བྱེད་མ for གཡོ་བྱེད་མ, Sañcālinī. The faculty of faith is assigned to བརྟན་པའི་བློ་ཆེན་མ, here identified with Vīramatī, the goddess of the corresponding place, though the Tibetan wording differs from her usual name. The awakening factor of diligence is assigned to ཁྭ་གདོང་མ, Kākāsyā, as written, although in this sequence Khagānanā would be expected. The "great conch" rosary (དུང་ཆེན) is in tantric usage commonly understood as one made of human bone. In the sequence of places, Khaṇḍarohā appears beside Vajraratna, sharing her name with the goddess of the inner circle, as the text has it.  The goddess of right speech is written དཔའ་མོ་ཆེན་མོ, here identified with Mahāvīryā, the goddess of the knees in the preceding list, whose name there is written བརྩོན་འགྲུས་ཆེན་མོ. In "liberated from dharma and from what is not dharma," the Tibetan reads ཆོས་དང་ཆོས་ལས, literally "from dharma and from dharma"; the second is taken in the sense of its opposite, as in the parallel phrase "abandoning dharma and non-dharma" earlier in the text, but this is uncertain. The verses on the womb-born and egg-born modes of meditation are terse, and several lines (for example "the laughter of separation" and "the egg-born is together with a thing") are rendered literally. The repetition of the lines on abandoning song is in the Tibetan and is kept.  "The thirty" (sum cu pa) is taken literally; it may refer to the gods of the Thirty-Three, here set beside the classes moving on and beneath the earth. "Sipika" (si pi ka), apparently a charnel-ground creature, is left in its Tibetan spelling because it cannot be identified with confidence. "Severed parts" renders the obscure rje ngar, and "charred remains" the obscure rag rog; both are uncertain. "Clad in the directions" means naked (digambara). The five kinds of hungry ghosts encircling the outer maṇḍala are kept as written. The title in the colophon reads nges par brjod pa ("discourse") where the opening has mngon par brjod pa (Abhidhāna); it is translated as it stands.  "The Great Lord over all the tathāgatas" renders dbang phyug chen po here as an epithet of the presiding buddha, not the god Śiva. The verbs gsungs in the petition are read as honorific imperatives ("speak"). The word gha ti in the offering verse is unidentified and left as written. Ser ba in the description of the faces is the color yellow, not hail. Bsgral ba at the start of the final section is taken in its general sense "released, delivered", since the context is not a wrathful rite; the sense of the line is uncertain.  The three colors given for each pair are presumably those of the hero's three faces; the text simply lists them. Ser ba here is the color yellow, not hail. The hero paired with Suvīrā in Nagara is written khrag 'thung ma, with a feminine ending; it is taken as Heruka (khrag 'thung, "blood drinker"), whose place this normally is, but the ending is unexplained.  "Great conch" (dung chen, Skt. mahāśaṅkha) in tantric usage commonly denotes human bone; here it describes the material of the rosary. "The four acts" by which the wisdom wheel is blessed are not specified in the text; they are probably the four stages of summoning, drawing in, binding and delighting. The words gsungs pa after the assembly's request are read as introducing their own verses of praise.  Several pairs here differ from the previous chapter's list and are translated as they stand: in Lampāka the pair is Vajrabhadra and Subhadrā (shin tu bzang mo), and Mālava's hero is rdo rje'i sku, taken as the same Vajradeha. Gshin rje sreg ma is rendered Yamadāhī ("Yama-burner"), the head of the four Yama goddesses. "Of half form" (gzugs phyed pa) for the four goddesses of the intermediate quarters is literal; its exact sense is uncertain. The sentence on the donkey, camel, goat and cat faces is translated as an independent statement; the text does not say plainly which goddesses bear these faces.  "The maṇḍala of pride" renders snyems pa, whose exact sense here (pride, exultation) is uncertain. "The egg-born" (sgong skyes) may refer to birds, or to the "twice-born"; the intended referent of this line is unclear. "Rosary of human bone" renders dung chen, literally "great conch," a standard tantric term for human bone.  "The family of permanence" renders rtag pa'i rigs literally; in the sequence of four families given to Māmakī, Locanā, Tārā and Pāṇḍaravāsinī it presumably corresponds to the tathāgata family, the place where Locanā is usually assigned. "Half-female Lord" and "half-female sovereign ladies" render phyed ni bud med dbang phyug, evidently alluding to the androgynous Ardhanārīśvara form.  In the list of body sites the Tibetan gives Vīramatī (as "steadfast mind" at Godāvarī and "mind of a hero" at Nagara) where the earlier list placed Vīramatī at Godāvarī and Suvīrā at Nagara; the text is followed as it stands. "Menstrual fluid" renders rdul (rajas). The element goddesses and the aggregate deities are given their standard Sanskrit names (Pātanī, Māraṇī, Ākarṣaṇī, Narteśvarī, Padmajālinī; Vajrasūrya, Narteśvara, Vajrarāga), which the Tibetan renders by meaning.  "The thirty-seven" are presumably the thirty-seven factors conducive to awakening. "The wound-born" (rma skyes) is a covert term, evidently for blood. The verses on eating one who died by violence, slaughter, and union with mother, sister and daughter belong to the deliberately transgressive coded language of the yoginī tantras, which the commentarial tradition reads esoterically; they are translated as they stand.  "Bhavagī" transcribes the Tibetan bha ba gi, a name for the western gate goddess that cannot be identified with confidence. "The earth-holding goddess" (sa 'dzin lha mo) is translated literally, as her name is not securely identifiable. "Śrī Heruka" renders dpal khrag 'thung, "glorious blood-drinker."  "Moving and unmoving" renders rgyu dang mi rgyu (Skt. carācara, the animate and inanimate world), not "cause." The verb bsngam in "held firm the vital breath" is uncertain; it is read in the sense of restraining the breath in prāṇāyāma. "bsgom sa nas" is read as bsgoms nas, "having meditated," and "phyag rgyal" as phyag rgya la, "with the mudrā," as evident copyists' slips.  The color term nam mkha' ser skya, recurring for every third pair of the twenty-four, is rendered "pale yellow like the sky"; its exact shade is uncertain. "Clad in the directions" means naked. The text as it stands gives both legs of Heruka as extended; this is translated as written. Pullīramalaya is written Pullīramaya in this text.  "Clad in the directions" means naked. The goddess at Godāvarī is here written brtan pa'i blo can ma, "steady-minded," rather than the earlier dpa' ba'i blo gros ma; she is kept as Vīramatī for consistency. In "unwavering, they are joined in the same way," mi g.yo ba might also be read as the deity Acala; the plain sense is taken. The body part skra mtshams, "hairline," is translated literally.  "With the boundary or with mantra" renders tsham ngam sngags, read as mtshams sam sngags; the first word is uncertain. The figure for right speech is here called dpa' bo chen mo; she is kept as Mahāvīryā, the goddess of Kulatā, for consistency.  "Making the index fingers a spear" follows the text as written (mdung); the parallel mudrā earlier reads mdud, "knotting." "The three realities" renders de nyid gsum. "One's own special deity" renders rang lhag lha, which is uncertain.  "Khaṇḍakapāla" here renders thod pa'i dum ("skull fragment"), taken as the hero Khaṇḍakapāla of the Cakrasaṃvara maṇḍala, who is paired with Caṇḍākṣī's circle; the Tibetan says literally "his skull-fragment and the others." The goddess gshin rje sreg ("Yama-burner") is given as Yamadāhī; she is the same corner goddess elsewhere called Yamadāḍhī. "Marrow" renders rkang, which could also mean "feet" or "bones"; marrow fits a filled skull cup best. The line "the three wheels are sixteen" is translated literally.  "The Great Terrifier" renders 'jigs pa chen po, probably an epithet of Mahābhairava, to whose contorted, wrathful appearance the hero's is compared. The line "gazing with the names of passion" is translated literally; its sense is obscure. Where the sixteenth chapter says "display the wheel of Dharma," the Tibetan has bstang, read as bstan ("display, teach").  The hero and heroine names are those of the twenty-four pairs of the Cakrasaṃvara maṇḍala. The Tibetan rta rnam in the speech wheel's northwest is read as rta rna, Hayakarṇā. In the body wheel's southeast, "the Blood Drinker" (khrag 'thung) is the hero usually called Heruka or Śrī Heruka. The three colors given for each figure are presumably the colors of its three faces.  "The scattering and the drinking" renders 'thor 'thung literally; the exact ritual acts meant are not certain. "Lip is pressed tightly to lip" takes the obscure pi di dam as describing a close kiss. "The life breath and its control" renders srog dang rtsol ba, the Tibetan equivalent of prāṇāyāma.  "The Youth Who Scatters All" translates kun 'gyed gzhon nu, a deity in the list of trampled gods whom I cannot identify with confidence. "Excrement and water" renders dri chen chu; "water" may stand for urine, as in the lists of the five nectars. "The five lamps" is a standard tantric code for the five meats or substances. The last line, sa 'dzin thams cad, is taken as "all those who hold the grounds"; it could also mean "all the mountains."  "Beasts" renders ཕྱུགས (Skt. paśu), the tantric term for those deemed fit for ritual liberation, here defined as enemies of the teaching. The line སྐྱེ་བ་ཅན་ནི་བཤར་གྲགས is a syllable short; བཤར is read as བཤད ("declared"). In བྱ་རྣམས་ལས་ནི་དང་སྒྱུར, དང is read as ངང ("goose"). "In accordance with the sūtra" renders མདོ་ལ, whose sense here is uncertain. The "one of great mantra" (སྔགས་ཆེན་པོ) and the kaṅbuka bird (ཀང་བུ་ཀ) could not be identified with confidence. "The taking of the essence" is the elixir practice (rasāyana). "The supreme vow" may also allude to Saṃvara.  གནོད་མཛེས is taken as Sumbha (Sumbharāja), the wrathful deity whose mantra appears in the preceding chapter. The "self-arisen flower" and "elephant flower" are coded names for ritual substances, conventionally menstrual blood and an elephant's ichor; the "awakening mind" here is the seminal substance. "Rocana" is the yellow pigment gorocanā. "Inside a head" may mean a skull.  "The wisdom being" and "the commitment being" render ཡེ་ཤེས་སེམས་དཔའ and དམ་ཚིག་སེམས་དཔའ (jñānasattva and samayasattva). In the list of syllables, ཧཱུྃ་ཧཱུྃ་དག་ནི་འཁོར་བའོ is rendered "saṃsāra"; in this list of ritual functions it may instead mean "circling" or "surrounding."  སྐུལ་བྱེད་མ ("the Inciting Goddess") could not be identified with confidence and is translated by meaning. The line "made proud by that" (དྲེགས་པར་བྱས་པ) probably refers to the divine pride of identifying with the deity meditated upon.  The grounds named are the first six bodhisattva grounds: Very Joyful (Pramuditā), Stainless (Vimalā), Luminous (Prabhākarī), Radiant (Arciṣmatī), Difficult to Master (Sudurjayā), and Manifest (Abhimukhī). "The family of the Eternal" (རྟག་པའི་རིགས) and Vajrasūrya (རྡོ་རྗེ་ཉི་མ) are given as written; which tathāgatas they stand for here is not certain. "The food of recitation" renders བཟླ་བའི་ཟས as written; it may be a slip for ཟླ་བའི་ཟས, "the food of the moon." Place names are given in their standard Sanskrit forms (the Tibetan has ཨོ་ཏྲེ for Oḍra). Goddess names not yet met in this text are identified from the standard Cakrasaṃvara pairs: Vīramatī, Laṅkeśvarī, Hayakarṇā, Suvīrā, Mahābalā, Mahāvīryā.  ཕྱེད་ནི་བུད་མེད་དབང་ཕྱུག is read as a rendering of Ardhanārīśvara, the half-female form of Śiva. སྩོལ་བ in སྲོག་དང་སྩོལ་བ is read as རྩོལ་བ, giving the standard term for breath control (prāṇāyāma). ཟླ་དྲུང is read as ཟླ་དྲུག, "six months". ཇི་སྲིད in the list of emblems is unclear and is rendered loosely as "in their order". The four grounds named (Gone Far, Unmoving, Good Intelligence, Cloud of Dharma) are the seventh through tenth bodhisattva grounds.  "The Victor's hide" renders རྒྱལ་བའི་པགས་པ literally; it probably reflects a misreading of Sanskrit gajājina, "elephant hide", the hide Heruka holds aloft. In the list of colors the second yoginī is called སྐུལ་བྱེད་མ, "Urger", where the list of positions has Caurī; she is taken to be Caurī. སྦོས་བྱེད་མ / སྤོས་བྱེད་མ is identified with Ghasmarī, the fifth of the standard eight, but this is not certain. ཚམ་ངམ in the gestures of the speech-wheel goddesses could not be identified and is rendered only as "gestures of the hands". The text writes ཚནྡོ and ཚནྡོ་ཧ, given here as "chandos and chandohas"; the usual pair is chandoha and upachandoha. The four goddesses of the speech wheel are named by the instrument they play (vīṇā, flute, great drum, drum) and are translated descriptively.  བཀའ་ཐུབ is read as དཀའ་ཐུབ, "austerity". "Great conch" (དུང་ཆེན) for the rosary is in tantric usage commonly understood as human bone. "Sending out and drawing in" renders འཐོར་འཐུང, literally "scattering and drinking", here taken as the outward and inward movement of the breath. "Whatever is its reversal" (ཕྱིར་བཟློག་པ) probably refers to the hundred-syllable mantra in reverse order, set out on sixteen petals. The mantra run beginning "ra ma he" is a song in Apabhraṃśa, the "song of the king of spring" mentioned after it. The vidyā is the female consort.  ཐོད་པ་སངས་རྒྱས at the start of the twenty-sixth chapter, and སངས་རྒྱས་ཐོད་པ་ཅན just after, are taken as the deity Buddhakapāla, who is the subject of this chapter. པ་ལ་ཤ is read as palāśa, the tree with flame-colored flowers. "The fifty letters" renders བརྒྱ་ཕྱེད, literally "half a hundred". The last line of the long verse before the colophon is compressed in the Tibetan and is rendered loosely.  "The neuter letters" (མ་ནིང) are the vowels ṛ, ṝ, ḷ, ḹ, which the grammatical tradition calls neuter. "Sending out and drawing in" renders འཐོར་འཐུང, as in the earlier chapter. "The practice outside meditative equipoise" renders མཉམ་པར་མ་བཞག་སྦྱོར་བ, the post-meditative state. The chapter title phrase དམ་ཚིག་ལས་ལྡང་བ is taken to mean restoring, literally "rising from", the broken samaya, which matches the content of the chapter. The second uṃ in the Buddhakapāla mantra is written exactly as the Tibetan has it.  The line on the fourth goddess reads literally "from ṝ the Jewel truly arises"; her mantra calls her Ratnajālinī, and "Jewel Lamp and Wisdom Lamp" in the next line may be names or epithets of goddesses rather than descriptions. They are translated literally since the names cannot be identified with confidence. In both chapter colophons the Tibetan reads rgyu ("cause") where rgyud ("tantra") is evidently meant; translated as the title of the tantra. "Clad in the directions" means naked. The six mudrās are the six bone ornaments.  "Thukri" (thug ri), the first of the animal faces, could not be identified and is given as written. The phrase rendered "the Ḍāka Moon" may be the name of the teacher of the mantra or an epithet; it is translated literally. The names of the eight ḍākinīs in the final section ("Supreme Samaya," "Supreme Variety," and so on) are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be established with confidence. The colophon of chapter twenty-nine reads nges par brjod pa for mngon par brjod pa, and the colophon's rgyu is evidently rgyud; both are taken as the title of this tantra.  "Holding a peaceful half moon" reads zla ba phyed pa for the written zla ba byed pa, evidently a copyist's slip, as in the parallel lines elsewhere in this text. The "twelve links" (yan lag bcu gnyis) are here equated with the twelve grounds, a tantric scheme that extends the usual ten bodhisattva grounds with "the Formless" and "the One Possessed of Wisdom," and adds the vajra ground as a thirteenth. Pi la and nye bi la are taken as Pīlava and Upapīlava, the classes of sacred sites in the ḍākinī-tantra geography.  "The great brahmin" is a coded term of the tantric feast for a sacramental substance, commonly understood as human flesh, listed here beside "great meat"; it is translated literally. Pīlava and Upapīlava are classes of sacred sites in the ḍākinī-tantra geography; the passage reinterprets them as pairs of inner principles.  The four figures in the intermediate directions are named in the Tibetan simply "Buddha Ḍāka," "Karma Ḍāka" and so on (mkha' 'gro, which does not mark gender); the description that follows is of female figures, so they are given as ḍākinīs. In the list of goddesses, names that could not be identified with confidence are translated by meaning (Lovely Form, Vajra Lip, Light Maker, Horse Face, Fierce Face, Vajra Fierce Lady, the Renowned One, the Fragrant One, Terrifying Face, Moon Crest, Fame Maker, the One with Joined Palms); Kāmbojī renders kam po ci and Nalarki is given as written. "The ḍākinīs cause it to be attained" reads thob par for the written thod par ("skull"), evidently a copyist's slip.  On the southern band the Tibetan reads "four deities" with the masculine form lha po, where the other bands have lha mo, "goddesses"; the mantras are feminine, so lha po may be a slip for lha mo. Here ser ba is the color "yellow." In the northern-band line, the text has bzhad pa, "laugh," which is evidently a slip for bshad pa, "taught"; I have translated "taught." The "wrathful one of cessation" (or "of blocking") renders 'gog pa'i khro bo, and the reference is uncertain. The line about joining śrī "at the end of the consonants" (gsal byed mtha') is obscure, and the rendering is tentative.  Sbyangs pa'i yon tan here means the ascetic virtues (dhūtaguṇa), which the text then lists. Some readings are uncertain or look like slips. In the list of face colors, skye ba, "birth," is taken as skya ba, "pale." The goddess rdo rje sring mchog ma is rendered literally as "the Vajra Supreme Sister," because the matching mantra name (rūkṣasī) does not settle her Sanskrit name. 'Theb is read as thob, "attained." 'Du is read as 'di, "this." Brjed pa in the chapter title is read as brjod pa. In the rain rite, chu lta is taken as chu lha, "water god." La pa is read as ral pa, "locks." The color term lnga dgu is obscure and is tentatively rendered "multicolored." Stobs kyi rgyu is identified as Karkoṭaka.  In the bathing of the Buddha image, dri chu is rendered "urine," following its usual sense in tantric rites; "scented water" is also possible here. The phrase rgyal ba'i pags pa, "the skin of a Victor," is unclear; it may be a slip, but no evident correction suggests itself. The line bri gzugs spangs pa'i mngon sum du is tentatively rendered "directly, dispensing with a drawn image." The opening phrase 'dzam bu'i chu bo, "the Jambu river," refers to the gold of that river (jāmbūnada).  The twelve goddesses of Vārāhī's maṇḍala are named in Sanskrit from their own mantras. The second gate goddess is called rdo rje gzi brjid mchog ma, the same name as the western one, although her mantra is amṛtottame; the text's reading is kept. The line zla ba'i de nyid 'grub par 'gyur, "the reality of the moon will be accomplished," is obscure; it may mean "within a month that very goal is accomplished." Several apparent slips were read as follows: mdangs and mdang as dang, "and"; thong as thod, "skull cup"; and gdod as gdong, "face." The consort called khrag 'thung (literally "blood-drinker") is male here and is rendered Heruka, since the color list names him "glorious Heruka."  The phrase 'jam pa'i rdo rje 'chag rgyal po is unclear; 'chag may be a slip for 'chang, "holder." It is rendered simply "Mañjuvajra, the king." The phrase rta mchog tsa ba'i ka appears to be a corrupt writing of Caṇḍikā, Paramāśva's consort in the preceding list, and is translated that way. The "ten perfections" list adds skillful means, power, aspiration, and wisdom to the usual six, and the ten grounds follow in their standard order.  In two lines of the code list the syllable is missing (the text reads བཞེས་བྱ་བ, a syllable short), so the code syllable for "eating" and for "speaking honestly" cannot be given; they are rendered "another syllable." "Code signs" renders བརྡ; the line closing the list reads ཡི་གེའི་བརྔ, taken as a slip for བརྡ. The chapter number སུ་བཅུ་རྩ་བརྒྱད is read as "thirty-eighth." In the chapter on the yoginīs several lines are obscure: རིགས་ཀྱིས་བོན་རབ་ཏུ་སྨྲ ("speaks much of her family") and ཀོས་ཀོ (rendered "jaw") are uncertain, and ཀུན་ཏུ་སྤྱོད་པ་བསུར is a short, unclear line. The names of the yoginīs rendered as "the Desirous One" (འདོད་ལྡན), "the One of Terrible Form" (འཇིགས་གཟུགས་ཅན), "the All-Roaming One" (ཀུན་ཏུ་སྤྱོད་པ), "the Transformed One" (ཡོངས་གྱུར), "the One with a Child" (བྱིས་བཅས་མོ) and "the Unturning One" (མི་ལྡོག) are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms could not be confirmed.  Several readings are uncertain. ཞེས་པས་ལྟ་བའི་རྗེས་འགྲོ་བ is taken as ཤེས་པས ("knowingly follows one's gaze"). སྟོང་ཕྲག་བཅུ་དང་ལྡ་པ is read as ལྡན་པ, "endowed with ten thousand," whose sense is unclear (perhaps a following of ten thousand). གཏུག་པ is read as གདུག་པ ("fierce"), ཟེར་སྐྱ as སེར་སྐྱ ("tawny"), ཕྱག་རྒྱའི་ལམ as ལན ("reply"), and བརྔ as བརྡ ("code signs"). རྣམ་པར་འདྲེན་པ, "the Guide," may stand for Vināyaka, an epithet of the Buddha; the family meant is not certain. The "mudrā of song" (གླུ་ཡི་ཕྱག་རྒྱ) may be a slip for another emblem.  Several words are read as evident slips: གསོར་པོ as གསོང་པོ ("honest"), བུ for དུ ("my child"), ལམ་བུར་ཚོས as the cheek, སྙོན as སྟོན, སྟེ་བ as ལྟེ་བ ("navel"), and the twice-occurring གང་ཞིགས་ནི as གང་ཞིག་ས་ནི ("she who shows the ground"), which fits the sequence ground, sky, sun. ཀོས་ཀོ is uncertain (rendered "jaw"). The line on gāndha and kar is obscure and is given literally; several lines of the forty-fifth chapter (on reverse looking, "wandering," and the perishing substance) are also obscure and are rendered as closely as the wording allows.  Several of the code glosses are obscure: ས་སྐྱེས ("earth-born") is taken as a tree; དྲིས་བསྒོ་བ is rendered "asking"; and མེ་ཐུན་གྱིས་ཤིག་པ ("fire means destroy it with the hurled substances") is uncertain. ཕོ་ཉ་མེ་བརྒྱད is read as ཕོ་ཉ་མོ་བརྒྱད, "eight messenger women." In the list of eight forms of samaya conduct the items are given as literally as the wording allows. Titles in the list of tantras: དེ་ཉིད་བསྡུས is the Compendium of Reality (Tattvasaṃgraha), དཔལ་མཆོག་དང་པོ the Glorious Supreme Primal (Śrīparamādya), and གསང་བའི་རྒྱུད "the Secret Tantra," probably the Guhyasamāja. Per the established name list, ཁྲག་འཐུང is kept as Herukī, though in this verse it may refer to Heruka.  The line སངས་རྒྱས་དག་ཀྱང་ངེས་པར་འཇིག literally says "even the buddhas surely perish"; it is read as འཇུག, "will surely enter," which suits the context of the triangular maṇḍala and the samaya meats, but the reading is uncertain. "The vow of the net of ḍākinīs" (མཁའ་འགྲོ་མ་ཡི་དྲ་བའི་སྡོམ) may allude to the Ḍākinījālasaṃvara. Several lines on the enjoyment of the messenger and the substances are terse and are rendered as literally as English allows.  "Great conch" (dung chen) is probably a coded term for human bone. Nara, "the great glorious substance," and "the great hero" (dpa' bo chen po, dpa' bo bzang) seem to be coded names for transgressive ritual substances such as human flesh; I kept them literal. "The supreme state" translates bla ma'i go 'phang. Elsewhere in this passage bla ma is read as the yoginī class Lāmā, as in the preceding piece. In "yellow earth" (ser ba'i sa), ser ba stands for ser po, "yellow," which the sequence of colors for the four rites requires; it does not mean hail here. "The self-arisen flower" is menstrual blood. "Seeing beyond" (pha rol blta ba) is uncertain: it may mean clairvoyant sight or seeing the enemy.  The plant names in the vase lists, from cūta to sahade, are romanized as the Tibetan spells them; several, such as moṭa, kṛtañjaḥ, kedri and karikar, could not be identified with confidence. Zhosha is a medicinal fruit named in Tibetan. "Exalted Heruka" translates mngon mtho; it may stand for Abhyudaya, but this is uncertain. The line about joining "the stout one, the end and the nape" (sbom po mtha' ltag) is obscure and probably refers to a yogic or sexual practice. "Curved ornaments" (gug skyed) is a tentative rendering. Here khrag 'thung is the masculine blood drinker of the maṇḍala, so it is given as Heruka, not as Herukī as in an earlier context. "The five nectars" translates "the immortal half of ten."  "The Secret" (gsang ba) probably refers to the Guhyasamāja Tantra. "Guardian of the vowels" (dbyangs skyong) is a tentative rendering. "The cavern" (phug) in the list of accomplishments is uncertain; it may refer to the power to enter caverns or the underworld. "The junctures" (mtshams) appear to be the junctures of the day, the sandhyās, observed in four sessions.  Dbyangs skyong is the Tibetan rendering of Gāyatrī, as the mantra in Gāyatrī form (vidmahe ... prajodaya) shows. It would be better to read "Gāyatrī" also where the preceding piece rendered it "guardian of the vowels." The "junctures" are the sandhyā observances. In the list of substances, Ratnasambhava, Amitābha and Amoghasiddhi stand as code names for ritual substances. Drag gtum ma is translated literally as "the Fierce Wrathful One," since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with certainty. Vajraraudrī for rdo rje drag mo is a probable reconstruction.  Several names of the retinue goddesses are translated literally, because their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence: the Illuminating Ḍākinī (gsal byed), the Horse-Faced Ḍākinī (rta gdong), Blue Utpala, Supreme Net, and the four door goddesses (the Fierce One, the Playful Fierce One, the Supreme Blazing One and the Supreme Victorious Garland). "Has cast aside the skin of Brahmā" renders tshangs pa'i pags pa rnam par spangs and is uncertain.  The text announces "sixteen arms" as emptinesses, but the list that follows names seventeen; it is translated as it stands. The four reliances, four fearlessnesses and four summaries of the Dharma are given here in this tantra's own wording, which departs from the standard lists (for example, Kaṅkālī is "reliance on consciousness"). Several goddess names are rendered by meaning because a Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence: the Horse-Faced One, the Golden-Hued Ḍākinī, the Blue Utpala Goddess, Supreme Mindfulness, She Who Makes Fierce, the Playfully Fierce One, the Supreme Blazing One, and the Supreme Victorious One. Rucakamile is given as the Tibetan transcribes it.  The correspondences of the maṇḍala parts to the bodhisattva grounds list only nine grounds (the sixth, Abhimukhī, is not named), and are given as the text has them. In the final lines, the three petal deities with Lāmā and Khaṇḍarohā are evidently the goddess Ḍākinī; the Tibetan here reads "Vajraḍāka," kept for consistency with earlier usage. "The skin of the Victor" renders the text as written; one would expect an elephant hide here. "Yamadāḍhī" renders a Tibetan form meaning literally "Burner of Yama."  In the opening color list, སེར་བ is the color yellow, not hail. "Yamadāḍhī" renders གཤིན་རྗེ་བརྟན་མ per the house glossary. The line "There, there is no protection and nothing to eat" renders the Tibetan as it stands; the sense of "nothing to eat" is uncertain and may refer to ritual food offerings.  "A mere flash of illuminating lightning" reads གློག ("lightning") for the written ཀློག ("reading"), which makes no sense here in a list of signs following "lamp." In the color lists, སེར་བ is "yellow," not hail. "Durgā, the Peaceful One" may instead be two figures, Durgā and Śivā.  This chapter encodes a mantra by naming letters by their position in the classes of the Sanskrit alphabet; the positions are translated exactly as given. "The semivowels" renders the Tibetan calque མཐར་གནས་པ, while ཨནྟ་སྠཱ (antastha, the same grammatical class) and ཨུཥྨ / ཨུཥྨ་ཎ (ūṣman, the sibilants) are left in their Sanskrit forms as the text spells them. "The letter to be made" (ཡི་གེ་བྱ) is uncertain; it may name a particular letter or syllable.  The whole passage is a coded extraction (uddhāra) of the root mantra, naming letters by their position in the groups of the Sanskrit syllabary (the five consonant classes, the antasthas or semivowels, the ūṣmans or sibilants) and by running numbers; the code-words "Agni" (the letter ra), "the sun", "the Radiant One" or "radiance", "Mahendra", "the sixteenth" and "the fifteenth" (vowel signs, anusvāra, visarga and similar) are given literally as they stand. "The bidding to depart" renders གཤེགས་སུ་གསོལ, most likely a code for the visarga. "The Son of the Sun" (ཉི་མའི་བུ) is a code name whose referent (Yama or Śani) is not certain here, so it is translated literally. སུམ་ཅུ་རྩ་གསུམ here is the ordinal "thirty-third" letter, not the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.  As in the preceding chapter, this is a coded extraction of mantra syllables; the letters are named by vowel number, by running number and by class, and the code words (Agni for the letter ra, the sun, the fifteenth vowel and the like) are kept literal. ཕྱི་མོ is rendered "the alphabet" and མ་མོ here, in the same coded sense of the letter-matrix, as "the mātṛkā" rather than as the class of mother goddesses. "The six limbs" are the six limb mantras placed on the heart, crown, topknot, armor, eyes and weapon.  Like the preceding chapters, this is a coded extraction of mantra syllables by vowel, letter number and class; ཕྱི་མོ is rendered "the alphabet" and མ་མོ "the mātṛkā", both in the sense of the letter-matrix, except where the text speaks of the bliss of "all the mātṛkās", which may also refer to the mother goddesses. The line ཡི་གེ་དྲུག་ཅུ་དྲུག་ཅུ་སྦྱར repeats "sixty" and is rendered as "the sixtieth letter".  Several of the twelve goddesses around Vārāhī are named by Tibetan descriptive names that cannot be matched with confidence to Sanskrit forms (the Sound of the Wicked, the Fierce One, the Moving One, the Stealer of Men, the One of the Burnt Offering, the Self-Arisen One, and the Terrifying Intoxicated One trampled with Bhairava and Cāmuṇḍī); these are translated by meaning, and the division of the list into twelve is not certain. "Seated upon Indra" renders དབང་པོའི་སྟེང་དུ. "The lacing of a clay drum" (རྫ་རྔའི་བཅིང) appears to describe the pattern of the grid and is uncertain. "The munis" is a number code for seven (seven times seven giving the forty-nine squares); "the half of fifty" is twenty-five, and "the son of Agni" and "the seed of water" are code names for letters.  The texts named at the start (དེ་ཉིད་བསྡུས, དཔལ་མཆོག, བདེ་མཆོག, རྡོ་རྗེ་འཇིགས་བྱེད) are taken as the Tattvasaṃgraha (Compendium of Reality), the Śrīparama, the Cakrasaṃvara and the Vajrabhairava tantras. The couplet ཤ་ཆེན་དག་གིས་ཐམས་ཅད་ཀུན། །རྡོ་རྗེ་ཡང་ནི་མེད་པར་བཤད is obscure, and its rendering is tentative. "The one with the copper crest" is most likely the rooster. The names of the four wrathful ones (the Vajra of Desire, the Blue Vajra, the Moving Vajra and the Vajra of Acala) are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence.  The run of syllables after "the middle of the bhaga" appears to be a coded listing of the eight substances to be eaten; it is given as the Tibetan spells it. "Dwelling within the seventh birth" likely alludes to the "seven-times-born" (saptajanman) ingredient of tantric rites; the line is rendered literally. "Vajra Equal to Space" and "Vajra Solid" are literal renderings of རྡོ་རྗེ་མཁའ་མཉམ and རྡོ་རྗེ་སྲ་བ, whose Sanskrit forms I cannot fix with confidence. "Donkey" renders the transliterated Sanskrit ག་རྡ་བ (gardabha), confirmed by the chapter title's བོང་བུ. "What is to be approached and not approached" reflects the tantric pairing of permitted and forbidden (gamyāgamya), here applied antinomian fashion.  Several of the heroes and wrathful kings are rendered literally because their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence: Vajra Fire-Glory (རྡོ་རྗེ་མེ་དཔལ), Vajra Destroyer (རྡོ་རྗེ་འཇོམས་པ), Vajra Burner (རྡོ་རྗེ་སྲེག་བྱེད), Vajra Swaggerer (རྡོ་རྗེ་འགྱིང་བ), Vajra Horse-Face (རྡོ་རྗེ་རྟ་གདོང) and Vajra Bear-Face (རྡོ་རྗེ་དྲེད་གདོང). Vajraṭahara keeps the Tibetan transliteration as given. "The goddesses arisen from the neuter syllables" takes མ་ནིང as the neuter vowels of the Sanskrit alphabet. "Consonant" takes གསལ་བྱེད in its grammatical sense, in line with the consonant seed syllables of the five ḍākas.  The colophon of chapter sixty-four reads མདོན་པར and བཞིན་པ, evident slips for མངོན་པར and བཞི་པ; it is translated as "the sixty-fourth chapter" of the Abhidhānottara. ཆེ་གེ་མོ in the mantra is the Tibetan placeholder "so-and-so," where the name of the target is inserted, and is translated accordingly. "Great conch" (དུང་ཆེན) in tantric usage commonly denotes human bone, so the rosary is likely of bone. "It is the bone beyond counting" renders བགྲང་དུ་མེད་པའི་རུས་པ literally; its sense is unclear. The names of the goddesses Vajramāyā, Dharmavajrī and Viśvamāyā are reconstructed from the Tibetan.  This chapter describes the "seven-times-born" (saptajanman) paśu whose body supplies the ritual substances that follow. "The great sesame oil" (ཏིལ་མར་ཆེན་པོ) likely denotes human fat in tantric code. "The two organs" renders དབང་པོ་གཉིས, here the sense or sexual organs, not the god Indra. In the first mantra, མཚམས་མེད་པ་འདི་ཞེས་བྱ་བ is taken as the slot where the target, a perpetrator of immediate retribution, is named.  Several ingredients are Sanskrit or Tibetan plant and substance names whose exact identity is uncertain; they are given as the text writes them: tsupma (ཚུབ་མ), nyangtré (མྱང་སྤྲས), indravāru, putrañjali, putra, jīvantī, nāgamado, brahmadaṇḍī, "makara's hand" (ཆུ་སྲིན་ལག) and "vajra splendor" (རྡོ་རྗེ་གཟི་བརྗིད). "Self-arisen flower" is a standard code for menstrual blood. "The Wealth Guardian" renders ནོར་སྐྱོང literally, a name I cannot identify with confidence. "Four kings of the teeth" likely means the canine teeth.  The two lists of bodhisattvas do not match exactly. The first list's second "from saṃ" figure is "Destroyer of All Lower Realms," while the summary list has "Destroyer of All Sorrow" (probably Sarvaśokatamonirghātamati); both are translated as written. In the summary, "Jewel-Attainer" stands where the first list has Mahāsthāmaprāpta and may be a variant for him. "Manifesting syllable" renders gsal byed, which here seems to mean the opening syllable oṃ. The "hide of the Victor" (rgyal ba'i pags pa) may stand for the usual elephant hide. The list of tantra titles in the section beginning "The supreme of greatness is the Samāja" is terse; its titles are given in literal translation where they cannot be securely identified. Several goddess names (Kampoji, Buddhabodhī, Jayakarī, Vajrabhairavī, Dhvajā) are tentative reconstructions or are given as spelled.  The line rendered "The buddhas keep it hidden from the tīrthikas" could also be read as "keep it hidden from Buddhists and tīrthikas." Ritual pilgrimage to the outer sacred places (pīṭha) is contrasted with the places as they exist within the body. The identification of the goddess "Dharaṇī" (literally "Earth Holder") is tentative, and "Jambhala" is given as spelled in the goddess list. "Some other lord" renders dbang po gzhan, read here as a deity other than one's own and not as the god Indra.  The opening four lines are taken as the close of what all the tathāgatas spoke at the end of the previous piece, so they are given in quotation marks. Lochung, "the Junior Translator," is left as a title because the person it names is not identified here.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
